A <left singular vector> $u_j$ is a <unit vector> in the output space satisfying $AA^*u_j=\sigma_j^2u_j$. For positive <singular values>, the pairing also satisfies $A^*u_j=\sigma_jv_j$. Thus $u_1=(1,0)^T$ and $u_2=(0,1)^T$ describe the output axes of the preceding <ellipse>, while the <right singular vectors> describe its input directions. At zero <singular values>, division by $\sigma_j$ is impossible: complete the <orthonormal bases> independently in the <null spaces> of $A$ and $A^*$. Repeated <singular values> allow different matched <orthonormal bases> within the corresponding <eigenspaces>. The same positive pairing extends to a <compact operator> through its <singular value system>.

Even distinct <singular values> do not fix arrow signs: replacing both $u_j$ and $v_j$ by their negatives preserves $Av_j=\sigma_j u_j$ and the <outer product> $u_jv_j^*$. Reversing only one arrow would change that term's sign. The paired directions, rather than independently chosen arrow orientations, encode the <matrix>.

= Low-rank approximation
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$\min_{\operatorname{rank}B\leq r}\|A-B\|$}

A low-rank approximation replaces an $m\times n$ <matrix> $A$ by a <matrix> $B$ of <matrix rank> at most a chosen integer $r$, with $0\leq r\leq\min(m,n)$, while making an explicitly chosen error <norm> small. The point is to describe many entries using a few shared patterns: an <image compression> may retain broad brightness patterns while discarding finer ones. The approximation is useful when the saved storage or computation justifies the lost detail. A small <matrix rank> alone says nothing about the error.

The compact representation is a sum of <outer products>, equivalently a product of two thin <matrices>:
$$
B=XY^*=\sum_{j=1}^r x_jy_j^*,\qquad
X\in\mathbb C^{m\times r},\quad Y\in\mathbb C^{n\times r}.
$$
Here $x_j,y_j$ are the columns of $X,Y$, and the star denotes the <conjugate transpose>; over the <real numbers> use the <transpose>. The <rank bound for a matrix product> gives $\operatorname{rank}B\leq r$. Conversely, choose a <basis> of the <column space> of $B$ as the columns of $X$, and put the coordinates of each column of $B$ into $Y^*$; pad with zero columns if the <matrix rank> is smaller than $r$. Each nonzero <outer product> is a <rank-one matrix>: it repeats a single column pattern at different strengths across the columns.

Keeping the two factors stores $r(m+n)$ <scalars> instead of $mn$. This saves space only when $r(m+n)<mn$. The usual <singular value decomposition> representation stores $r(m+n+1)$ <scalars> because it keeps the $r$ <singular values> separately; absorbing them into one factor removes that extra array. These are representation sizes at equal <scalar> precision, before file overhead. See https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-030-31351-7_9[Uschmajew and Vandereycken, §9.2: thin factors and their storage], and https://cs357.cs.illinois.edu/notes/ref-16-svd.html[Illinois CS 357, Low-rank approximation: storing singular vectors and values].

The factors also give $Bz=X(Y^*z)$ without forming the full <matrix> $B$. Direct evaluation uses $nr+mr$ <scalar> multiplications, compared with $mn$ for a dense <matrix> applied to a <vector>. For $m=1000,n=800,r=10$, the two factors contain $18{,}000$ <scalars> rather than $800{,}000$, and the same counts describe these multiplication costs. Constructing the factors is a separate cost. Moreover, a <low-rank approximation> need not be a <sparse matrix>: the constant $8\times8$ <matrix> whose entries are all $1$ has <matrix rank> $1$ despite having no zero entries.

For the standard unweighted problem, write a <singular value decomposition> with $p=\min(m,n)$ and <singular values> $\sigma_1\geq\cdots\geq\sigma_p\geq0$. Retain the first $r$ paired <left singular vectors> and <right singular vectors>:
$$
A_r=\sum_{j=1}^r\sigma_j u_jv_j^*,\qquad A_0=0.
$$
The <Eckart-Young-Mirsky theorem> says that this same $A_r$ minimizes both of the following error <norms> over all <matrices> with <matrix rank> at most $r$:
$$
\min_{\operatorname{rank}B\leq r}\|A-B\|_F
=\|A-A_r\|_F=\left(\sum_{j=r+1}^p\sigma_j^2\right)^{1/2},
\qquad
\min_{\operatorname{rank}B\leq r}\|A-B\|_2
=\|A-A_r\|_2=\sigma_{r+1},
$$
where $\sigma_{p+1}=0$ and an empty sum is zero. Here the <Frobenius norm> is $\|E\|_F=(\sum_{i,j}|E_{ij}|^2)^{1/2}$, whereas the <operator norm> is specifically induced by the <Euclidean norm>, $\|E\|_2=\max_{\|z\|_2=1}\|Ez\|_2$. The first aggregates errors in all entries; the second bounds the worst output error for a <unit vector> input. These formulas apply to real and complex <matrices>. https://math.uchicago.edu/~may/REU2016/REUPapers/Mazeika.pdf[Mazeika, §§4.1 and 4.3: norms and optimal singular-value truncation]{ref}

For $A\ne0$, a relative <Frobenius norm> tolerance $\varepsilon$ is met precisely when the discarded squared <singular values> sum to at most $\varepsilon^2\sum_j\sigma_j^2$. Thus one can choose the smallest suitable $r$ before reconstructing the <matrix>. Many individually small discarded <singular values> can still have a large combined error. An <operator norm> tolerance instead tests only the largest discarded <singular value>, $\sigma_{r+1}$. The two questions can therefore select different ranks.

The following stipulated $8\times8$ <matrix> makes the tradeoff visible. Define four real <vectors> of length $8$:
$$
h_0=(1,1,1,1,1,1,1,1)^T,\quad
h_1=(1,1,1,1,-1,-1,-1,-1)^T,
$$
$$
h_2=(1,1,-1,-1,1,1,-1,-1)^T,\quad
h_3=(1,-1,1,-1,1,-1,1,-1)^T,
\qquad
A=h_0h_0^T+\frac38h_1h_1^T+\frac18h_2h_2^T+\frac1{16}h_3h_3^T.
$$
The <vectors> $h_j/\sqrt8$ are <orthonormal>, so this is already a <singular value decomposition> written as <outer products>, with positive <singular values> $8,3,1,1/2$. All entries lie between $7/16$ and $25/16$ and can be displayed as brightnesses. The first component is uniform brightness, the second distinguishes broad blocks, and the last two supply finer alternating patterns. This is a designed arithmetic example, not measured image data.

\Image[low-rank-pattern-reconstruction.png]
{title=Shared patterns in a stipulated $8\times8$ <matrix>}
{description=The first three panels use the same brightness scale. Rank one retains uniform brightness; rank two adds the broad blocks. The signed residual $A-A_2$ has its own color scale to expose the discarded patterns. The factors apply a <linear map> through just $r$ intermediate coordinates.}

Here $A_1=h_0h_0^T$ has every entry equal to $1$, while $A_2=h_0h_0^T+3h_1h_1^T/8$ has entries $11/8$ within the two diagonal blocks and $5/8$ in the other two blocks. For instance, $A_{11}=25/16$ becomes $(A_2)_{11}=11/8$: its error is $3/16$. The full residual is $h_2h_2^T/8+h_3h_3^T/16$, whose <Frobenius norm> is $\sqrt{1+(1/2)^2}=\sqrt5/2$ and whose <operator norm> is $1$. Since $\|A\|_F=\sqrt{297}/2$, the relative <Frobenius norm> error is $\sqrt{5/297}\approx12.97\%$. Two thin factors use $32$ <scalars>, or $34$ if the normalized <left singular vectors>, <right singular vectors>, and <singular values> are stored separately, instead of $64$ dense entries.

\Image[low-rank-error-storage-tradeoff.png]
{title=Error falls as storage rises for the stipulated <matrix>}
{description=The exact <Frobenius norm> and <operator norm> errors are plotted for $r=0,1,2,3,4$. The storage panel counts the two thin factors, or the normalized <left singular vectors> and <right singular vectors> plus separate <singular values>, at equal <scalar> precision. Lines join discrete rank choices; they do not represent intermediate ranks or measured data.}

Retaining three components reduces the <Frobenius norm> error to $1/2$ while using $48$ <scalars> in two factors. Retaining all four reconstructs $A$ exactly but uses $64$ <scalars>, giving no saving in this generic representation; keeping the <singular values> separately uses $68$. Our particular sign patterns could be encoded more economically, but that would exploit an additional structure beyond the general <low-rank approximation>. The striking feature is that numerical error and storage can both be read from the decomposition, rather than judged only by how smooth the picture looks.

The constraint is deliberately <matrix rank> \i[at most] $r$. The truncation has $\operatorname{rank}A_r=\min(r,\operatorname{rank}A)$. If $\operatorname{rank}A<r$, the best approximation is $A$ itself, with zero error. Requiring <matrix rank> exactly $r$ can destroy attainment: for $A=\operatorname{diag}(1,0)$ and $r=2$, the <matrices> $B_\delta=\operatorname{diag}(1,\delta)$ have <matrix rank> $2$ for $\delta>0$, and both error <norms> equal $\delta$. Their error has <infimum> zero, but no rank-two <matrix> attains it. If $r\leq\operatorname{rank}A$, the displayed $A_r$ does have <matrix rank> exactly $r$ and attains the same minimum under either constraint.

Optimality also does not always mean uniqueness. For $0<r<\operatorname{rank}A$, the best <Frobenius norm> approximation is unique when $\sigma_r>\sigma_{r+1}$. https://link.springer.com/chapter/10.1007/978-3-030-31351-7_9[Uschmajew and Vandereycken, Theorem 9.1: uniqueness across a singular-value gap]{ref} A positive tie across the cutoff permits different choices: for $A=\operatorname{diag}(3,3,1)$ and $r=1$, both $\operatorname{diag}(3,0,0)$ and $\operatorname{diag}(0,3,0)$ have <Frobenius norm> error $\sqrt{10}$ and <operator norm> error $3$. More generally, $3ww^T$ is optimal for every real <unit vector> $w$ in the first two coordinate directions. Rotating which tied direction is kept changes the approximation. Repeated <singular values> entirely inside the retained group do not change its sum of <outer products>. At $r=0$ the only feasible <matrix> is zero; at $r\geq\operatorname{rank}A$ the unique zero-error approximation is $A$, even if some <singular values> are tied at zero.

A strict gap does not guarantee uniqueness for the <operator norm>. For $A=\operatorname{diag}(3,1)$ and $r=1$, every $B_t=\operatorname{diag}(t,0)$ with $2\leq t\leq4$ has <matrix rank> $1$ and error $\|A-B_t\|_2=\max(|3-t|,1)=1$, which is optimal. Only $t=3$ minimizes the <Frobenius norm>, since $\|A-B_t\|_F=\sqrt{(3-t)^2+1}$. An unchanged worst direction can hide extra error in another direction under the <operator norm>.

Finally, these guarantees concern the stated <norms> and the sole <matrix rank> constraint. They do not establish that a discarded pattern is noise or irrelevant to a particular task: the finer patterns in our constructed example were put there intentionally. Adding entrywise weights or restrictions on the factors changes the problem, so the <Eckart-Young-Mirsky theorem> above does not automatically solve it. For historical context, the original 1936 matrix problem is documented in the existing <Eckart-Young-Mirsky theorem> section.

= Eckart-Young-Mirsky theorem
{c}
{parent=Low-rank approximation}
{title2=$\min_{\operatorname{rank}B\leq k}\|A-B\|_F=(\sum_{j>k}\sigma_j^2)^{1/2}$}

For a finite <matrix> with <singular values> in nonincreasing order, truncating its <singular value decomposition> after $k$ terms minimizes the <Frobenius norm> error, whose square is the sum of the discarded squared <singular values>. It also minimizes the <operator norm> error, which equals the next <singular value>, or zero if none remain. See https://math.uchicago.edu/~may/REU2016/REUPapers/Mazeika.pdf[Mazeika, Section 4.3: Low rank approximation].

The problem of replacing a <matrix> by one of lower <matrix rank> is explicit in Eckart and Young's 1936 paper, https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/psychometrika/article/approximation-of-one-matrix-by-another-of-lower-rank/B29672E1EDD0FA1B7611D4DFAFC321B3[The approximation of one matrix by another of lower rank, Psychometrika 1, 211-218]{ref}. The publication predates the modern <image compression> example, showing how a general matrix question can support later applications.

= Unimodular matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

An integral square matrix is unimodular when its determinant is $1$ or $-1$. Its inverse again has integral entries, so it defines an invertible change of integer coordinates.

= Householder transformation
{c}
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$H=I-2uu^T$}

= Householder reflection
{c}
{synonym}

A <Householder transformation> is a <reflection> that leaves every direction in a chosen <hyperplane> fixed and reverses its normal direction. For a real <unit vector> $u$, its <matrix> is
$$
\boxed{H=I-2uu^T.}
$$
To understand the formula, decompose any <vector> into its <orthogonal projection> onto the normal and a perpendicular component:
$$
x=x_\parallel+x_\perp,\qquad
x_\parallel=(u^Tx)u,\qquad u^Tx_\perp=0.
$$
Then $Hx=x_\perp-x_\parallel$. Subtracting twice the normal component changes its sign while retaining everything parallel to the mirror. A nonzero normal $v$ gives the equivalent formula $H=I-2vv^T/(v^Tv)$, so normalization can be deferred.

For example, take $u=(2,1)^T/\sqrt5$. Its mirror is the <straight line> $2x_1+x_2=0$, and
$$
H=\begin{pmatrix}-3/5&-4/5\\-4/5&3/5\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
H\begin{pmatrix}3\\4\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}-5\\0\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The midpoint of these two <vectors> is $(-1,2)^T$, which lies on the mirror; their connecting segment is parallel to its normal. Both <vectors> have <Euclidean norm> five.

\Image[householder-reflection-geometry.png]
{title=A Householder reflection reverses the normal component and preserves the mirror component}
{description=The mirror is $2x_1+x_2=0$. The <vector> $(3,4)^T$ becomes $(-5,0)^T$; their midpoint $(-1,2)^T$ is the <orthogonal projection> onto the mirror.}

Several useful properties follow from this decomposition. The <Householder reflection> is a <symmetric matrix> and an <orthogonal matrix>:
$$
H^T=H,\qquad H^2=I,\qquad H^TH=I.
$$
Reflecting twice restores the original <vector>; <Euclidean norms> and <inner products> are preserved. The <eigenvalue> is $-1$ along $u$ and $+1$ on $u^\perp$, so $\det H=-1$. A reflection reverses <orientation> even though it preserves lengths.

The computational payoff is that the mirror can be chosen to send a whole <vector> onto one coordinate axis, eliminating several entries at once. <Householder QR decomposition> repeats this operation on the remaining parts of successive columns. For a real <symmetric matrix>, applying the same <Householder reflection> on both sides gives a <matrix similarity> $HAH$ that preserves symmetry and <eigenvalues>; this is the basis of <Householder tridiagonalization>.

Alston S. Householder's 1958 paper \i[Unitary Triangularization of a Nonsymmetric Matrix] develops this elimination method. Its motivation includes reducing the arithmetic needed for triangularization while retaining the conditioning advantages of <orthogonal transformations>. https://web.stanford.edu/class/cme324/classics/householder.pdf[Householder (1958), Journal of the ACM 5(4), pp. 339–342: original paper]{ref} Over the <complex numbers>, $I-2uu^*$ for a unit $u$ is <Hermitian> and <unitary>, with the star denoting the <conjugate transpose>; the coordinate-axis construction below is stated for real <vectors>.

= Householder reflection sending a vector to a coordinate axis
{c}
{parent=Householder transformation}
{title2=$Hx=\alpha e_1,\quad |\alpha|=\|x\|_2$}

For a nonzero real <vector> $x\in\mathbb R^n$, choose $\alpha$ with $|\alpha|=\|x\|_2$ and put $v=x-\alpha e_1$. When $v\ne0$, the <Householder reflection>
$$
H=I-\frac{2vv^T}{v^Tv}
$$
satisfies $Hx=\alpha e_1$: every component except the first becomes zero. Here $e_1$ is the first <Cartesian basis> <vector>. The calculation is short:
$$
v^Tv=2(\|x\|_2^2-\alpha x_1),
\qquad
v^Tx=\|x\|_2^2-\alpha x_1,
\qquad
Hx=x-v=\alpha e_1.
$$
If $v=0$, the <vector> is already at the selected target, so the <identity matrix> suffices.

In <floating-point arithmetic>, a customary choice is
$$
\alpha=
\begin{cases}
-\|x\|_2,&x_1\ge0,\\
\|x\|_2,&x_1<0.
\end{cases}
$$
Then $|v_1|=|x_1|+\|x\|_2$, avoiding subtraction of nearly equal positive quantities in the first component. Either sign can be valid in exact arithmetic; the numerical choice protects the construction from <catastrophic cancellation>. https://math.mit.edu/classes/18.085/summer2016/handouts/qr.pdf[Olver, Orthogonal Bases and the QR Algorithm: Householder factorization and sign selection]{ref}

For $x=(3,4)^T$, this choice gives $\alpha=-5$ and $v=(8,4)^T$. Dividing $v$ by its <Euclidean norm> gives $u=(2,1)^T/\sqrt5$, exactly the normal in the geometric example.

= Householder QR example in two dimensions
{c}
{parent=Householder transformation}
{title2=$A=QR$}

Apply the preceding <Householder reflection> to the columns of
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}3&1\\4&2\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The first column becomes $(-5,0)^T$ and the second becomes $(-11/5,2/5)^T$:
$$
R=HA=\begin{pmatrix}-5&-11/5\\0&2/5\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
A=HR.
$$
Thus an <orthogonal matrix> multiplying an <upper triangular matrix> reconstructs $A$. The zero below the diagonal came from a geometric operation that preserves <Euclidean norms> and <inner products>.

\Image[householder-qr-column-elimination.png]
{title=A single Householder reflection makes a two-column matrix upper triangular}
{description=Both columns are reflected by the same <orthogonal transformation>. The first lands on the first coordinate axis; the second retains a nonzero second component. The intermediate triangular factor has a negative first diagonal entry.}

Our positive-diagonal convention for <QR decomposition> is obtained by a sign adjustment. With $D=\operatorname{diag}(-1,1)$, put
$$
Q=HD=\begin{pmatrix}3/5&-4/5\\4/5&3/5\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
R_+=DR=\begin{pmatrix}5&11/5\\0&2/5\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Then $Q^TQ=I$ and $\boxed{A=QR_+}$. The intermediate sign chooses an <orientation> for a <basis vector>; the adjustment makes the diagonal positive.

For a larger <matrix>, the next <Householder reflection> acts only on the lower rows of the remaining columns, retaining the zeros already created. Store its defining <vector> and apply $z\mapsto z-2u(u^Tz)$ instead of forming a dense reflection <matrix>. This links a simple mirror operation to efficient <QR decomposition>, including <least-squares solutions>.

= Change of basis
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

A <change of basis> rewrites a <vector> using a different <ordered basis> of the same <vector space>. The <vector> stays fixed; its <coordinate vector> changes because the measuring directions change. Both <bases> must describe every <vector> uniquely, so a <change of basis> is reversible.

Start with the <Cartesian basis> $e_1=(1,0)$, $e_2=(0,1)$, and choose the new <basis vectors> $u=(1,1)$ and $v=(1,-1)$. For the <vector> $z=(2,0)$,
$$
z=2e_1+0e_2=u+v,\qquad
[z]_{\rm old}=\begin{pmatrix}2\\0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
[z]_{\rm new}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The second <coordinate vector> says to travel once along $u$ and once along $v$. Their vertical contributions cancel, leaving exactly the original horizontal arrow. The diagram shows two descriptions of that same arrow, with the same origin and endpoint.

\Image[change-of-basis-coordinates.png]
{title=The same <vector> expressed in two <bases>: $2e_1=u+v$}

Choosing a useful <basis> can reveal simple structure hidden by the old <coordinate vectors>. Here the new <basis> describes common and opposing contributions. For two equal measurements $(5,5)$, the new <coordinate vector> is $(5,0)$: only the common contribution is present. In larger examples, a suitable <basis> can concentrate important information into a few <coefficients>. Strang's lecture on <change of basis> develops this principle through applications: the reversible <change of basis> preserves information, while subsequently discarding small <coefficients> loses information.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06-linear-algebra-spring-2010/resources/lecture-31-change-of-basis-image-compression/[Strang, Lecture 31: Change of basis; image compression]{ref}

= Change-of-basis matrix
{parent=Change of basis}
{title2=$[z]_{\rm old}=P[z]_{\rm new}$}

Fix the convention: the columns of the <change-of-basis matrix> $P$ are the new <basis vectors> written in the old <basis>. Thus $P$ converts new <coordinate vectors> into old ones; its <matrix inverse> converts old <coordinate vectors> into new ones. For the preceding <basis>,
$$
P=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
P^{-1}=\frac12\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
P^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}2\\0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Its <determinant> is $-2$, so $P$ is an <invertible matrix>. Checking which direction $P$ converts is essential: different books may call either $P$ or $P^{-1}$ the <change-of-basis matrix>.

For a <linear operator>, convert the input into the old <basis>, apply the old <matrix>, and convert the output back. Reading that sequence from right to left gives
$$
A_{\rm new}=P^{-1}A_{\rm old}P.
$$
For example, let the old <diagonal matrix> stretch horizontal components by $2$ and vertical components by $3$. Then
$$
A_{\rm old}=\begin{pmatrix}2&0\\0&3\end{pmatrix},\qquad
A_{\rm new}=\begin{pmatrix}2.5&-0.5\\-0.5&2.5\end{pmatrix},\qquad
A_{\rm new}\begin{pmatrix}1\\1\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}2\\2\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The output represents $2u+2v=(4,0)$, just as $A_{\rm old}(2,0)^T=(4,0)^T$. The <matrices> look different because their <bases> differ, but they encode the same <linear operator>. This relation is called <matrix similarity>; it preserves the underlying action.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06-linear-algebra-spring-2010/46108efb43dcf33e989eb5e101fcf9b5_MIT18_06S10_L31.pdf[Strang, Lecture 31 transcript: coordinate conversion and similar matrices]{ref}

= Circulant matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$C_{nj}=c_{(n-j)\bmod N}$}

A circulant <matrix> applies the same rule at every position of a finite ring: moving both the input and output indices one step leaves its entries unchanged. Its first column determines the whole <matrix>. For a length-$N$ <vector> $c$, index rows and columns by $0,\ldots,N-1$ and define
$$
C_{nj}=c_{(n-j)\bmod N}.
$$
The indices use <modular arithmetic>. Thus each column is the previous column shifted down one place, with the last entry wrapping around to the top. Multiplication is exactly <circular convolution>:
$$
(Cx)_n=\sum_{j=0}^{N-1}c_{(n-j)\bmod N}x_j
=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}c_\ell x_{(n-\ell)\bmod N}.
$$
This makes a circulant <matrix> a natural model for a periodic <finite difference> rule or a filter acting on a periodic record. Strang's MIT lecture develops this connection between circulant <matrices>, <circular convolution> and the <discrete Fourier transform>.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-065-matrix-methods-in-data-analysis-signal-processing-and-machine-learning-spring-2018/resources/lecture-31-eigenvectors-of-circulant-matrices-fourier-matrix/[Strang, MIT 18.065 Lecture 31: eigenvectors of circulant matrices]{ref}

For example, the first column $c=(2,-1,0,-1)^T$ gives
$$
L=\begin{pmatrix}
2&-1&0&-1\\
-1&2&-1&0\\
0&-1&2&-1\\
-1&0&-1&2
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad (Lx)_n=2x_n-x_{n-1}-x_{n+1}.
$$
Here the indices wrap around modulo four. Each output compares a value with its two neighbors; this is the <Graph Laplacian> of a four-vertex <cycle graph>. A constant input gives zero, while an alternating input gives a large response. The <Fourier diagonalization of a circulant matrix> makes this distinction exact: its four <eigenvalues> are $0,2,4,2$.

\Image[circulant-fourier-modes.png]
{title=Fourier eigenvectors of the four-point cycle Laplacian}

The plotted <discrete Fourier modes> are complex <vectors>, shown through their real and imaginary parts. Each entry has <modulus> one; the <Euclidean norm> of each length-four <vector> is two. In particular, modes $k=1$ and $k=3$ have the same real part but opposite imaginary parts, so they are distinct <eigenvectors> with the same <eigenvalue>.

The useful surprise is that every length-$N$ circulant <matrix> shares the same <basis> of <eigenvectors>, whatever its first column. One <change of basis> therefore simplifies an entire family of <linear maps>, instead of requiring a separate <eigenvalue> computation for each one. Bamieh's tutorial develops the <discrete Fourier transform> from precisely this simultaneous <diagonalization> problem.https://arxiv.org/abs/1805.05533[Bamieh, Discovering Transforms: A Tutorial on Circulant Matrices, Circular Convolution, and the Discrete Fourier Transform (2018; revised 2022)]{ref}

= Fourier diagonalization of a circulant matrix
{c}
{parent=Circulant matrix}
{title2=$C=F^{-1}\operatorname{diag}(Fc)F$}

Use the negative-exponential forward <discrete Fourier transform> and the $1/N$-normalized <inverse discrete Fourier transform>:
$$
F_{kn}=e^{-2\pi ikn/N},
\qquad
(F^{-1})_{nk}=\frac1N e^{2\pi ink/N}.
$$
For the circulant <matrix> with first column $c$, the <discrete Fourier mode> $w^{(k)}_n=e^{2\pi ikn/N}$ is an <eigenvector> with <eigenvalue>
$$
\boxed{\lambda_k=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}c_\ell e^{-2\pi ik\ell/N}=(Fc)_k.}
$$
Indeed, substitution into <circular convolution> gives
$$
(Cw^{(k)})_n
=\sum_{\ell=0}^{N-1}c_\ell e^{2\pi ik(n-\ell)/N}
=w^{(k)}_n\lambda_k.
$$
The columns of $F^{-1}$ are $w^{(k)}/N$, so the <diagonalization of a matrix> takes the form
$$
C=F^{-1}\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_0,\ldots,\lambda_{N-1})F.
$$
The <roots of unity> give <orthogonality> of these <eigenvectors>; dividing each $w^{(k)}$ by $\sqrt N$ makes the <basis> <orthonormal>. Consequently, every complex circulant <matrix> is a <normal matrix>, including ones that are not <Hermitian matrices>.

The sign convention matters. For $c=(1,2,0,0)^T$, the circulant <matrix> is
$$
C=\begin{pmatrix}
1&0&0&2\\
2&1&0&0\\
0&2&1&0\\
0&0&2&1
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
(\lambda_0,\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3)=(3,1-2i,-1,1+2i).
$$
Changing the direction of the shift or the sign in the <discrete Fourier transform> changes which complex <eigenvalue> goes with which <discrete Fourier mode>. A real symmetric example alone would conceal that distinction.

For a <linear system> $Cx=b$, put $\widehat x=Fx$ and $\widehat b=Fb$. The equation becomes $N$ independent scalar equations
$$
\lambda_k\widehat x_k=\widehat b_k.
$$
If every $\lambda_k$ is nonzero, divide to find the unique <vector> $x$ and apply the <inverse discrete Fourier transform>. If some $\lambda_k=0$, a <vector> $x$ satisfying the <linear system> exists exactly when $\widehat b_k=0$ at every such index; the corresponding $\widehat x_k$ remain free. This is a concrete version of the <Fredholm alternative> in a finite-dimensional setting.

With an appropriate <fast Fourier transform>, multiplication or a compatible solve takes $O(N\log N)$ arithmetic operations, instead of forming a dense <matrix> and applying general <Gaussian elimination>. The gain comes from the periodic structure; an arbitrary <matrix> does not have this shared <basis> of <discrete Fourier modes>.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-065-matrix-methods-in-data-analysis-signal-processing-and-machine-learning-spring-2018/resources/lecture-31-eigenvectors-of-circulant-matrices-fourier-matrix/[Strang, MIT 18.065 Lecture 31: Fourier eigenvectors and fast computation]{ref}

= Periodic Poisson equation on a four-point cycle
{parent=Circulant matrix}
{title2=$2x_n-x_{n-1}-x_{n+1}=b_n$}

Consider the discrete <linear system> $Lx=b$ with
$$
L=\begin{pmatrix}
2&-1&0&-1\\
-1&2&-1&0\\
0&-1&2&-1\\
-1&0&-1&2
\end{pmatrix},
\qquad b=(1,0,-1,0)^T.
$$
This is a finite periodic analogue of the <Poisson equation>, using the negative second <finite difference> with unit grid spacing. It is also a <Graph Laplacian> equation on a <cycle graph>; no continuum approximation is being claimed here.

\Image[periodic-circulant-poisson-solution.png]
{title=Balanced forcing and the mean-zero solution on a four-node ring}

Summing the four equations gives zero on the left, since each value occurs twice positively and twice negatively. A necessary condition is therefore $\sum_n b_n=0$. The <Fourier diagonalization of a circulant matrix> shows that this is also sufficient: the only zero <eigenvalue> of $L$ is the constant mode $k=0$, and $\widehat b_0=\sum_n b_n$.

For the displayed forcing, the <discrete Fourier transform> gives
$$
\widehat b=(0,2,0,2)^T,
\qquad
(\lambda_0,\lambda_1,\lambda_2,\lambda_3)=(0,2,4,2).
$$
Divide only at the three nonzero <eigenvalues>, and choose $\widehat x_0=0$ to impose zero <arithmetic mean>:
$$
\widehat x=(0,1,0,1)^T,
\qquad
\boxed{x=(1/2,0,-1/2,0)^T.}
$$
Direct substitution gives $Lx=(1,0,-1,0)^T$. All other <vectors> satisfying the <linear system> are $x+a(1,1,1,1)^T$, because the <kernel> is the space of constant <vectors>. Thus balanced forcing determines the differences between values, while a reference level is an extra choice. This small model explains why a periodic <Poisson equation> needs both a compatibility condition and a normalization.

= Invertible matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$\operatorname{rank}A=n\iff\det A\ne0$}

An $n$-by-$n$ <square matrix> $A$ over a <field> is invertible when it has a two-sided <matrix inverse>. Equivalently, its <linear map> $x\mapsto Ax$ is a <bijection>: every output comes from exactly one input. For a <square matrix>, the following conditions are equivalent:
$$
A\text{ is invertible}
\iff\operatorname{rank}A=n
\iff\ker A=\{0\}
\iff\det A\ne0
\iff Ax=b\text{ has exactly one solution for every }b.
$$
Here <matrix rank> $n$ means both <full column rank> and <full row rank>. The square hypothesis matters: a rectangular <matrix> can have <full column rank> without reaching every output, or <full row rank> without determining every input.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06-linear-algebra-spring-2010/resources/lecture-3-multiplication-and-inverse-matrices/[Strang, MIT 18.06 Lecture 3: multiplication and inverse matrices]{ref}

The equivalences explain why invertibility is a question about retaining information. A <trivial kernel> makes the <linear map> <injective>; the <rank-nullity theorem> then gives <matrix rank> $n$, so its columns form a <basis> and the <linear map> is also <surjective>. Assigning to each output its unique input gives a <linear map>: the preimage of $\alpha b+\beta c$ is $\alpha A^{-1}b+\beta A^{-1}c$. The <determinant> is nonzero precisely when the columns are <linearly independent>. Thus algebraic solvability and independent directions describe the same phenomenon; over the <real numbers>, nonzero <oriented volume> supplies its geometric interpretation.

= Singular matrix
{parent=Invertible matrix}

An $n\times n$ <square matrix> $A$ over a <field>, with $n\geq1$, is singular when it has no <matrix inverse>. Geometrically, its <linear map> loses at least one input direction: some nonzero <vector> $v$ satisfies $Av=0$, so $A(x+v)=Ax$. Over the <real numbers>, the following family makes that loss visible:
$$
A(t)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&t\end{pmatrix},\qquad A(t)(x,y)=(x,ty).
$$

\Image[singularity-nullity-collapse.png]
{title=Compression by $A(t)=\operatorname{diag}(1,t)$ becomes collapse only at $t=0$}
{description=The <square> $[-1,1]^2$ retains two-dimensional extent for $t=1$ and $t=0.15$, but maps to a segment at $t=0$. The orange points come from three inputs with the same first coordinate and different second coordinates. They coincide only at $t=0$. This is exact illustrative geometry, not measured data.}

For this <diagonal matrix>, $\det A(t)=t$. Whenever $t\ne0$, the input can be recovered as $(x,y)=(b_1,b_2/t)$ and the <kernel> is $\{0\}$. At $t=0$, the <kernel> becomes $\operatorname{span}\{(0,1)\}$: the entire second coordinate is forgotten. Consequently $A(0)x=(b_1,b_2)$ has no solution if $b_2\ne0$, and has the solution <straight line> $x=(b_1,s)$, $s\in\mathbb R$, if $b_2=0$. A <singular matrix> therefore need not make a particular <linear system> inconsistent; it prevents unique recovery when that <linear system> is consistent.

For an $n\times n$ <square matrix>, all these tests describe the same failure:
$$
\boxed{
A\text{ singular}
\iff\det A=0
\iff\operatorname{rank}A<n
\iff\ker A\ne\{0\}
\iff\operatorname{im}A\ne\mathbb F^n.
}
$$
Here $\mathbb F$ is the <field> of <scalars>. Equivalently, the columns are <linearly dependent>, the <linear map> is neither <injective> nor <surjective>, and some outputs cannot be reached. See the <invertible matrix> equivalences and https://www.ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06sc-linear-algebra-fall-2011/1963da71c4d96e5d14e7939780f79bcc_MIT18_06SCF11_Ses1.3sum.pdf[Strang's MIT 18.06SC lecture summary, Multiplication and inverse matrices, p. 2]. Over an infinite <field>, every consistent <linear system> for a <singular matrix> has infinitely many solutions; over a finite <field> with $q$ elements and <nullity> $d$, it has exactly $q^d$ solutions. This follows by adding each <kernel> <vector> to one solution.

The square hypothesis is essential. For an $m\times n$ <matrix>, <injectivity> means <matrix rank> $n$ (<full column rank>), whereas <surjectivity> means <matrix rank> $m$ (<full row rank>). A rectangular <matrix> can satisfy one condition without the other; its <kernel> and <image of a linear map> give the useful tests without calling it a <singular matrix>.

Exact singularity also differs from sensitivity. For $0<|t|\leq1$, $A(t)$ is an <invertible matrix>, but its smallest <singular value> is $|t|$ and its <condition number> in <Euclidean norm> is $1/|t|$. A change $\delta b=(0,\varepsilon)$ changes the recovered input by $\delta x=(0,\varepsilon/t)$: with $t=1/100$ and $\varepsilon=1/1000$, an output change of $0.001$ causes an input change of $0.1$. These are exact toy values, not measurements. The <condition number> quantifies this sensitivity before a direction is lost; see https://www.cs.cornell.edu/courses/cs322/2007sp/notes/condition.pdf[Steve Marschner, Condition numbers, §2]. A small <determinant> alone is not a reliable sensitivity test: $\varepsilon I_2$ has <determinant> $\varepsilon^2$ yet <condition number> one for every nonzero real $\varepsilon$, since both <singular values> shrink equally.

= Kernel of a linear map
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$\ker T=\{v:T(v)=0\}$}

= Kernel
{synonym}

= Null space
{synonym}

The <kernel of a linear map> $T:V\to W$ is the <vector subspace> of inputs sent to zero. <Linearity> makes it a <vector subspace>: if $T(v)=T(w)=0$, then $T(av+bw)=0$ for all <scalars> $a,b$. A <vector> in the <kernel> represents a change that the map cannot detect, since $T(x+v)=T(x)$ for every input $x$.

For $T:\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R^2$ given by $T(x,y,z)=(x+y,z)$, the zero-output equations are $x+y=0$ and $z=0$. Thus
$$
\ker T=\operatorname{span}\{(1,-1,0)\}.
$$
Increasing the first input while decreasing the second by the same amount leaves the output unchanged. For the <linear functional> $\varphi(x,y)=2x-y$, the <kernel> is the <straight line> $y=2x$ shown in its diagram. A <linear map> is <injective> exactly when its <kernel> is the zero <vector subspace>, because $T(v)=T(w)$ is equivalent to $T(v-w)=0$.

The <kernel> describes invisible changes, rather than all inputs that produce a specified nonzero output. If $T(x_0)=b$, the corresponding <fiber of a function> is the <affine solution space of a linear equation> $x_0+\ker T$. The difference of two inputs in that <fiber> belongs to the <kernel>; an input in that <fiber> need not. In particular, for $b\ne0$ the <fiber> cannot be a <vector subspace>, since it does not contain the <zero vector>.<Interactive Linear Algebra by Dan Margalit and others/Solution Sets>[Margalit and Rabinoff, Interactive Linear Algebra, §2.4.2, Key Observation and Remark: translate the homogeneous solution set by any particular solution]{ref}

For the <linear functional> $S:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb R$, $S(x,y)=x+2y$, the <kernel> is $\operatorname{span}\{(2,-1)\}$. Each <fiber> $S^{-1}(\{c\})$ is the parallel <straight line> $(c,0)+\operatorname{span}\{(2,-1)\}$. The input <vector space> is two-dimensional, but the output is one <scalar>: a whole <straight line> of inputs collapses to one point of the output <real line>.

\Image[kernel-rank-one-parallel-fibres.png]
{title=Parallel input fibres of $S(x,y)=x+2y$ map to scalar outputs}
{description=The blue input <straight line> is the <kernel>; the other colored input <straight lines> are its <translations>. The output panel is the one-dimensional <real line>, drawn vertically for alignment. All displayed geometry is exact illustrative mathematics.}

= Null direction of a linear map
{parent=Kernel of a linear map}
{title2=$v\ne0,\quad T(v)=0$}

= Null directions of a linear map
{synonym}

A <null direction of a linear map> $T$ is represented by a nonzero <vector> $v$ in its <kernel>. Every <scalar multiple> $tv$ is an undetectable displacement: $T(x+tv)=T(x)$. The <vector subspace> $\operatorname{span}\{v\}$ describes the direction independently of the chosen nonzero representative. More generally, a <basis> of the <kernel> supplies all independent <null directions of a linear map>; their number is the <nullity>. The <zero vector> belongs to the <kernel> but supplies no direction.

= Rectangular kernel and solution-fibre example
{parent=Kernel of a linear map}

Consider the <linear map> $A:\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R^2$ represented by the rectangular <matrix>
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2&0\\0&1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
A\begin{pmatrix}x\\y\\z\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}x+2y\\y+z\end{pmatrix}.
$$
To compute its <kernel>, solve the <homogeneous linear system> $x+2y=0$, $y+z=0$. Taking $y=t$ gives $x=-2t$, $z=-t$, so
$$
\ker A=\left\{t\begin{pmatrix}-2\\1\\-1\end{pmatrix}:t\in\mathbb R\right\},\qquad
v=\begin{pmatrix}-2\\1\\-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad Av=\begin{pmatrix}-2+2\\1-1\end{pmatrix}=0.
$$
Thus $v$ represents a <null direction of a linear map>: decreasing the first input by two, increasing the second by one, and decreasing the third by one changes neither output. Solving the <homogeneous linear system> is the concrete computation behind a <kernel>; the <column space> instead decides which outputs are attainable.<Linear Algebra, MIT 18.06, Spring 2010/Lecture 6: Column space and nullspace>[Gilbert Strang, Lecture 6: Column space and nullspace, opening lecture description: the distinct roles of Ax=0 and Ax=b]{ref}

Now prescribe the nonzero output $b=(3,2)^T$. The <linear system> $Au=b$ becomes $x+2y=3$, $y+z=2$. Again take $y=t$:
$$
u=\begin{pmatrix}3-2t\\t\\2-t\end{pmatrix}
=\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}3\\0\\2\end{pmatrix}}_{u_0}
+t\underbrace{\begin{pmatrix}-2\\1\\-1\end{pmatrix}}_{v},\qquad t\in\mathbb R.
$$
Direct substitution gives $Au_0=(3,2)^T$, and $A(u_0+tv)=Au_0+tAv=b$. Conversely, the two <linear equations> force exactly this form, so no solutions have been omitted. This <fiber> is an <affine subspace> of <dimension> one in the three-dimensional input <vector space>. It is parallel to the <kernel>, but does not pass through the origin. For instance, $u_0=(3,0,2)^T$ and $u_1=(1,1,1)^T$ both give $b$, while their difference $u_1-u_0=v$ gives zero. Adding $u_0$ and $u_1$ gives output $2b$, which is another way to see that this nonzero-output <fiber> is not a <vector subspace>.

\Image[kernel-rectangular-input-output-ambiguity.png]
{title=Three-dimensional inputs along one null direction give the same two-dimensional output}
{description=The blue <straight line> is the <kernel> in the actual three-dimensional input <vector space>. The orange solution <straight line> is its <translation> by $u_0$. Three marked inputs map to the single orange output $(3,2)$ in the two-dimensional output <vector space>; the blue <kernel> maps to zero. Finite plotted segments represent infinite <straight lines>.}

The first and third columns of $A$ are $(1,0)^T$ and $(0,1)^T$, a <basis> of the output <vector space>. Hence $A$ has <matrix rank> two and reaches every output. The <rank-nullity theorem> predicts <nullity> $3-2=1$ before we solve any <linear system>. It provides a useful completeness check: one nonzero <kernel> <vector> already forms a <basis> here, so a second independent <null direction of a linear map> would signal a calculation error. In general, <matrix rank> $r$ for an $m\times n$ <matrix> leaves $n-r$ independent <kernel> directions, rather than $m-r$.<Interactive Linear Algebra by Dan Margalit and others/The Rank Theorem>[Margalit and Rabinoff, Interactive Linear Algebra, §2.9, Rank Theorem: column count equals rank plus nullity]{ref}

= Noninjective linear measurement
{parent=Kernel of a linear map}
{title2=$T(x)=T(x')\iff x-x'\in\ker T$}

When a measurement model is a <linear map> $T$, its <kernel> records changes that its readout cannot distinguish. For example, the <rectangular kernel and solution-fibre example> measures only $x+2y$ and $y+z$. Even exact readouts $(3,2)$ leave the whole <fiber> $u_0+\ker A$ possible. Repeating either readout with greater precision cannot distinguish those inputs: their outputs agree exactly. This ambiguity follows from a nontrivial <kernel>, rather than from measurement error.

Additional information can remove a <null direction of a linear map>. In that example, adding a third <linear functional> that reads $y$ distinguishes $u_0+tv$, because its third readout is $t$. More generally, additional <linear functionals> $c_1,\ldots,c_k$ give unique recovery of all inputs exactly when
$$
\ker T\cap\ker c_1\cap\cdots\cap\ker c_k=\{0\}.
$$
This follows by applying the <trivial kernel> criterion to the combined <linear map> $x\mapsto(Tx,c_1(x),\ldots,c_k(x))$. Merely adding repeated <linear equations> does not meet this condition. On a finite-dimensional input <vector space>, each added <scalar> readout can increase the <matrix rank> by at most one, so at least $\dim\ker T$ extra independent readouts are needed for recovery of unrestricted inputs.

Restrictions on the possible inputs can also make recovery unique. If the allowed inputs form a <set> $D$, two allowed inputs are indistinguishable exactly when their difference is in the <kernel>. Thus $T$ is <injective> on $D$ precisely when no difference of distinct points of $D$ is a <kernel> <vector>. Such a restriction is extra information about the input; the original readout alone determines only its <fiber>.

= Nullity of a linear map
{parent=Kernel of a linear map}
{title2=$n(T)=\dim\ker T$}

= Nullity
{synonym}

The <nullity of a linear map> $T:V\to W$ is $n(T)=\dim\ker T$, the <dimension> of its <kernel>. It counts independent input directions that can change without changing the output. For a <finite-dimensional vector space> $V$, the <rank-nullity theorem> gives
$$
n(T)=\dim V-\operatorname{rank}T.
$$
For an $m\times n$ <matrix> $A$ of <matrix rank> $r$, this is $n-r$, using the number of columns, not the number of rows. This dimension identity is <Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[Sheldon Axler, Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition, §3B, theorem 3.21]{ref}.

The <nullity> answers a practical question about a <linear system>: once one input is known to produce the requested output, how much freedom remains? If $T(x_0)=b$, its <fiber of a function> over $b$ is
$$
\boxed{T^{-1}(\{b\})=x_0+\ker T.}
$$
Indeed, $T(x)=T(x_0)$ exactly when $T(x-x_0)=0$. Thus every nonempty <fiber> is an <affine subspace> of <dimension> $n(T)$, with the same direction space as the <kernel>. The <kernel> itself is the <fiber> over zero. Outputs outside the <image of a linear map> have empty <fibers>; the <nullity> does not guarantee existence, and we assign no solution-space <dimension> to an empty <fiber> here.

\Image[singularity-nullity-fibers.png]
{title=Nullity one gives parallel solution lines for $F(x,y)=(x+y,0)$}
{description=Each colored input <straight line> $x+y=c$ is the <fiber> over the corresponding output $(c,0)$. The blue line through the origin is the <kernel>. The orange inputs $(1,0)$ and $(0,1)$ give the same output; their difference belongs to the <kernel>. The output $(1,1)$ lies outside the <image of a linear map> and has no input. Exact toy geometry, not measured data.}

In the diagram, $F:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb R^2$ has <matrix> $\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$, <matrix rank> one and <nullity> one. Its <kernel> has <basis> $(1,-1)$, and the full solution of $F(x,y)=(c,0)$ is $(x,y)=(c,0)+s(1,-1)$. One free <scalar> moves the solution along a <straight line>; the output $(c,1)$ is impossible.

The existing three-input example $T(x,y,z)=(x+y,z)$ instead has <rank> two and <nullity> one. Its <image of a linear map> is all of $\mathbb R^2$, and
$$
T^{-1}(\{(a,b)\})=(a,0,b)+\operatorname{span}\{(1,-1,0)\}.
$$
Every output has a solution <straight line>. In contrast, $U(x,y,z)=(x+y,0)$ has <rank> one and <nullity> two, with <kernel> <basis> $(1,-1,0),(0,0,1)$. Its reachable output $(a,0)$ has the solution <affine subspace>
$$
(x,y,z)=(a,0,0)+s(1,-1,0)+u(0,0,1),\qquad s,u\in\mathbb R,
$$
a plane, while $(a,b)$ with $b\ne0$ has no solution. These two <linear maps> have the same input and output dimensions but different freedom and reachability. Computing the <kernel> separates what an output determines from what an extra constraint would have to fix. For either example, a <basis> of the <kernel> gives one independent free <scalar> per <basis vector>, which is why <nullity> measures freedom rather than the number of solutions.

= Trivial kernel
{parent=Kernel of a linear map}

A <linear map> $T:V\to W$ has a trivial <kernel> when $\ker T=\{0_V\}$: the <zero vector> is the only input producing the zero output. The <kernel> is a <singleton set>, not the <empty set>, because every <linear map> sends zero to zero. Equivalently, its <nullity> is zero and $T$ is <injective>. This holds even without a finite-dimensional hypothesis; see <Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>.

The reason this zero-output test controls all outputs is <linearity>:
$$
T(v)=T(w)\iff T(v-w)=0\iff v-w\in\ker T.
$$
With a <trivial kernel>, equal outputs force equal inputs. Therefore every equation $T(x)=b$ has at most one solution. Existence is a separate question: $b$ must belong to the <image of a linear map>. In the <fiber> description under <nullity>, $x_0+\{0\}$ is just the single point $x_0$.

For example, the <linear map> $J:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb R^3$, $J(x,y)=(x,y,0)$, has <trivial kernel> and <full column rank>:
$$
J=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\ker J=\{0\},\qquad\operatorname{rank}J=2.
$$
The output $(3,4,0)$ comes from exactly $(3,4)$, while $(3,4,1)$ is unreachable. Conversely, $P:\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R^2$, $P(x,y,z)=(x,y)$, is <surjective> and has <full row rank>, but its <kernel> is $\operatorname{span}\{(0,0,1)\}$: every output $(a,b)$ comes from the solution <straight line> $(a,b,s)$.

For an $m\times n$ <matrix>, a <trivial kernel> is equivalent to <matrix rank> $n$ and <linear independence> of its columns. It can therefore occur only if $n\leq m$. If $m=n$, the <rank-nullity theorem> also forces <surjectivity>, giving an <invertible matrix>; if $m>n$, the example $J$ shows why unique recovery of reachable inputs does not imply that every output is reachable. Finally, a <trivial kernel> guarantees exact uniqueness without guaranteeing numerical stability: the <singular matrix> example $A(t)$ has <trivial kernel> for every $t\ne0$, even as its <condition number> grows without bound when $t$ approaches zero.

= Rank-nullity theorem
{parent=Kernel of a linear map}
{wiki}
{title2=$\dim V=\dim\ker T+\dim\operatorname{im}T$}

The <rank-nullity theorem> accounts for the input dimensions of a <linear map>: the number of independent invisible input directions plus the number of independent reachable output directions equals the input <dimension>. For $T:V\to W$ over a <field>, with finite-dimensional <domain of a function> $V$,
$$
\boxed{\dim V=\dim\ker T+\dim\operatorname{im}T.}
$$
Here $\dim\ker T$ is the <nullity>, and $\dim\operatorname{im}T$ is the <rank of a linear map>. The <kernel> lives in the input <vector space>, while the <image of a linear map> lives in the output <vector space>. For an $m$-by-$n$ <matrix> of <matrix rank> $r$, the formula is $n=(n-r)+r$: the number of columns counts input coordinates. <Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[Sheldon Axler, Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition, §3B: fundamental theorem of linear maps]{ref}

For a computed example over the <real numbers>, take
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
T(x,y)=A\begin{pmatrix}x\\y\end{pmatrix}=(x+2y,0).
$$
The <kernel> is $\operatorname{span}\{(-2,1)\}$ and the <image of a linear map> is $\operatorname{span}\{(1,0)\}$. Each has <dimension> one, so the two input dimensions split as $2=1+1$. The <vector> $(3,1)$ can be written
$$
(3,1)=(1,2)+(2,-1),\qquad
T(1,2)=(5,0),\qquad T(2,-1)=(0,0).
$$
The second summand is undetectable: $T(3,1)=T(1,2)$. The picture uses the standard <dot product> to choose a visible direction perpendicular to the <kernel>; this is a convenient geometric choice, not an extra hypothesis of the <rank-nullity theorem>.

\Image[rank-nullity-domain-decomposition.png]
{title=Two input dimensions split into one visible direction and one kernel direction}
{description=For $T(x,y)=(x+2y,0)$, the input $(3,1)$ splits into $(1,2)$ and $(2,-1)$. Both $(3,1)$ and $(1,2)$ map to $(5,0)$, while the <kernel> component maps to zero. The horizontal output line is the entire <image of a linear map>.}

This explains a practical tradeoff in a <linear system>. If $T(x_0)=b$, every other input with output $b$ is $x_0+k$ for $k\in\ker T$. The <nullity> therefore counts independent freedom in an existing solution; the <rank> counts independent reachable output directions. In the example, $T(x,y)=(3,0)$ has all solutions $(x,y)=(3-2t,t)$, while $T(x,y)=(3,1)$ has none. The <cokernel> records this failure of reachability.

For an $m$-by-$n$ <matrix>, <injectivity> requires <matrix rank> $n$, and <surjectivity> requires <matrix rank> $m$. When $m\ne n$, these conditions differ. For example, $B:\mathbb R^3\to\mathbb R^2$ with $B(x,y,z)=(x+2y,0)$ has <rank> one and <nullity> two, because $\ker B=\operatorname{span}\{(-2,1,0),(0,0,1)\}$. The input accounting is $3=2+1$, although only one of two output directions is reached.

Proof outline: extend a <basis> $k_1,\ldots,k_q$ of the <kernel> to a <basis> $k_1,\ldots,k_q,v_1,\ldots,v_r$ of $V$. Every output is a <linear combination> of $T(v_1),\ldots,T(v_r)$ because the $k_i$ vanish under $T$. If a <linear combination> of the $T(v_j)$ is zero, the corresponding combination of the $v_j$ lies in the <kernel>; <linear independence> of the full <basis> forces all its coefficients to vanish. Thus the added <basis vectors> give a <basis> of the <image of a linear map>. Counting them proves the formula.

= Equality in the rank-sum and nullity-product inequalities
{parent=Rank-nullity theorem}

For <endomorphisms> $S,T$ of a finite-dimensional space of <dimension> $d$, $r(S+T)\le r(S)+r(T)$ and $n(ST)\le n(S)+n(T)$. If both equalities hold, the first gives $r(S)+r(T)\le d$, while the second gives $2d-r(S)-r(T)\le d$. Thus the <rank> sum is $d$, $S+T$ is an <isomorphism>, and $ST=0$. Conversely, $ST=0$ implies $\operatorname{im}T\subseteq\ker S$ and hence <rank> sum at most $d$, whereas invertibility of $S+T$ gives <rank> sum at least $d$. This proves both equalities.

= Kernel jump in a parameter-dependent linear map
{parent=Rank-nullity theorem}

For a matrix whose entries depend on a parameter, a nonzero maximal minor certifies a fixed <matrix rank> away from its zeros. At a zero, other minors must still be checked: one vanishing minor does not itself imply a rank drop. Once the <matrix rank> is determined, the <rank-nullity theorem> gives the dimension of the <kernel of a linear map>. Exceptional parameters can then be solved separately to exhibit the additional <kernel of a linear map> directions.

= Cokernel
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$\operatorname{coker}T=W/\operatorname{im}T$}

The <cokernel> records output information that a <linear map> cannot produce. For $T:V\to W$, it is the <quotient vector space>
$$
\operatorname{coker}T=W/\operatorname{im}T.
$$
Its elements are <cosets> $[b]=b+\operatorname{im}T$. Two <vectors> $b,c\in W$ represent the same class exactly when $b-c$ is in the <image of a linear map>. The zero class is the whole <image of a linear map>, so
$$
\boxed{T(x)=b\text{ is solvable}\iff[b]=0\text{ in }\operatorname{coker}T.}
$$
Changing $b$ by an attainable output does not change its obstruction. This is useful because one can test compatibility before trying to find an input. The <quotient vector space> construction is described in Axler's §3E. <Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[Sheldon Axler, Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition, §3E: quotient spaces]{ref}

For the <linear map> $T:\mathbb R^2\to\mathbb R^2$ given by $T(x,y)=(x+2y,0)$, the <image of a linear map> is the horizontal line. All outputs $(b_1,b_2)$ with the same second coordinate have the same <cokernel> class, because their difference is horizontal. Thus this <cokernel> is <isomorphic> to $\mathbb R$ via $[(b_1,b_2)]\mapsto b_2$. In particular, $(3,0)$ has zero class and is attainable, while $(3,1)$ has nonzero class and is unattainable.

\Image[cokernel-solvability-obstruction.png]
{title=The second output coordinate is a cokernel obstruction to solving $T(x,y)=b$}
{description=The horizontal axis is the <image of a linear map> $T(x,y)=(x+2y,0)$. The class of $b=(3,1)$ is the parallel line $b+\operatorname{im}T$, which never meets the image. The projection $(3,0)$ is attainable but has a different <cokernel> class.}

If $W$ is finite-dimensional, the dimension rule for a <quotient vector space> gives
$$
\dim W=\operatorname{rank}T+\dim\operatorname{coker}T.
$$
Compare this with the <rank-nullity theorem>, $\dim V=\operatorname{rank}T+\dim\ker T$. The <kernel> counts invisible inputs; the <cokernel> counts missing output directions. They need not have the same <dimension>: $B(x,y,z)=(x+2y,0)$ has a two-dimensional <kernel> and a one-dimensional <cokernel>. A <linear map> is <surjective> exactly when its <cokernel> is zero.

The <cokernel> is a quotient, rather than a chosen perpendicular <vector subspace>. With a finite-dimensional <inner product space> as output, one may represent each class by its unique component in the <orthogonal complement> of the <image of a linear map>. That representation depends on the chosen <inner product>. For <matrices> over the <complex numbers>, this complement is $\ker A^*$, leading to the <cokernel compatibility equations>.

The same quotient definition applies to a <group homomorphism> of <abelian groups>. For $T:\mathbb Z\to\mathbb Z$ given by $T(n)=2n$, the <cokernel> is $\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$. The equation $2n=b$ is solvable exactly when $b$ is an <even integer>; the two <cosets> record <integer parity>. A quotient captures the obstruction here just as it captures a missing output coordinate for a <linear map>.

= Cokernel compatibility equations
{parent=Cokernel}
{title2=$Ax=b\text{ solvable}\iff y^*b=0\ \forall y\in\ker A^*$}

For a <matrix> $A$ over the <complex numbers> with the standard <Hermitian inner product>, the <linear system> $Ax=b$ is solvable exactly when
$$
y^*b=0\qquad\text{for every }y\in\ker A^*.
$$
The star is the <conjugate transpose>; for <matrices> over the <real numbers>, use the <transpose>. Necessity follows from $y^*Ax=(A^*y)^*x=0$. For sufficiency, $(\operatorname{im}A)^\perp=\ker A^*$, and taking the <orthogonal complement> again recovers $\operatorname{im}A$ in finite <dimension>. This is the <cokernel> obstruction expressed as scalar equations. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06-linear-algebra-spring-2010/resources/lecture-10-the-four-fundamental-subspaces/[Gilbert Strang, MIT 18.06, Lecture 10: the four fundamental subspaces]{ref}

For $A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$, $\ker A^T=\operatorname{span}\{(0,1)\}$, so the single compatibility equation is $b_2=0$. If it holds, the <kernel> supplies the remaining input freedom; if it fails, changing the input cannot remove the obstruction.

= Dual space
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$V^*=\operatorname{Hom}_F(V,F)$}

= Dual vector space
{synonym}

= Algebraic dual
{synonym}

The <dual space> of a <vector space> $V$ over a <field> $F$ is the <vector space> of all <linear functionals> $\varphi:V\to F$. Think of a <vector> as an input and a <linear functional> as a scalar-valued measurement of that input. For $V=\mathbb R^2$, the rules $(x,y)\mapsto x$, $(x,y)\mapsto y$, and $(x,y)\mapsto2x-y$ are different elements of the <dual space>. None needs a notion of <length> or <angle>.

Its operations combine measurements pointwise:
$$
(\varphi+\psi)(v)=\varphi(v)+\psi(v),\qquad
(a\varphi)(v)=a\varphi(v).
$$
The <dual basis> explains the finite-dimensional structure: specifying a <linear functional>'s values on a <basis> determines its value on every <vector>. Thus an $n$-dimensional <vector space> has an $n$-dimensional <dual space>. The spaces have the same <dimension>, but their elements play different roles: an input and a rule that measures an input. <Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[Axler, Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition, §3F, definitions 3.109–3.112]{ref}.

This separation is useful when changing coordinates. The <dual pairing> $\varphi(v)$ must stay the same while the component lists for $v$ and $\varphi$ change in opposite ways. <Covector transformation under a change of basis> makes the compensation explicit. An <inner product> on a finite-dimensional real <vector space> gives one way to represent each <linear functional> by a <vector>, but that representation uses the chosen <inner product>; the <dual space> itself does not require one.

Here <dual space> means the <algebraic dual>: all <linear functionals>. For a <topological vector space>, the <continuous dual space> keeps only the <continuous linear functionals>. In infinite <dimension>, this can be a proper <vector subspace>; <discontinuous functional detected by vanishing sequence tails> gives an example. The distinction matters when a scalar-valued measurement must respond continuously to small input changes.

= Dual pairing
{parent=Dual space}
{title2=$\langle\varphi,v\rangle=\varphi(v)$}

= Evaluation pairing
{synonym}

The <dual pairing> evaluates a <linear functional> on a <vector>:
$$
\langle\varphi,v\rangle=\varphi(v),\qquad (\varphi,v)\in V^*\times V.
$$
It is <bilinear>: it is linear in each argument separately. Even over the <complex numbers>, this algebraic evaluation has no <complex conjugation>. An <inner product> instead has two <vectors> as inputs and, over the <complex numbers>, is <sesquilinear>. For $\varphi(x,y)=2x-y$ and $v=(3,1)$, the <dual pairing> equals five. Its value is independent of the <basis> used to describe its two inputs.

= Algebraic dual of a direct sum
{parent=Dual space}
{title2=$(\bigoplus_iV_i)^*\cong\prod_iV_i^*$}

Restricting a <linear functional> to each summand gives a family of functionals, with no finite-support restriction. Conversely an arbitrary such family acts on a vector by the finite sum of its nonzero coordinates. These constructions are inverse and prove the displayed algebraic <dual space> identity. A group action on the summands induces the product action by their algebraic contragredients.

= Bidual of a normed space
{parent=Dual space}
{title2=$X^{**}$}

= Bidual space
{synonym}

The bidual of a <normed vector space> $X$ is the continuous dual $X^{**}=(X^*)^*$. Evaluation defines the <canonical embedding into the bidual> $J_X(x)(f)=f(x)$. The <norm> inequality gives $\|J_Xx\|\le\|x\|$, and a norming functional from the <Hahn-Banach theorem> gives equality. Thus $J_X$ is isometric, and its image is dense in its closure, a <completion of a normed space>.

= Perfect pairing
{parent=Dual space}
{title2=$B:V\times W\to F$}

For finite-dimensional <vector spaces> $V,W$ over a <field> $F$, a bilinear pairing $B:V\times W\to F$ is perfect when the induced <linear maps> $V\to W^*$ and $W\to V^*$ are <isomorphisms>. Equivalently, both dimensions agree and the matrix of the pairing in any two bases is invertible. The evaluation pairing between a finite-dimensional <vector space> and its <dual space> is perfect. This notion allows two different spaces; a <nondegenerate bilinear form> is the case $V=W$.

= Separating subspace of an algebraic dual
{parent=Dual space}

= Separating subspaces of an algebraic dual
{synonym}

A <linear subspace> $W\subseteq X^*$ is separating if every nonzero $x\in X$ has $T(x)\ne0$ for some $T\in W$. For finite-dimensional $X$, an evaluation map $X\to F^{\dim W}$ is <injective>, so the <rank-nullity theorem> forces $\dim W\geq\dim X=\dim X^*$ and $W=X^*$. For the infinite-dimensional <polynomial> space, the span of its coefficient functionals is a proper separating <linear subspace> of the <dual space>, which contains all sequences of coefficients, not just finitely supported ones.

= Bidual evaluation map
{parent=Dual space}
{title2=$J(x)(T)=T(x)$}

For an algebraic <dual space> $X^*=\operatorname{Hom}_F(X,F)$, the <linear map> $J:X\to X^{**}$ sends $x$ to evaluation at $x$. It is <injective> because <linear functionals> separate nonzero vectors: extend any nonzero vector to a <basis> and use its coordinate functional. If $X$ has a finite <basis> $e_i$ with <dual basis> $e_i^*$, every $B\in X^{**}$ equals $J(\sum_iB(e_i^*)e_i)$, so $J$ is an <isomorphism>. For infinite-dimensional spaces, <surjection> does not follow from this finite sum argument.

= Covector
{parent=Dual space}
{title2=$\varphi\in V^*$}

A <covector> is an element of the <dual space>, hence a <linear functional> on a <vector space>. In finite-dimensional coordinates, it acts as a row multiplied by a <coordinate vector> column. The row $\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\end{pmatrix}$ acts on $\begin{pmatrix}3\\1\end{pmatrix}$ to give five. The terminology emphasizes its role as a scalar-valued measurement and its behavior under a <change of basis>.

For example, the <total derivative> of $f(x,y)=x^2+y^2$ at $(1,2)$ sends a small displacement $h$ to the linear change $df(h)=2h_x+4h_y$. This <covector> describes the first-order response. The <gradient> $\nabla f=(2,4)$ represents the same rule through the chosen Euclidean <inner product>. In <differential geometry>, the <cotangent space> collects these scalar-valued measurements of <tangent vectors>.

= Covector transformation under a change of basis
{parent=Covector}
{title2=$x'=B^{-1}x,\quad\ell'=\ell B$}

Let the columns of an <invertible matrix> $B$ be a new <basis> expressed in the old <basis>. The same <vector> has old <coordinate vector> $x$ and new <coordinate vector> $x'=B^{-1}x$. If a <covector> has old row $\ell$, its new row is $\ell'=\ell B$, since
$$
\ell'x'=\ell B B^{-1}x=\ell x.
$$
Thus the scalar <dual pairing> stays unchanged. If <covector> coefficients are stored as a column $c=\ell^T$, their new column is $c'=B^Tc$; this is the inverse transpose of the transformation $x\mapsto B^{-1}x$ on <vector> coordinates. These conventions express the <covariance and contravariance of vectors>. https://www.math.brown.edu/streil/papers/LADW/HTML_2026_04-30/Ch8.html[Sergei Treil, Linear Algebra Done Wrong, §8.1.1: coordinate changes in the dual space]{ref}.

For the <basis> $b_1=(1,0)$, $b_2=(1,1)$,
$$
B=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
x=\begin{pmatrix}3\\1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
x'=\begin{pmatrix}2\\1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The <linear functional> $\varphi(x,y)=2x-y$ has rows $\ell=\begin{pmatrix}2&-1\end{pmatrix}$ and $\ell'=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\end{pmatrix}$. Both calculations give five: $2\cdot3-1\cdot1=2\cdot2+1\cdot1$. Changing the <basis> changes the descriptions, not the measured value.

= Linear functional
{parent=Dual space}
{wiki}
{title2=$\varphi:V\to F$}

= Linear form
{synonym}

A <linear functional> is a <linear map> from a <vector space> $V$ to its scalar <field> $F$:
$$
\varphi(av+bw)=a\varphi(v)+b\varphi(w),\qquad a,b\in F,\quad v,w\in V.
$$
Its output is one <scalar>, rather than an arbitrary <vector> in another space. In $\mathbb R^2$, $\varphi(x,y)=2x-y$ gives $\varphi(3,1)=5$, $\varphi(1,2)=0$, and $\varphi(4,3)=5$: adding the two inputs adds their measured values.

The <level sets> $\varphi(v)=c$ visualize what the measurement retains and discards. For this example they are parallel <straight lines> $y=2x-c$. Moving parallel to $(1,2)$ changes the input but leaves $\varphi$ unchanged. The zero <level set> is its <kernel of a linear map>, while the other <level sets> are translates of that <vector subspace>.

\Image[linear-functional-level-sets.png]
{title=A <linear functional> groups <vectors> into parallel <level sets> with the same measured value}

Inputs need not be geometric arrows. On the <vector space> of <polynomials> of <polynomial degree> at most two, evaluating at zero is a <linear functional>, as is taking the <definite integral> from zero to one. For $p(t)=a+bt+ct^2$, they give $p(0)=a$ and $\int_0^1p(t)\,dt=a+b/2+c/3$. Thus <polynomial evaluation> and <integration> are two different measurements of the same <polynomial>. Coordinate extraction is another measurement, developed under <dual basis>.

A <linear functional> is defined by <linearity>, not by <continuity>. In finite-dimensional real or complex <normed vector spaces> it is automatically continuous; in infinite <dimension> it need not be. The <continuous dual space> adds this requirement when small changes in the input should give small changes in the output.

= Minimizing a linear functional on a sphere
{parent=Linear functional}
{title2=$\min_{\|z\|=r}z\cdot w=-r\|w\|$}

For a nonzero Euclidean vector $w$, the <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality> gives $z\cdot w\geq-\|w\|$ when $\|z\|=1$. Equality is attained uniquely at $z=-w/\|w\|$. On a sphere of radius $r>0$ the minimizer is $-rw/\|w\|$, and the minimum is $-r\|w\|$. This turns many constrained vector problems into a normalization of one fixed vector. If $w=0$, every point minimizes the functional.

= Discontinuous functional detected by vanishing sequence tails
{parent=Linear functional}
{title2=$f(v_N)=1,\quad\|v_N\|_2\to0$}

If a <linear functional> on a <normed vector space> has $f(v_N)=1$ for a sequence tending to zero in norm, it is discontinuous and unbounded. An explicit example is the algebraic span in $\ell^2$ of the coordinate vectors and $w=(2^{-n})_{n\geq1}$. Define $f(w)=1$ and $f(e_n)=0$. The tails $v_N=w-\sum_{n=1}^N2^{-n}e_n$ have norm $2^{-N}/\sqrt3$ but image one. Thus the functional vanishes on the dense finite-support subspace while being nonzero elsewhere.

= Dual basis
{parent=Dual space}
{wiki}
{title2=$e_i^*(e_j)=\delta_{ij}$}

= Dual bases
{synonym}

For a <basis> $e_1,\ldots,e_n$ of a finite-dimensional <vector space> $V$, its <dual basis> consists of <linear functionals> $e_1^*,\ldots,e_n^*$ satisfying
$$
e_i^*(e_j)=\delta_{ij}.
$$
The <Kronecker delta> means that each rule gives one on its own <basis vector> and zero on the others. These rules extract coordinates:
$$
v=\sum_{i=1}^n e_i^*(v)e_i.
$$
Applying $e_i^*$ to a <linear combination> removes every term except the $i$th. Consequently the <dual basis> is a <basis> of the <dual space>, and every <linear functional> has expansion $\varphi=\sum_i\varphi(e_i)e_i^*$. <Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[Axler, Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition, §3F, definitions and results 3.112–3.116]{ref}.

Coordinate extraction works for a nonorthogonal <basis> too. With $b_1=(1,0)$ and $b_2=(1,1)$, the <dual basis> is
$$
b_1^*(x,y)=x-y,\qquad b_2^*(x,y)=y.
$$
The checks $b_1^*(b_1)=1$, $b_1^*(b_2)=0$, $b_2^*(b_1)=0$, and $b_2^*(b_2)=1$ fix the two rules. For $v=(3,1)$ they give **coordinates $(2,1)$**, so $v=2b_1+b_2$. The usual <dot products> with $b_1,b_2$ instead give $3,4$ and do not extract these coordinates: simple projection formulas depend on an <orthonormal basis>.

\Image[dual-basis-skew-coordinates.png]
{title=The <dual basis> extracts coefficients in a skew <basis> by two scalar-valued measurements}

The same idea explains <polynomial interpolation>. On <polynomials> of <polynomial degree> at most $n$, evaluation at distinct <interpolation nodes> $x_0,\ldots,x_n$ gives the <dual basis> to the <Lagrange cardinal polynomials>, because $\ell_i(x_j)=\delta_{ij}$. Samples are coordinates in this <basis>, so the reconstruction $p=\sum_i p(x_i)\ell_i$ is a dual-basis expansion. <Differentiation weights from nodal evaluations> and <factorial monomial basis for polynomial differentiation> apply the same principle to different measurements.

= Coordinate functional
{parent=Dual basis}
{title2=$e_i^*(\sum_j a_je_j)=a_i$}

A <coordinate functional> extracts one coefficient in a specified finite <basis>. It is an element of that <basis>'s <dual basis>, rather than a coordinate rule independent of the <basis>. For the <standard basis> of $F^n$, the $i$th <coordinate functional> sends $(a_1,\ldots,a_n)$ to $a_i$.

= Factorial monomial basis for polynomial differentiation
{parent=Dual basis}
{title2=$p_j(x)=x^j/j!$}

On polynomials of degree at most $n$, the derivative-evaluation functionals $e_j(\phi)=\phi^{(j)}(0)$ are dual to $p_j=x^j/j!$, since $e_i(p_j)=\delta_{ij}$. Differentiation maps $p_j$ to $p_{j-1}$, with $p_0$ mapped to zero. Its matrix is a single nilpotent Jordan block with ones above the diagonal, and its dual has the transpose matrix.

= Annihilator of a vector subspace
{parent=Dual space}
{title2=$U^\circ=\{\varphi\in V^*: \varphi|_U=0\}$}

= Annihilators of vector subspaces
{synonym}

For a <vector subspace> $U\leq V$,
$$
U^\circ=\{f\in V^*:f|_U=0\}.
$$
In finite <dimension>, $\dim U^\circ=\dim V-\dim U$.

An <annihilator of a vector subspace> collects the <linear functionals> that ignore every input from that <vector subspace>. For $U=\operatorname{span}\{(1,2)\}\subseteq\mathbb R^2$, a <linear functional> $\varphi(x,y)=ax+by$ vanishes on $U$ exactly when $a+2b=0$. Hence $U^\circ$ is the one-dimensional <span> of $(x,y)\mapsto2x-y$. The zero <level set> in the <linear functional> diagram is precisely $U$.

= Transpose of a linear map
{parent=Dual space}
{wiki}
{title2=$T^*:W^*\to V^*,\quad T^*(\varphi)=\varphi\circ T$}

= Dual map
{synonym}

For a <linear map> $T:V\to W$, the <dual map> $T^*:W^*\to V^*$ sends a <linear functional> $\varphi$ on the output space to the input measurement $T^*(\varphi)=\varphi\circ T$. It reverses direction because a measurement of the output can be evaluated on an input by first applying $T$:
$$
(T^*\varphi)(v)=\varphi(Tv).
$$
For $T(x,y)=(x+y,2y)$ and $\varphi(u,v)=2u-v$, the input measurement is $T^*\varphi(x,y)=2(x+y)-2y=2x$. The output measurement ignores the part of this transformation contributed by $y$.

If $T$ has <matrix> $A$ in specified <bases>, a row representing $\varphi$ changes to $\ell A$. Storing the coefficients of a <linear functional> as a column in the corresponding <dual basis> gives $A^T\ell^T$, explaining why the <transpose of a linear map> is represented by the <matrix transpose>. This algebraic operation uses no <inner product>; it agrees with a matrix transpose even over the <complex numbers>, rather than automatically introducing <complex conjugation>. The <adjoint operator> is defined using an <inner product> and is a different construction. https://www.math.brown.edu/streil/papers/LADW/HTML_2026_04-30/Ch8.html[Treil, Linear Algebra Done Wrong, §8.3: dual transformations and transpose]{ref}.

In finite <dimension>, the <kernel> and image of the <dual map> are described by <annihilators of vector subspaces>:
$$
\ker T^*=(\operatorname{im}T)^\circ,
\qquad
\operatorname{im}T^*=(\ker T)^\circ.
$$

= Dual image and kernel annihilator identity
{parent=Transpose of a linear map}
{title2=$\operatorname{im}(\alpha^\vee)=(\ker\alpha)^\circ$}

For a <linear map> between finite-dimensional <vector spaces>, the image of its <dual map> is the <annihilator of a vector subspace> given by its kernel. Inclusion follows by composition. Conversely a <linear functional> vanishing on the kernel descends to the image and extends to the target by <basis extension>. The identity and the <rank-nullity theorem> show that a linear map and its dual have equal rank.

= Equality of row rank and column rank
{parent=Transpose of a linear map}

For the map represented by a matrix $A$, column rank is the rank of the map and row rank is the rank of its dual. Since the dual map has kernel equal to the annihilator of the original image, rank-nullity gives equal ranks.

= Simultaneous basis exchange from a nonzero minor
{parent=Equality of row rank and column rank}

If the columns $v_1,\ldots,v_m$ of an $n$ by $m$ matrix are independent, some $m$ by $m$ row minor is nonzero. A nonzero term of that minor's determinant selects distinct nonzero coordinates $f(i)$, and replacing the corresponding standard basis vectors $e_{f(i)}$ by the $v_i$ leaves a basis.

= Duals of a subspace and its quotient
{parent=Transpose of a linear map}

For a finite-dimensional vector space and $U\leq V$, dualizing the quotient and inclusion maps gives
$$
(V/U)^*\cong U^\circ,
\qquad
U^*\cong V^*/U^\circ.
$$

= Surjectivity of independent linear functionals
{parent=Dual space}

If $q_1,\ldots,q_n\in V^*$ are linearly independent, then
$$
x\longmapsto(q_1(x),\ldots,q_n(x))
$$
maps $V$ onto $F^n$. Otherwise a nonzero functional annihilating its proper image would give a nontrivial linear relation among the $q_j$.

= Polynomial moment functional
{parent=Dual space}

A polynomial moment functional evaluates a polynomial through a weighted integral, such as $p\mapsto\int p(t)w(t)dt$; finitely many moments determine it on a bounded-degree polynomial space.

= Rank of a matrix with affine columns
{parent=Linear algebra}

If the $j$th column of a matrix is $v+c_j\mathbf1$, then its image lies in $\operatorname{span}\{v,\mathbf1\}$. If $v$ is not constant and at least two $c_j$ differ, the rank is exactly two; for $m$ columns the nullity is $m-2$.

= Rank-one matrix update
{parent=Linear algebra}
{title2=$B=A+uv^T$}

A <rank-one matrix update> changes a <matrix> $A$ by an <outer product> $uv^T$. For real <vectors> $u,v\in\mathbb R^n$, its action on an input <vector> $x$ is
$$
(A+uv^T)x=Ax+u(v^Tx).
$$
The <dot product> $v^Tx$ reads one <scalar> from the input, and the correction moves the output along $u$. All correction outputs therefore lie in one <linear span>. If either <vector> is zero, the update is zero; otherwise the correction is a <rank-one matrix>. The full updated <matrix> need not have rank one.

For example, choose
$$
u=\begin{pmatrix}1\\2\end{pmatrix},\qquad
v=\begin{pmatrix}3\\-1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
uv^T=\begin{pmatrix}3&-1\\6&-2\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The update sends $x=(x_1,x_2)^T$ to the correction $(3x_1-x_2)(1,2)^T$. Although all four entries change, there is only one direction of correction. Adding it to the <identity matrix> gives $B=\begin{pmatrix}4&-1\\6&-1\end{pmatrix}$.

This special structure allows existing work to be reused. The <matrix determinant lemma> updates the <determinant> using one <scalar>, and the <Sherman–Morrison formula> updates the <matrix inverse> using an <outer product>. Such formulas are useful when a <linear system> changes slightly after a new observation or a parameter adjustment: the unchanged part can retain its <LU decomposition>. The term rank-one describes the rank of the *correction*, not the size of the entry changes. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-065-matrix-methods-in-data-analysis-signal-processing-and-machine-learning-spring-2018/988b4c05680f40a5a4b3be7004a32500_XhSk_Lw2X_U.pdf[Gilbert Strang, MIT 18.065, Lecture 14: Low Rank Changes in A and Its Inverse]{ref}.

= Matrix determinant lemma
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$\det(A+uv^T)=\det(A)(1+v^TA^{-1}u)$}

For an <invertible matrix> $A\in\mathbb R^{n\times n}$ and real <vectors> $u,v\in\mathbb R^n$, the <matrix determinant lemma> states
$$
\det(A+uv^T)=\det(A)\left(1+v^TA^{-1}u\right).
$$
The expression $v^TA^{-1}u$ is a <scalar>, so a <rank-one matrix update> changes the <determinant> by a single multiplicative factor. One need not expand the updated <determinant> entry by entry.

For the preceding example with $A=I$, the <dot product> is $v^Tu=3-2=1$. Hence the updated <determinant> is $1+1=2$. Direct evaluation confirms
$$
\det\begin{pmatrix}4&-1\\6&-1\end{pmatrix}
=4(-1)-(-1)6=2.
$$
More generally, let $B(t)=I+tuv^T$ for a real <scalar> $t$. The same calculation gives $\det B(t)=1+t$. The <determinant> is one at $t=0$, two at $t=1$, and zero at $t=-1$.

\Image[rank-one-matrix-update-geometry.png]
{title=A rank-one correction can preserve, stretch, or collapse the area of a square}
{description=Each panel maps the unit <square> by $B(t)=I+tuv^T$ with $u=(1,2)^T$ and $v=(3,-1)^T$. Blue and orange arrows are the images of the two <standard basis> <vectors>. At $t=1$, the <parallelogram> has <area> two, despite its strong skew. At $t=-1$, the images are <linearly dependent> and the full <square> collapses onto a <line>. Panel scales differ; the stated areas come from the <determinant>, rather than from comparing pixel areas.}

A compact proof explains the scalar reduction. Put $w=A^{-1}u$ and factor $A+uv^T=A(I+wv^T)$. If $w\ne0$, choose a <basis> starting with $w$. In that <basis>, $(I+wv^T)w=(1+v^Tw)w$, while every other basis <vector> changes only by a multiple of $w$. Its <matrix> is triangular with diagonal $1+v^Tw,1,\ldots,1$, giving the required <determinant>. If $w=0$, the correction vanishes. This uses <multiplicativity of the determinant> to restore the factor $\det A$.

The denominator in the <Sherman–Morrison formula> is exactly this factor. The <rank-one update singularity criterion> therefore identifies when the updated <matrix inverse> ceases to exist. Batson, Spielman and Srivastava use both identities for the symmetric update $A+vv^T$ in https://people.eecs.berkeley.edu/~brecht/cs294docs/week4/09.Batson.sparsification.pdf[Twice-Ramanujan Sparsifiers, section 2.3, Lemmas 2.1–2.2].

= Permanent
{disambiguate=mathematics}
{title2=$\operatorname{perm}A$}
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

= Permanent of a matrix
{synonym}

The <permanent of a matrix> adds the products obtained by choosing one entry in each row and one in each column. For an $n\times n$ <matrix> $A=(a_{ij})$ with entries in the <real numbers> or <complex numbers>,
$$
\operatorname{perm}A=\sum_{\sigma\in S_n}\prod_{i=1}^n a_{i,\sigma(i)}.
$$
Here each <permutation> $\sigma$ chooses column $\sigma(i)$ in row $i$, and the <symmetric group> $S_n$ lists every such <bijection>. The formula resembles the <Leibniz formula for determinants>, but omits the <sign of a permutation>. For a two-by-two <matrix>,
$$
\operatorname{perm}\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}=ad+bc,
\qquad
\det\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\c&d\end{pmatrix}=ad-bc.
$$
The absence of alternating signs makes the <permanent of a matrix> particularly useful for counting allowed assignments.

For a zero-one <matrix>, let rows represent people, columns represent jobs, and $a_{ij}=1$ mean that person $i$ can do job $j$. This is the <biadjacency matrix> of a <bipartite graph>. A product in the permanent is one exactly when all the selected assignments are allowed; otherwise it is zero. Each surviving <permutation> assigns every person a different job, which is a <perfect matching>. Thus the permanent counts <perfect matchings>. This interpretation and the definition are given in https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-226-probabilistic-methods-in-combinatorics-fall-2022/mit18_226_f22_lec_full.pdf[Yufei Zhao, Probabilistic Methods in Combinatorics, §10.2, pp. 178–179].

For a fully enumerated example, take
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\1&1&1\\0&1&1\end{pmatrix}.
$$
