Among the six <permutations> of three columns, exactly three give nonzero products: $(1,2,3)$, $(1,3,2)$ and $(2,1,3)$. These tuples list the chosen column in each successive row. Each product is one, so $\operatorname{perm}A=3$. Their <permutation signs> are $+1,-1,-1$, giving $\det A=-1$. The diagram displays all three allowed assignments.

\Image[permanent-perfect-matching-assignments.png]
{title=Three allowed one-to-one assignments contribute positively to the permanent, while their permutation signs give a determinant of minus one}

A zero <determinant> need not mean that no assignment exists. The two-by-two all-ones <matrix> has permanent $2$ and <determinant> $0$: its two assignments cancel in the determinant. The <nonzero determinant criterion for a perfect matching> is sufficient for existence, but the converse fails for a fixed zero-one <matrix>.

For nonnegative entries, the permanent instead sums assignment weights, with each assignment weighted by the product of its selected entries. It is then a total weight rather than necessarily an <integer> count. With arbitrary entries in the <real numbers> or <complex numbers>, even the permanent can have cancellation; the counting interpretation specifically uses zero-one entries. The <permanent as probability of distinct independent choices> gives another useful nonnegative interpretation.

There are $n!$ terms in the defining sum, linking direct evaluation to the <factorial>. For the all-ones $n\times n$ <matrix>, every term is one and the permanent is exactly $n!$; the <identity matrix> has permanent one. Finding one allowed assignment and counting all allowed assignments are different questions. Their relationship to <computational complexity theory> was studied in https://www.sciencedirect.com/science/article/pii/0304397579900446[L. G. Valiant, The complexity of computing the permanent, Theoretical Computer Science 8(2), 189–201, 1979]. The elementary sum is useful for small examples, while the existing <dense permanent approximation> describes a more specialized way of estimating large counts.

= Permanent as probability of distinct independent choices
{parent=Permanent (mathematics)}
{title2=$\mathbb P(X_1,\ldots,X_n\text{ all distinct})=\operatorname{perm}P$}

Let $X_1,\ldots,X_n$ be <independent random variables>, each choosing a label from $\{1,\ldots,n\}$. Put their <probabilities> in the <matrix> $P=(p_{ij})$, where $p_{ij}=\mathbb P(X_i=j)$. Each row sums to one. All choices are distinct precisely when their ordered list is one of the <permutations> of the $n$ labels. These are mutually exclusive <events>, and <independence> makes the <probability> of one list $\sigma$ equal to $\prod_i p_{i,\sigma(i)}$. Adding the list probabilities gives the <permanent of a matrix>:
$$
\mathbb P(X_1,\ldots,X_n\text{ all distinct})=\operatorname{perm}P.
$$
In particular, this permanent lies between zero and one.

For a two-person example,
$$
P=\begin{pmatrix}3/4&1/4\\1/5&4/5\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The distinct outcomes $(1,2)$ and $(2,1)$ have <probabilities> $3/5$ and $1/20$. Therefore
$$
\operatorname{perm}P=\frac35+\frac1{20}=\frac{13}{20}=0.65.
$$
The colliding outcomes $(1,1)$ and $(2,2)$ have <probabilities> $3/20$ and $1/5$, totaling $0.35$. All four outcomes are shown below; their <probabilities> sum to one.

\Image[permanent-independent-choice-probability.png]
{title=The permanent adds the probabilities of distinct choices: two successful outcomes have total probability 0.65}

If every choice has the <uniform distribution on a finite set> of $n$ labels, then $p_{ij}=1/n$ and the distinct-choice <probability> is $n!/n^n$. For three choices and three labels it is $6/27=2/9$. This counts a subset of all $n^n$ ordered outcomes, not independent pairwise collision events. <Independence> of the original choices is essential: without it, the individual rows of $P$ do not determine the joint <probabilities>.

= Dense permanent approximation
{parent=Permanent (mathematics)}
{title2=$\delta(G)>n/2\Longrightarrow\text{a randomized relative approximation scheme}$}

If every row and column of an $n$-by-$n$ zero-one <matrix> has more than $n/2$ ones, its <permanent of a matrix> has a <fully polynomial randomized approximation scheme>. The <short augmenting paths in a dense bipartite graph> bound makes the leading coefficient dominate the <matching generating function> at polynomially large activity. The <weighted matching Markov chain> samples polynomially bounded activities in polynomial <mixing time>. Estimate successive <annealing ratios for a matching generating function> and divide by the final activity to the power $n$.

= Matrix inverse
{title2=$A^{-1}$}
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

= Inverse matrix
{synonym}

For an $n$-by-$n$ <square matrix> $A$ over a <field>, its matrix inverse is an $n$-by-$n$ <matrix> $A^{-1}$ satisfying both identities
$$
AA^{-1}=A^{-1}A=I_n,
$$
where $I_n$ is the <identity matrix>. It exists exactly when $A$ is an <invertible matrix>, equivalently when its <matrix rank> is $n$ or its <determinant> is nonzero. It reverses the <linear map>: applying $A$ and then $A^{-1}$ recovers the input, while applying $A^{-1}$ and then $A$ recovers the output.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06-linear-algebra-spring-2010/resources/lecture-3-multiplication-and-inverse-matrices/[Strang, MIT 18.06 Lecture 3: the two inverse identities]{ref}

The <matrix inverse> is unique. If $B$ and $C$ both satisfy the identities, then $B=B(AC)=(BA)C=C$. Although the definition is two-sided, for finite <square matrices> either identity alone suffices: $BA=I$ forces a <trivial kernel>, whereas $AB=I$ forces <surjectivity>; either makes $A$ an <invertible matrix> by the <rank-nullity theorem>. Rectangular <matrices> may have only one of these properties.

For a concrete example, take
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\det A=2\cdot1-1\cdot1=1,\qquad
A^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}1&-1\\-1&2\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Direct <matrix multiplication> gives
$$
AA^{-1}=\begin{pmatrix}2-1&-2+2\\1-1&-1+2\end{pmatrix}=I_2,
\qquad
A^{-1}A=\begin{pmatrix}2-1&1-1\\-2+2&-1+2\end{pmatrix}=I_2.
$$
Consequently the <vector> $v=(1,2)^T$ follows a reversible journey:
$$
\begin{pmatrix}1\\2\end{pmatrix}
\xrightarrow{\ A\ }\begin{pmatrix}4\\3\end{pmatrix}
\xrightarrow{\ A^{-1}\ }\begin{pmatrix}4-3\\-4+6\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1\\2\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The columns $(2,1)^T$ and $(1,1)^T$ are the images of the <standard basis> vectors. They turn the unit square into a <parallelogram>; the <matrix inverse> restores both the square and its surrounding grid. Since $\det A=1$, this example changes shape while preserving <area>, illustrating that invertibility does not require preserving lengths or angles.

\Image[matrix-inverse-undoing-a-map.png]
{title=A <matrix inverse> restores a grid and a <vector>; a <singular matrix> merges distinct inputs}

For comparison, the <singular matrix>
$$
S=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix},\qquad
S\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}
=S\begin{pmatrix}0\\1\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}1\\0\end{pmatrix}
$$
cannot have a <matrix inverse>: the output $(1,0)^T$ cannot specify which of the two inputs occurred. Their difference $(1,-1)^T$ belongs to its <kernel>. Indeed $S(x,y)^T=(x+y,0)^T$ collapses the <plane> to a <line>, its <matrix rank> is one, and its <determinant> is zero. No subsequent <function> of the output alone can reconstruct every input.

An important use of the <matrix inverse> is describing the unique solution of a <linear system>:
$$
Ax=b\iff x=A^{-1}b.
$$
For $b=(4,3)^T$, the preceding computation solves $2x_1+x_2=4$ and $x_1+x_2=3$, giving $x=(1,2)^T$. In computation, usually solve the <linear system> using <Gaussian elimination> and a factorization such as an <LU decomposition>, rather than forming every entry of the <matrix inverse>. The inverse formula specifies the answer; a factorization provides a reusable way to compute it for each right-hand side.https://www.mit.edu/~18.06/Spring17/final-sol.pdf[MIT 18.06, Spring 2017 final solutions, problem 5(b): solve with triangular factors rather than computing inverses]{ref}

Here the <LU decomposition> is especially simple:
$$
A=LU,\qquad
L=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\\tfrac12&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
U=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\0&\tfrac12\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Solve $Ly=b$ by <forward substitution in a triangular system>: $y_1=4$ and $y_2=3-\tfrac12\cdot4=1$. Then solve $Ux=y$ by <backward substitution in a triangular system>: $x_2=2$ and $x_1=(4-2)/2=1$. The same factors work when $b$ changes. If the whole <matrix inverse> is actually needed, its $j$th column is the solution of $Ax=e_j$, where $e_j$ is the $j$th <standard basis> vector.

Reversing several operations means undoing the last one first. For <invertible matrices> of the same size,
$$
(AB)^{-1}=B^{-1}A^{-1},
$$
because $(AB)(B^{-1}A^{-1})=A(BB^{-1})A^{-1}=I$, and the product in the other order is also $I$. This is why a <change-of-basis matrix> uses its <matrix inverse> to reverse the direction of coordinate conversion.

= Block matrix inverse
{parent=Matrix inverse}

The inverse of a block matrix can be expressed through the inverse of one diagonal block and the inverse of its <Schur complement>. This reduces many deletion and conditioning identities to smaller matrix inversions.

= Sherman–Morrison formula
{c}
{parent=Matrix inverse}
{wiki}
{title2=$(A+uv^T)^{-1}=A^{-1}-A^{-1}uv^TA^{-1}/(1+v^TA^{-1}u)$}

The <Sherman–Morrison formula> computes the <matrix inverse> after a <rank-one matrix update>. If $A$ is an <invertible matrix> and $\delta=1+v^TA^{-1}u\ne0$, then
$$
(A+uv^T)^{-1}
=A^{-1}-\frac{A^{-1}uv^TA^{-1}}{1+v^TA^{-1}u}.
$$
The inverse correction is itself an <outer product>, divided by the <scalar> $\delta$. The <matrix determinant lemma> shows that the nonzero-denominator condition is exactly the condition that the updated <matrix> remains an <invertible matrix>.

For $A=I$, $u=(1,2)^T$, and $v=(3,-1)^T$, we have $\delta=2$. Thus the computed inverse is
$$
\boxed{
\begin{pmatrix}4&-1\\6&-1\end{pmatrix}^{-1}
=I-\frac12\begin{pmatrix}3&-1\\6&-2\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}-1/2&1/2\\-3&2\end{pmatrix}
}.
$$
Multiplication in either order gives the <identity matrix>. The result reuses the original inverse $I$ and the same correction <vectors>, rather than solving independently for all four inverse entries.

Often only the solution of an updated <linear system> is needed. If $Ax_0=b$ and $Az=u$, then
$$
(A+uv^T)x=b
\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
x=x_0-z\,\frac{v^Tx_0}{1+v^Tz},
$$
provided $1+v^Tz\ne0$. The two solves use the same <LU decomposition> of $A$. This expresses the computational benefit without requiring an explicitly stored <matrix inverse>. Strang's https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-065-matrix-methods-in-data-analysis-signal-processing-and-machine-learning-spring-2018/988b4c05680f40a5a4b3be7004a32500_XhSk_Lw2X_U.pdf[Lecture 14, transcript pp. 9–10] explains this reuse of the original factorization.

To see why the inverse correction works, first consider $A=I$ and put $s=v^Tu$. The <rank-one matrix> $R=uv^T$ satisfies $R^2=sR$. Expanding
$$
(I+R)\left(I-\frac{R}{1+s}\right)
=I+R-\frac{R}{1+s}-\frac{sR}{1+s}=I
$$
also works in the reverse order. The general case follows by factoring out $A$ and replacing $u$ by $A^{-1}u$. A matrix identity has reduced to cancellation of scalar coefficients.

Historically, Jack Sherman and Winifred J. Morrison studied updating an inverse when a single matrix entry changes in their https://doi.org/10.1214/aoms/1177729893[1950 paper, Adjustment of an Inverse Matrix Corresponding to a Change in One Element of a Given Matrix]{ref}. Such an entry change is the special <rank-one matrix update> $\alpha e_ie_j^T$, with <standard basis> <vectors> $e_i,e_j$. The reference and the earlier 1949 abstract on row or column changes are recorded in https://epubs.siam.org/doi/10.1137/1023004[Henderson and Searle, On Deriving the Inverse of a Sum of Matrices (1981), references 40–41]. This distinguishes the specific original setting from the more general $uv^T$ formula used here.

= Rank-one update singularity criterion
{parent=Sherman–Morrison formula}
{title2=$A+uv^T\text{ singular}\iff1+v^TA^{-1}u=0$}

For an <invertible matrix> $A$, the <rank-one matrix update> $B=A+uv^T$ is a <singular matrix> exactly when $1+v^TA^{-1}u=0$. This follows directly from the <matrix determinant lemma>. At that value, $u\ne0$ and the nonzero <vector> $w=A^{-1}u$ satisfies
$$
Bw=u+u(v^TA^{-1}u)=0.
$$
Every <vector> in the <kernel> is a multiple of $w$, since $Bx=0$ implies $x=-A^{-1}u(v^Tx)$. The <matrix rank> therefore drops by exactly one.

For $B(t)=I+tuv^T$ with $u=(1,2)^T$ and $v=(3,-1)^T$, the update becomes a <singular matrix> at $t=-1$, and $B(t)u=(1+t)u$. At $t=-0.99$, this direction is compressed by a factor $0.01$ and the inverse amplifies it by a factor 100. The actual <spectral norm> of the <matrix inverse>, which maximizes amplification over every direction, is about $700.17853409$.

\Image[sherman-morrison-near-singularity.png]
{title=An inverse grows large as the rank-one update approaches its singular denominator}
{description=Here $\delta=1+t>0$ and $B(t)=I+tuv^T$ uses the same example <vectors>. The left panel shows $\|B(t)^{-1}\|_2$ and its lower bound $1/\delta$, obtained from the direction $u$. The right panel shows the <condition number> $\kappa_2(B)=\|B\|_2\|B^{-1}\|_2$. Both are computed from <singular values>; the missing point $\delta=0$ has no <matrix inverse>.}

The exact formula can expose a large <condition number> in the updated <linear system> even when the original one had a small <condition number>. It does not guarantee <numerical stability> merely because its evaluation uses fewer arithmetic operations. The denominator condition certifies existence; the <condition number> and floating-point behavior address different questions about using the resulting answer.

= Moore-Penrose inverse
{title2=$A^+$}
{c}
{parent=Matrix inverse}
{wiki}

= Moore-Penrose pseudoinverse
{c}
{synonym}

= Pseudoinverse
{synonym}

The <Moore-Penrose inverse> extends the useful part of a <matrix inverse> to rectangular and singular <matrices>. A <linear system> $Ax=b$ may have no exact solution, or may have many. The <pseudoinverse> resolves both ambiguities: $x_*=A^+b$ is the <minimum-norm least-squares solution>. First minimize the <Euclidean norm> $\|Ax-b\|_2$; among all <vectors> attaining that minimum, choose the one with smallest <Euclidean norm> $\|x\|_2$. It does not pretend that inconsistent equations can all be satisfied.

For $A\in\mathbb C^{m\times n}$, its <Moore-Penrose inverse> is the unique $n\times m$ <matrix> satisfying the four <Penrose equations>. The <singular value decomposition> gives a direct construction:
$$
A=U\Sigma V^*,
\qquad
\boxed{A^+=V\Sigma^+U^*.}
$$
Here $U,V$ are <unitary matrices>, the star is the <conjugate transpose>, and $\Sigma^+$ is the $n\times m$ rectangular <diagonal matrix> obtained by replacing each nonzero <singular value> $\sigma_j$ by $1/\sigma_j$ and leaving zeros at zero. Over the <real numbers>, use <orthogonal matrices> and <transposes>. The <pseudoinverse> reverses the stretches that can be reversed; it discards output components the original <linear map> cannot produce and does not invent an input component in its <kernel>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06sc-linear-algebra-fall-2011/resources/mit18_06scf11_ses3-8sum/[Strang, MIT 18.06SC: Left and Right Inverses; Pseudoinverse]{ref}

The two products have especially clear meanings:
$$
AA^+=P_{\operatorname{ran}A},
\qquad
A^+A=P_{(\ker A)^\perp}.
$$
They are <orthogonal projection matrices>: $AA^+$ selects the producible part of an output, while $A^+A$ selects the detectable part of an input. They are generally not <identity matrices>. For an <invertible matrix>, both are identities and $A^+=A^{-1}$.

This gives a useful order of operations for <least-squares solutions>: project the requested output onto the <column space>, then invert the restriction of $A$ from $(\ker A)^\perp$ to its <column space>. Adding a <kernel> <vector> leaves the residual unchanged but increases the squared <Euclidean norm> by the <Pythagorean theorem in an inner-product space>, because $A^+b$ is <orthogonal> to that <kernel>. The <pseudoinverse> therefore provides one canonical answer without hiding either lost information or irreconcilable data.

The name records a history with several contributors. E. H. Moore gave an earlier generalization in 1920; M. H. Pearl's 1966 paper records that work, Arne Bjerhammar's 1951 rediscovery for maximal-rank rectangular <matrices>, and Roger Penrose's independent 1955 treatment. https://www.cambridge.org/core/journals/mathematical-proceedings-of-the-cambridge-philosophical-society/article/abs/on-generalized-inverses-of-matrices/9CD637F604C76ECD8D03AC029212D56E[Pearl, On generalized inverses of matrices (1966), introduction and references 6–9]{ref} Penrose's original paper proves existence and uniqueness from the four <Penrose equations> for arbitrary complex <matrices>, including rectangular ones. https://faculty.kfupm.edu.sa/math/jaafarm/lec-notes/Moore-Pinrose.pdf[Penrose, A generalized inverse for matrices (1955), Theorem 1, pp. 406–408]{ref}

= Overdetermined least-squares example
{parent=Moore-Penrose inverse}
{title2=$x_*=(2,3)^T$}

Suppose three measurements constrain two unknowns:
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
b=\begin{pmatrix}1\\2\\6\end{pmatrix}.
$$
The <linear system> asks for $x_1=1$, $x_2=2$, and $x_1+x_2=6$, which are incompatible. A <least-squares solution> makes a balanced compromise. The <matrix> has <full column rank>, so
$$
A^+=(A^TA)^{-1}A^T
=\frac13\begin{pmatrix}2&-1&1\\-1&2&1\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
\boxed{x_*=A^+b=(2,3)^T.}
$$
The fitted output and residual are
$$
Ax_*=(2,3,5)^T,
\qquad
r=b-Ax_*=(-1,-1,1)^T,
\qquad
A^Tr=0.
$$
Thus the two direct measurements are each overshot by one, and the measured sum is undershot by one. The residual has <Euclidean norm> $\sqrt3$ and is <orthogonal> to the <column space>, the <plane> $z=y_1+y_2$ in output coordinates $(y_1,y_2,z)$.

\Image[pseudoinverse-least-squares-projection.png]
{title=The pseudoinverse projects inconsistent data onto the attainable output plane}
{description=For $b=(1,2,6)^T$, the closest attainable output is $Ax_*=(2,3,5)^T$. The segment between them is the residual $(-1,-1,1)^T$, perpendicular to the <column space>. The drawing is an orthonormal two-dimensional slice of output space; the attainable <plane> intersects this slice in the blue <straight line>.}

For any perturbation $h\in\mathbb R^2$, the <orthogonality> of $r$ to $Ah$ gives
$$
\|b-A(x_*+h)\|_2^2=\|r\|_2^2+\|Ah\|_2^2
=3+\|Ah\|_2^2.
$$
Since the <kernel> of $A$ is trivial, every nonzero $h$ increases the residual. The <least-squares solution> is unique in this example. The formula using $A^TA$ is convenient for these small exact calculations; an actual numerical computation can use a <QR decomposition> or <singular value decomposition> to avoid the extra conditioning cost of the <least-squares normal equations>. For <full column rank>, the squared <singular values> of $A$ are the <eigenvalues> of $A^TA$, so its <condition number> in the <Euclidean norm> is $\kappa_2(A^TA)=\kappa_2(A)^2$. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-065-matrix-methods-in-data-analysis-signal-processing-and-machine-learning-spring-2018/resources/lecture-9-four-ways-to-solve-least-squares-problems/[Strang, MIT 18.065 Lecture 9: four ways to solve least-squares problems]{ref}

= Underdetermined minimum-norm example
{parent=Moore-Penrose inverse}
{title2=$x_*=(3/5,6/5)^T$}

Now take one equation for two unknowns:
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\end{pmatrix},
\qquad b=3.
$$
The <linear system> $x_1+2x_2=3$ has an entire <affine line> of exact solutions. Since $A$ has <full row rank>, its <Moore-Penrose inverse> is
$$
A^+=A^T(AA^T)^{-1}
=\frac15\begin{pmatrix}1\\2\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
\boxed{x_*=A^+b=(3/5,6/5)^T.}
$$
The <kernel> is $\operatorname{span}\{(-2,1)^T\}$, and all exact solutions are
$$
x=x_*+t(-2,1)^T,\qquad t\in\mathbb R.
$$
Because $x_*$ is <orthogonal> to $(-2,1)^T$,
$$
\|x\|_2^2=\frac95+5t^2.
$$
The <Euclidean norm> is therefore smallest exactly at $t=0$. The zero residual alone cannot select a solution; the minimum-norm rule supplies the additional criterion.

\Image[pseudoinverse-minimum-norm-line.png]
{title=The minimum-norm solution is the closest point of the solution line to the origin}
{description=The <affine line> $x_1+2x_2=3$ is perpendicular to $x_*=(3/5,6/5)^T$. Moving along the line changes only a <kernel> component and increases $\|x\|_2^2$ by $5t^2$.}

This selection depends on the specified <inner product>. Rescaling one unknown changes what counts as a small <Euclidean norm>, so units and weighting matter in an application. A minimum-norm answer is mathematically canonical for the chosen geometry, rather than an automatic guarantee of physical plausibility.

= Small singular values in the Moore-Penrose inverse
{parent=Moore-Penrose inverse}
{title2=$\|A^+\|_2=1/\sigma_{\min,+}$}

For a nonzero <matrix> $A$, the <operator norm> induced by the <Euclidean norm> satisfies $\|A^+\|_2=1/\sigma_{\min,+}$, where $\sigma_{\min,+}$ is its smallest positive <singular value>. The <singular value decomposition> makes the danger visible: a small nonzero stretch becomes a large amplification of errors when inverted.

For instance, when $\varepsilon>0$,
$$
A_\varepsilon=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&\varepsilon\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
A_\varepsilon^+=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1/\varepsilon\end{pmatrix}.
$$
A data perturbation $(0,\eta)^T$ changes the reconstructed input by $(0,\eta/\varepsilon)^T$. At $\varepsilon=0$, however, the <pseudoinverse> is $\operatorname{diag}(1,0)$: exactly zero and merely small are different cases. Thus the <pseudoinverse> need not be <continuous> across a change of <matrix rank>. With uncertain data, <truncated singular value decomposition> or <Tikhonov regularization> can trade exact inversion of weak directions for stability; the cutoff or penalty adds a modelling choice.

= Moore--Penrose inverse of a Hilbert-space operator
{c}
{parent=Moore-Penrose inverse}

For a bounded operator $A:X\to Y$ between <Hilbert spaces>, $A^\dagger$ has domain $\mathcal R(A)\oplus\mathcal R(A)^\perp$, maps into $\mathcal N(A)^\perp$, and inverts the restriction of $A$ to $\mathcal N(A)^\perp$. It is bounded exactly when $\mathcal R(A)$ is closed.

= Penrose equations
{c}
{parent=Moore-Penrose inverse}

The Moore--Penrose inverse is characterized by
$$
AA^\dagger A=A,
\quad
A^\dagger AA^\dagger=A^\dagger,
\quad
(AA^\dagger)^*=AA^\dagger,
\quad
(A^\dagger A)^*=A^\dagger A.
$$

Here $A^\dagger=A^+$ denotes the <Moore-Penrose inverse>, while the star denotes the <conjugate transpose>. The first two identities express consistency when applying and reversing the <linear map>; the last two make the resulting <orthogonal projection matrices> <Hermitian>. They must hold together. For example, $A=(1\ \ 2)$ and $B=(1\ \ 0)^T$ satisfy $ABA=A$ and $BAB=B$, but
$$
BA=\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\0&0\end{pmatrix}
$$
is not <Hermitian>. This $B$ is a <right inverse>, yet $Bb=(3,0)^T$ is not the <minimum-norm least-squares solution> for $b=3$. The last two <Penrose equations> impose the <orthogonality> that selects the <pseudoinverse>.

= Reverse-order law for the Moore--Penrose inverse
{c}
{parent=Moore-Penrose inverse}

The identity $(AB)^\dagger=B^\dagger A^\dagger$ does not hold for arbitrary matrices. A sufficient condition is that $A$ have <full column rank> and $B$ have <full row rank>.

= Push-through identity
{parent=Matrix inverse}

For a rectangular matrix $X$ and $\lambda>0$,
$$
X^T(XX^T+\lambda I)^{-1}=(X^TX+\lambda I)^{-1}X^T.
$$
Multiplying either side by $XX^T+\lambda I$ verifies the identity.

= Matrix similarity
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

= Similar matrix
{synonym}

= Similar matrices
{synonym}

= Similarity
{synonym}

Square matrices $A$ and $B$ are similar when $B=P^{-1}AP$ for some invertible matrix $P$. They represent the same linear operator in different bases and therefore have the same <Jordan normal form>.

= Similarity transformation
{parent=Matrix similarity}

A similarity transformation sends a square matrix $A$ to $SAS^{-1}$ for an invertible matrix $S$. It changes the basis of a linear operator and preserves its characteristic polynomial and eigenvalues.

= Positive diagonal symmetrization of a matrix
{parent=Similarity transformation}

If a real <matrix> $B$ has a positive <diagonal matrix> $W$ such that $WB$ is a <symmetric matrix>, then $H=W^{1/2}BW^{-1/2}$ is a <symmetric matrix> related to $B$ by a <similarity transformation>: $B^TW=WB$ gives $H^T=H$. Thus $B$ is a <diagonalizable matrix> with real <eigenvalues>. If $\mathbf z^TWB\mathbf z\le0$ for all real <vectors> $\mathbf z$, its <eigenvalues> are nonpositive. This converts symmetry in a weighted <inner product> into Euclidean symmetry.

= Every automorphism of a full matrix algebra is inner
{parent=Similarity transformation}

Every unital algebra automorphism $F:M_n(\mathbb C)\to M_n(\mathbb C)$ has the form $F(X)=SXS^{-1}$ for some invertible $S$. One proof transports the matrix units through $F$, chooses compatible bases for their rank-one images, and reconstructs the common similarity transformation.

= Transpose similarity
{parent=Matrix similarity}
{wiki}

Every square matrix over a field is similar to its transpose.

= Real similarity from complex similarity
{parent=Matrix similarity}

If real matrices satisfy $C=SDS^{-1}$ for an invertible complex $S=X+iY$, then $CS=SD$, so both real matrices $X,Y$ intertwine $C$ and $D$. Since $\det(X+tY)$ is a nonzero real polynomial, some real $t$ makes $X+tY$ invertible and supplies a real similarity.

= Matrix trace
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki=Trace (linear algebra)}
{title2=$\operatorname{tr}A=\sum_{i=1}^n a_{ii}$}

= Trace
{synonym}

For an $n$-by-$n$ <square matrix> $A=(a_{ij})$ over a <field>, its matrix trace is the sum of its diagonal entries:
$$
\operatorname{tr}A=\sum_{i=1}^n a_{ii}.
$$
For example, $A=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\1&1\end{pmatrix}$ has <trace> $2+1=3$. Adding corresponding diagonal entries immediately proves <linearity>:
$$
\operatorname{tr}(\alpha A+\beta B)=\alpha\operatorname{tr}A+\beta\operatorname{tr}B.
$$
Thus <matrix trace> is a <linear functional> on the <vector space> of $n$-by-$n$ <matrices>.<Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[Axler, Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition, §8D, definition 8.47 and result 8.56]{ref}

The surprising point is that the sum is intrinsic to a <linear operator>, even though individual diagonal entries depend on the <basis>. The <cyclic property of the trace> gives, for any <invertible matrix> $P$,
$$
\operatorname{tr}(P^{-1}AP)=\operatorname{tr}(APP^{-1})=\operatorname{tr}A.
$$
Hence <matrix similarity> preserves <trace>. For the example above, choose the <change-of-basis matrix> $P=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\0&1\end{pmatrix}$. Then
$$
P^{-1}AP=\begin{pmatrix}1&1\\1&2\end{pmatrix}:
\qquad 1+2=2+1=3.
$$
The diagonal entries have changed but their sum has not. This makes <trace> useful for extracting a feature of the underlying <linear operator> without finding a preferred <basis>.

Over the <complex numbers>, or over the <real numbers> after allowing complex <eigenvalues>, the <trace> equals their sum with <algebraic multiplicity>:
$$
\operatorname{tr}A=\lambda_1+\cdots+\lambda_n.
$$
No <diagonalizable matrix> hypothesis is needed.<Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[Axler, Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition, §8D, results 8.50–8.52]{ref} To see why, use <triangularization over an algebraically closed field>: an <upper triangular matrix> similar to $A$ has the <eigenvalues> on its diagonal, counted with <algebraic multiplicity>, because its <characteristic polynomial> is $\prod_i(z-\lambda_i)$. <Similarity> preserves <trace>, so summing that diagonal proves the formula.

For instance,
$$
J=\begin{pmatrix}2&1\\0&2\end{pmatrix},\qquad
\det(zI-J)=(z-2)^2,\qquad\operatorname{tr}J=2+2=4.
$$
There is only one independent <eigenvector>, since $(J-2I)(x,y)^T=(y,0)^T$, so $J$ is not a <diagonalizable matrix>. Nevertheless its repeated <eigenvalue> contributes twice. For our earlier $A$, the <characteristic polynomial> is $z^2-3z+1$, its <eigenvalues> are $(3+\sqrt5)/2$ and $(3-\sqrt5)/2$, and their sum is $3$. An inexpensive diagonal sum therefore gives spectral information before solving the <characteristic polynomial>.

The <trace> of a real <matrix> does not give that <linear map>'s <volume> factor: that factor is the <absolute value> of its <determinant>. For example, $2I_2$ has <trace> $4$ and <area> factor $4$, but $2I_3$ has <trace> $6$ and <volume> factor $8$. Their occasional numerical agreement in two dimensions is a coincidence. The connection between <trace and local volume change> gives a geometric interpretation near the <identity matrix>, and describes the <volume> rate of a <flow map> generated by a <linear system of differential equations>.

= Trace and local volume change
{parent=Matrix trace}
{title2=$\det(I+tA)=1+t\operatorname{tr}A+O(t^2)$}

For any fixed real $n$-by-$n$ <matrix> $A$, as the real parameter $t$ tends to zero,
$$
\det(I+tA)=1+t\operatorname{tr}A+O(t^2).
$$
The <big O notation> means the remainder is bounded in <absolute value> by $Ct^2$ for some constant $C$ near zero. The <determinant> stays positive for sufficiently small $t$, so its <oriented volume> factor is also its ordinary <volume> factor. Thus the <trace> is the first-order fractional <volume> change, measured from the <identity matrix>.

A proof follows directly from <multilinearity of the determinant>. Each column of $I+tA$ is $e_j+tAe_j$. The constant term chooses all the <standard basis> columns and equals one. A term containing exactly one factor $t$ replaces just column $j$ by $Ae_j$; expanding that <vector> in the <standard basis> leaves only its $e_j$ component, $a_{jj}$, because every other choice repeats a column. Summing gives $\operatorname{tr}A$. Terms with two or more replacements have order $t^2$ or higher. This also proves the <derivative of the determinant> at the <identity matrix>.

This coefficient has a direct visual meaning. At the point $e_j$, the <linear map> $x\mapsto Ax$ has component $a_{jj}$ along direction $e_j$. In a small step $x\mapsto x+tAx$, these are the first-order changes of the square's or box's sides in their own directions. Summing them gives its fractional <area> or <volume> change; the off-diagonal entries initially change shape rather than contributing to this first-order total.

For the constant-coefficient <linear system of differential equations> $x'=Ax$, the exact <linear flow> is $x(t)=e^{tA}x(0)$, using the <matrix exponential>. Its <volume> factor is
$$
V(t)=\det(e^{tA})=e^{t\operatorname{tr}A},\qquad V(0)=1,
\qquad V'(t)=(\operatorname{tr}A)V(t).
$$
This is the <matrix exponential determinant identity>. One can derive it without <diagonalization>: set $E(t)=e^{tA}$, whose <matrix inverse> is $E(-t)$, and use $E'=AE$ in <Jacobi's formula> to get $V'=\det E\operatorname{tr}(E^{-1}AE)=(\operatorname{tr}A)V$. The scalar <differential equation> with initial value one gives the displayed <exponential function>. Thus positive <trace> causes <volume> expansion, negative <trace> causes contraction, and zero <trace> preserves <volume>, even when individual directions behave differently.

In two dimensions, compare four generators at $t=0.4$:
$$
\begin{array}{c|c|c|c}
A&\operatorname{tr}A&e^{0.4A}&\text{area factor}\\\hline
\operatorname{diag}(1,1)&2&\operatorname{diag}(e^{0.4},e^{0.4})&e^{0.8}\approx2.2255\\
\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)&0&\operatorname{diag}(e^{0.4},e^{-0.4})&1\\
\operatorname{diag}(-1,-1)&-2&\operatorname{diag}(e^{-0.4},e^{-0.4})&e^{-0.8}\approx0.4493\\
\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}&0&\begin{pmatrix}1&0.4\\0&1\end{pmatrix}&1
\end{array}
$$
The first and third <diagonal matrices> expand or contract both directions. The second expands horizontally and contracts vertically by reciprocal factors, making a rectangle of unchanged <area>. For the last <nilpotent matrix>, $A^2=0$, so its <matrix exponential> is exactly $I+tA$; it sends $(x,y)$ to $(x+ty,y)$, a <shear mapping>. Zero <trace> allows substantial shape change and does not mean the <flow map> is stationary.

\Image[matrix-trace-local-volume.png]
{title=Four examples of a <linear system of differential equations>: <trace> controls <area> change while a saddle and a <shear mapping> preserve <area>}

The first-order statement and the exact <flow map> should be distinguished. For $A=\operatorname{diag}(1,-1)$, $\det(I+tA)=1-t^2$, whereas $\det(e^{tA})=1$ for all real $t$. Zero <trace> eliminates the first-order change in the approximate step, and it preserves <volume> exactly in the <matrix exponential> flow. The examples use an abstract parameter $t$ and coordinate <vectors>; no physical units are assumed.

= Scalar square of a traceless two-by-two matrix
{parent=Matrix trace}
{title2=$M^2=-(\det M)I$}

A two-by-two <matrix> $M$ with zero <trace> satisfies $M^2=-(\det M)I$ by the <Cayley-Hamilton theorem>. Over the <complex numbers>, either $\det M\ne0$ and its two distinct <eigenvalues> make it diagonalizable, or $M^2=0$. A matrix commutator has zero trace, so this alternative applies to it. Diagonalization over the real numbers need not follow when $-\det M<0$.

= Nilpotence from vanishing traces of powers
{parent=Matrix trace}
{title2=$\operatorname{tr}(A^k)=0\ (1\le k\le n)\ \Longrightarrow\ A^n=0$}

For a complex $n$ by $n$ <matrix>, triangularization gives $\operatorname{tr}(A^k)=\sum_j\lambda_j^k$. The <zero power sums force a finite complex multiset to vanish> criterion makes every diagonal eigenvalue zero. A strictly upper-triangular $n$ by $n$ matrix has zero $n$th power: every nonzero product entry would require $n$ strictly increasing index steps. <Similarity> then gives $A^n=0$.

= Trace orthogonality nilpotence lemma
{parent=Matrix trace}
{title2=$\operatorname{tr}(\alpha M)=0\ \Longrightarrow\ \alpha\text{ nilpotent}$}

Let $U\subseteq W\subseteq\operatorname{End}_{\mathbb C}(V)$ be <vector subspaces> and $M=\{T:[T,W]\subseteq U\}$. If $\alpha\in M$ and $\operatorname{tr}(\alpha\beta)=0$ for all $\beta\in M$, then $\alpha$ is a <nilpotent endomorphism>.

To prove it, let $\beta$ act by $\overline\lambda$ on the <generalized eigenspace> of $\alpha$ of <eigenvalue> $\lambda$. <Polynomial interpolation> on eigenvalue differences, together with <adjoint compatibility of additive Jordan decomposition>, expresses $\operatorname{ad}\beta$ as a <polynomial> in $\operatorname{ad}\alpha$ with zero constant term. Since $\operatorname{ad}\alpha$ maps $W$ into $U$ and preserves $U$, this gives $\beta\in M$. Taking the <matrix trace> on each <generalized eigenspace> yields $0=\sum_\lambda\dim(V_\lambda)|\lambda|^2$, so every <eigenvalue> is zero. This is the linear-algebra step behind the <Cartan solvability criterion>; $U,W$ need not be Lie subalgebras.

= Trace is the unique normalized cyclic linear functional
{parent=Matrix trace}
{title2=$f(AB)=f(BA),\ f(I)=n\ \Longrightarrow\ f=\operatorname{Tr}$}

Over $\mathbb R$ or $\mathbb C$, every <linear functional> on all $n\times n$ <matrices> satisfying $f(AB)=f(BA)$ is a scalar multiple of the <trace>. The <matrix units> show that the functional vanishes off the diagonal and has equal values on every diagonal unit. The normalization $f(I)=n$ fixes the scalar to one.

= Traceless matrix
{parent=Matrix trace}
{title2=$\operatorname{Tr}A=0$}

A traceless matrix is a square <matrix> whose <trace> is zero. Such <matrices> form the <kernel> of the trace <linear functional>.

= Traceless matrices are spanned by commutators
{parent=Traceless matrix}
{title2=$\ker\operatorname{Tr}=\operatorname{span}\{AB-BA\}$}

Every <commutator> of square <matrices> is traceless by cyclicity of the <trace>. Conversely, off-diagonal <matrix units> and differences of diagonal units are themselves <commutators> and span all <traceless matrices>. This proves equality with the linear span of commutators, without needing a theorem about representation as one commutator.

= Cyclic property of the trace
{parent=Matrix trace}
{title2=$\operatorname{tr}(AB)=\operatorname{tr}(BA)$}

If $A$ is an $m$-by-$n$ <matrix> and $B$ is an $n$-by-$m$ <matrix> over the same <field>, both products are <square matrices> and
$$
\operatorname{tr}(AB)=\sum_{i=1}^m\sum_{j=1}^n a_{ij}b_{ji}
=\sum_{j=1}^n\sum_{i=1}^m b_{ji}a_{ij}=\operatorname{tr}(BA).
$$
The products can even have different sizes: for $A=(1\ \ 2)$ and $B=(3\ \ 4)^T$, $AB=(11)$ and $BA=\begin{pmatrix}3&6\\4&8\end{pmatrix}$, but both have <trace> $11$.<Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[Axler, Linear Algebra Done Right, fourth edition, §8D, result 8.49]{ref}

Grouping all but one factor proves that $\operatorname{tr}(A_1A_2\cdots A_k)$ is unchanged by a cyclic permutation whenever the cyclic products are defined and square. In particular, every <commutator> has <trace> zero, since $\operatorname{tr}(AB-BA)=0$ by <linearity>.

Cyclic rotation does not allow arbitrary reordering. For
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
B=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix},\quad
C=\begin{pmatrix}0&0\\1&0\end{pmatrix},
$$
one has $ABC=A$, so $\operatorname{tr}(ABC)=1$, but $AC=0$, so $\operatorname{tr}(ACB)=0$. Rotating $ABC$ to $BCA$ or $CAB$ preserves its <trace>; swapping just $B$ and $C$ need not.

\Include[linear-operator-theory]{parent=Linear algebra}

= Multilinear algebra
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

Multilinear algebra studies maps linear in each of several arguments, including determinants, tensors, and exterior products.

= Coalgebra
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{title2=$(C,\Delta,\varepsilon)$}
{wiki}

A <comonoid> in the <monoidal category> of <modules> over a <commutative ring>, with tensor $\otimes_k$ and unit $k$.

= Coideal
{parent=Coalgebra}
{title2=$\Delta I\subseteq I\otimes C+C\otimes I,\quad\varepsilon(I)=0$}

A <linear subspace> $I$ of a <coalgebra> satisfying the displayed conditions. These are exactly the conditions for the <comultiplication> and <counit> to descend to the quotient <vector space> $C/I$. An ideal that is also a coideal in a <bialgebra> gives a quotient <bialgebra>.

= Measuring coalgebra
{parent=Coalgebra}
{title2=$p(c)(ab)=\sum p(c_{(1)})(a)p(c_{(2)})(b)$}

For unital <algebras over a field> $A,B$, a <coalgebra> $C$ measures $A$ into $B$ when a linear map $p:C\to\operatorname{Hom}_k(A,B)$ satisfies the displayed identity and $p(c)(1_A)=\varepsilon(c)1_B$. <Group-like elements> therefore act by unital <algebra homomorphisms over a field>.

= Universal measuring coalgebra
{parent=Measuring coalgebra}
{title2=$P(A,B)$}

A terminal <measuring coalgebra>: every measuring map from $C$ factors uniquely through its universal measuring map by a <coalgebra> homomorphism $C\to P(A,B)$. Composition of universal measurings makes $P(A,A)$ a <bialgebra>, with the identity map providing its unit.

= Group-like element
{parent=Coalgebra}
{title2=$\Delta g=g\otimes g,\quad\varepsilon(g)=1$}

An element of a <coalgebra> satisfying the displayed identities. A <group-like element> in a <Hopf algebra> is invertible with inverse its <antipode> image.

= Comodule
{parent=Coalgebra}
{title2=$(M,\rho)$}
{wiki}

A right comodule is an object $M$ with a <coaction> $\rho:M\to M\otimes C$ satisfying the coassociativity and <counit> laws. This definition extends from <coalgebras> to <comonoids> in a <monoidal category>.

= Comodule tensor transformation formula
{parent=Comodule}
{title2=$\varphi(u\otimes v)=\sum u_{(0)}\otimes v_{(0)}\gamma(u_{(1)},v_{(1)})$}

A natural underlying transformation of tensor bifunctors on right <comodules> over a <field> has components $\varphi_{M,N}(u\otimes v)=\sum u_{(0)}\otimes v_{(0)}\gamma(u_{(1)},v_{(1)})$. Recover $\gamma=(\varepsilon\otimes\varepsilon)\varphi_{H,H}$; colinearity and monoidal axioms impose further equations.

= Multiplication compatibility for a comodule tensor transformation
{parent=Comodule tensor transformation formula}
{title2=$m*\gamma=\gamma*n$}

For a transformation from the tensor built using $n$ to the tensor built using $m$, colinearity is $m*\gamma=\gamma*n$: $\sum m(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})\gamma(a_{(2)},b_{(2)})=\sum\gamma(a_{(1)},b_{(1)})n(a_{(2)},b_{(2)})$. Scalar maps enter this <convolution product for coalgebra maps> via the common algebra unit.

= Corestriction functor for comodules
{parent=Comodule}
{title2=$f_*$}

A <comonoid morphism> $f:H\to K$ induces a <functor> $f_*$ on right <comodules>, replacing $\rho$ by $(1\otimes f)\rho$ and preserving underlying objects and arrows. For <bimonoids>, it is strict monoidal exactly when $f$ is also a <monoid morphism>.

= Comodule natural transformation formula
{parent=Corestriction functor for comodules}
{title2=$\beta_M(u)=\sum u_{(0)}\alpha(u_{(1)})$}

A <natural transformation> between corestriction functors over a <field> has components $\beta_M(u)=\sum u_{(0)}\alpha(u_{(1)})$, recovered by $\alpha=\varepsilon\beta_H$. Monoidality forces $\alpha$ to be a unital <algebra homomorphism over a field>; for a <Hopf algebra>, $\alpha S$ supplies the inverse.

= Coaction
{parent=Comodule}
{title2=$\rho:M\to M\otimes C$}

The structure map of a <comodule>; a right coaction satisfies $(\rho\otimes1)\rho=(1\otimes\Delta)\rho$ with coherent parentheses, and $(1\otimes\varepsilon)\rho$ is the inverse right <unitor>.

= Convolution product for coalgebra maps
{parent=Coalgebra}
{title2=$f*g=m_A(f\otimes g)\Delta_C$}

For a <coalgebra> $C$ and a unital associative <algebra over a commutative ring> $A$, maps $f,g:C\to A$ have convolution $f*g=m_A(f\otimes g)\Delta_C$, with unit $j_A\varepsilon_C$. The two-sided convolution inverse of $1_H$ is the <antipode>.

= Sweedler notation
{parent=Coalgebra}
{c}
{title2=$\Delta h=\sum h_{(1)}\otimes h_{(2)}$}

The notation $\Delta h=\sum h_{(1)}\otimes h_{(2)}$ suppresses the summation index of a <comultiplication>; iterated indices use coassociativity. For a <comodule>, write $\rho(u)=\sum u_{(0)}\otimes u_{(1)}$.

= Multilinear map
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

= Multilinear
{synonym}

A <multilinear map> $F:V_1\times\cdots\times V_r\to W$ is a map between <vector spaces> that is linear in each argument when all other arguments are fixed. For example the evaluation $(\omega,v)\mapsto\omega(v)$ is bilinear, and the <determinant> is multilinear in its columns. The <universal property of a tensor product> replaces this separate linearity by a single <linear map> from $V_1\otimes\cdots\otimes V_r$.

= Bilinear map
{parent=Multilinear map}

= Bilinear
{synonym}

A bilinear map is a map $B:V\times W\to U$ between <vector spaces> that is <linear> in each argument when the other is fixed. The nonlinear <temperature> <advection> can be written as the diagonal of a bilinear map, $\mathcal N(\phi,\chi)=-\mathbf u[\phi]\cdot\nabla\chi$, when <velocity> depends linearly on <temperature>. Unlike a <bilinear form>, its output need not be a scalar.

= Bilinearity
{parent=Bilinear map}

A map $B:V\times W\to Z$ between <vector spaces> is bilinear when it is <linear> in either argument with the other held fixed. A scalar-valued <bilinear form> is the case $V=W$ and $Z$ is the scalar field. Checking <bilinearity> is one of the algebraic steps in verifying a real <inner product>; being a <symmetric bilinear form> and having positive squared <norm> impose further conditions.

= Symmetric bilinear diagonal-parallel lemma
{parent=Bilinear map}
{title2=$S(u,v)=V(u)v+V(v)u$}

Let $S:W\times W\to W$ be a symmetric <bilinear map> on a finite-dimensional real <vector space>. If $S(v,v)$ is parallel to $v$ for every $v$, then it has the displayed form for one <covector> $V$. Write $S(e_i,e_i)=\alpha_i e_i$ in a <basis>. Applying the condition to $e_i+e_j$ and $e_i-e_j$ excludes every component outside their span and gives $S(e_i,e_j)=(\alpha_j e_i+\alpha_i e_j)/2$. Set $V(e_i)=\alpha_i/2$ and extend by <bilinearity>. Dimension one follows by the same definition. The <trace> identity $S^a{}_{ac}=(n+1)V_c$ proves uniqueness without depending on the chosen <basis>.

= Derivative of a continuous bilinear map
{parent=Bilinear map}
{title2=$Df(a,b)(h,k)=f(h,b)+f(a,k)$}

A continuous bilinear map obeys $\|f(h,k)\|\le C\|h\|\|k\|$. Expanding $f(a+h,b+k)$ leaves the displayed linear term and remainder $f(h,k)$. For product <norm> $\rho=(\|h\|^2+\|k\|^2)^{1/2}$, the remainder has <norm> at most $C\rho^2/2$, hence is $o(\rho)$. This proves the <Fréchet derivative> formula.

= Rank bound for a nonsingular complex bilinear map
{parent=Bilinear map}
{title2=$\operatorname{rank}\phi\geq\dim U+\dim V-1$}

Let $U,V$ be nonzero finite-dimensional complex <vector spaces>, and let $\phi:U\otimes V\to W$ have injective restrictions when either nonzero factor is fixed. It induces a map from $\mathbb{CP}^{a-1}\times\mathbb{CP}^{b-1}$ to $\mathbb{CP}^{r-1}$, where $a=\dim U$, $b=\dim V$, and $r=\operatorname{rank}\phi$. Pullback of the degree-two generator is $x+y$, since both factor restrictions are projective linear embeddings. Its $(a+b-2)$th power is $\binom{a+b-2}{a-1}x^{a-1}y^{b-1}\ne0$ in integral <cohomology>. The target must therefore have complex dimension at least $a+b-2$. Positivity of both factor dimensions is necessary; a zero factor can make the claimed inequality false.

= Complex bilinear dimension bound
{parent=Bilinear map}
{title2=$\dim\operatorname{im}\phi\geq\dim U+\dim V-1$}

For nonzero finite-dimensional complex <vector spaces> $U,V$, suppose a <bilinear map> never vanishes on a pair of nonzero vectors. Equivalently, each map obtained by fixing one nonzero argument is injective. If its associated map on $U\otimes V$ has image dimension $r$, projectivization gives a map to $\mathbb{CP}^{r-1}$ whose positive degree-two class pulls back to $x+y$. The nonzero top power $(x+y)^{\dim U+\dim V-2}$ in the product <cohomology ring of complex projective space> proves the bound. Multiplication of polynomials of bounded degree attains equality. Nonzero hypotheses matter: with a zero factor, slice conditions can be vacuous.

= Alternating multilinear map
{parent=Multilinear algebra}

An alternating multilinear map vanishes whenever two arguments are equal. Swapping two arguments reverses its sign.

= Alternating trilinear form
{parent=Alternating multilinear map}

An alternating trilinear form is a scalar-valued map, linear in three arguments, which vanishes whenever two arguments agree. In characteristic zero, interchanging two arguments negates its value, while cyclic permutation preserves it. For a <symmetric bilinear form> $B$ on a <Lie algebra>, the tensor $B([x,y],z)$ is alternating exactly when $B$ is an <invariant bilinear form on a Lie algebra>.

= Tensor product
{title2=$\otimes$}
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

A <tensor product> turns a <bilinear map> into a <linear map>. For <vector spaces> $V,W$ over the same <field> $F$, its elements are finite sums of <pure tensors> $v\otimes w$, subject to <bilinearity>:
$$
\begin{aligned}
(v+v')\otimes w&=v\otimes w+v'\otimes w,\\
v\otimes(w+w')&=v\otimes w+v\otimes w',\\
(av)\otimes w&=a(v\otimes w)=v\otimes(aw).
\end{aligned}
$$
The <scalar> $a$ belongs to $F$; writing $V\otimes_F W$ makes this choice explicit. These rules preserve separate <linearity> while allowing arbitrary <linear combinations> of products.<Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[§9D, 9.71–9.74, printed pp. 372–373: tensor products, bilinearity, and bases]{ref}

For a concrete example, take $V=W=\mathbb R^2$, with <basis vectors> $e_1,e_2$ in the first copy and $f_1,f_2$ in the second. The four <tensor-product basis> elements are
$$
e_1\otimes f_1,\quad e_1\otimes f_2,\quad e_2\otimes f_1,\quad e_2\otimes f_2.
$$
The <pure tensor> made from $v=2e_1+e_2$ and $w=-f_1+3f_2$ expands to
$$
v\otimes w=-2e_1\otimes f_1+6e_1\otimes f_2-e_2\otimes f_1+3e_2\otimes f_2.
$$
Each <basis vector> in one factor can interact with each <basis vector> in the other. This is why <dimensions> multiply; the <tensor-product coefficient matrix> displays these four interactions as a two-by-two grid.

The <tensor product> is different from the <Cartesian product> $V\times W$. With componentwise <vector addition> and <scalar multiplication>, the latter contains pairs and has <dimension> $\dim V+\dim W$. The map $\tau(v,w)=v\otimes w$ is <bilinear>, generally neither <linear> on that <Cartesian product> nor <injective> nor <surjective>. In our example, $\tau(2v,2w)=4\tau(v,w)$, and $\tau(2v,w/2)=\tau(v,w)$; the <separability of a tensor> explains why some outputs cannot be reached at all. Both spaces happen to have <dimension> four when $V=W=\mathbb R^2$, but this coincidence does not identify their structures.

= Universal property of a tensor product
{parent=Tensor product}
{title2=$B=L\circ\tau$}

For a <bilinear map> $B:V\times W\to U$, there is exactly one <linear map> $L:V\otimes_F W\to U$ satisfying $L(v\otimes w)=B(v,w)$. Equivalently, $B=L\circ\tau$, where $\tau(v,w)=v\otimes w$. The <universal property of a tensor product> concerns the <vector space> together with this distinguished <bilinear map>.<Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[§9D, Theorem 9.79(a), printed p. 375: unique linear factorization]{ref}

Here is a complete calculation over the <real numbers>. Define the <bilinear map>
$$
B(v,w)=\bigl(v_1w_1+2v_1w_2+v_2w_2,\ v_2w_1-v_1w_2\bigr).
$$
Its values on pairs of <basis vectors> determine $L$ on the four <tensor-product basis> elements:
$$
\begin{aligned}
L(e_1\otimes f_1)&=(1,0),&L(e_1\otimes f_2)&=(2,-1),\\
L(e_2\otimes f_1)&=(0,1),&L(e_2\otimes f_2)&=(1,0).
\end{aligned}
$$
Consequently, for $t=\sum_{i,j=1}^2c_{ij}e_i\otimes f_j$,
$$
L(t)=(c_{11}+2c_{12}+c_{22},\ c_{21}-c_{12}).
$$
For $v=(2,1)$ and $w=(-1,3)$, the direct calculation gives $B(v,w)=(-2+12+3,-1-6)=(13,-7)$. Using the expansion of $v\otimes w$ instead gives $L(v\otimes w)=-2(1,0)+6(2,-1)-(0,1)+3(1,0)=(13,-7)$. The <linear map> also handles a nonseparable <tensor> such as $t=e_1\otimes f_1+e_2\otimes f_2$: $L(t)=(2,0)$, even though no single pair has $v\otimes w=t$.

\Image[tensor-product-bilinear-factorization.png]
{title=A bilinear calculation factors through one linear map on four tensor coefficients}

A short proof uses a <tensor-product basis>: assign $L(e_i\otimes f_j)=B(e_i,f_j)$ and extend by <linearity>. Expanding both inputs verifies $L(v\otimes w)=B(v,w)$ by <bilinearity>; the assigned values on a <basis> force uniqueness. More generally, every <multilinear map> $F:V_1\times\cdots\times V_r\to U$ factors uniquely through a <linear map> from $V_1\otimes\cdots\otimes V_r$. This lets formulas involving several inputs define <linear maps> without choosing <coordinates>; <tensor contraction> uses the evaluation pairing between a <vector> and a <covector>.

= Tensor-product basis
{parent=Tensor product}
{title2=$\{e_i\otimes f_j\},\quad\dim(V\otimes_F W)=\dim V\dim W$}

If $(e_1,\ldots,e_m)$ and $(f_1,\ldots,f_n)$ are <bases> of <finite-dimensional vector spaces> $V,W$ over $F$, then the $mn$ products $e_i\otimes f_j$ form a <basis> of $V\otimes_F W$. There is one independent <coordinate> for every pair of indices.<Linear Algebra Done Right by Sheldon Axler (4th edition)>[§9D, Theorems 9.72 and 9.74, printed pp. 372–373: dimension and tensor-product basis]{ref}

For a proof outline, <bilinearity> expands each <pure tensor> as
$$
\left(\sum_i v_ie_i\right)\otimes\left(\sum_j w_jf_j\right)=\sum_{i,j}v_iw_j(e_i\otimes f_j),
$$
so these products form a <spanning set>. For <linear independence>, take the <coordinate functionals> $e^k,f^\ell$ from the two <dual bases>. The <bilinear map> $(v,w)\mapsto e^k(v)f^\ell(w)$ induces a <linear functional> $\lambda_{k\ell}$ on the <tensor product>, satisfying $\lambda_{k\ell}(e_i\otimes f_j)=\delta_{ki}\delta_{\ell j}$. Applying it to a proposed zero <linear combination> selects just $c_{k\ell}$, forcing every coefficient to vanish. Thus <dimensions> multiply, rather than add. This finite-dimensional count includes a zero factor, whose <tensor product> has <dimension> zero.

= Tensor-product coefficient matrix
{parent=Tensor-product basis}
{title2=$C(t)=(c_{ij})$}

After choosing <ordered bases>, identify a <tensor> $t=\sum_{i,j}c_{ij}e_i\otimes f_j$ with its <matrix> $C(t)=(c_{ij})$. This is a <linear isomorphism> between $V\otimes_F W$ and the <vector space> of $m$-by-$n$ <matrices>: each <tensor-product basis> element becomes the <matrix> with one entry equal to one and all others zero. In particular, a <pure tensor> has the <outer product> coefficient formula
$$
C(v\otimes w)=vw^T,\qquad C\bigl((2,1)\otimes(-1,3)\bigr)=\begin{pmatrix}-2&6\\-1&3\end{pmatrix}.
$$
Here $v,w$ on the right denote column <coordinate vectors>. The identification depends on the chosen <bases>, while the <tensor> itself does not. Over the <complex numbers>, the same formula uses the ordinary <transpose> $w^T$, with no <complex conjugation>; replacing it by the <conjugate transpose> $w^*$ would conjugate the <scalar> in the second input, violating <bilinearity>.

\Image[tensor-product-coefficient-grids.png]
{title=A pure tensor has an outer-product coefficient grid, while the identity grid needs two pure tensors}

= Separability of a tensor
{parent=Tensor product}
{title2=$t=v\otimes w$}

In a two-factor <tensor product> over a <field>, a <pure tensor> is also called a separable or decomposable <tensor>: it can be expressed as one product $v\otimes w$. Every <tensor> is a finite sum of <pure tensors>, but a sum need not itself be a <pure tensor>.<Tensor Products I by Keith Conrad>[§3, printed p. 9: elementary, simple, decomposable, and pure tensors; Example 2.1, printed pp. 4–5: outer products do not fill the matrix space]{ref}

For <finite-dimensional vector spaces>, the <tensor-product coefficient matrix> gives a decisive test: a nonzero <tensor> is a <pure tensor> exactly when its <matrix rank> is one. Indeed, every column of $vw^T$ is a <scalar multiple> of $v$. Conversely, if all columns of a nonzero <matrix> are multiples of one nonzero column $v$, collect their multipliers in $w$ to obtain $C=vw^T$. The zero <tensor> is also a <pure tensor>, for example $0\otimes w$, but its <matrix rank> is zero.

In $\mathbb R^2\otimes_{\mathbb R}\mathbb R^2$, the <tensor>
$$
t=e_1\otimes f_1+e_2\otimes f_2,\qquad C(t)=\begin{pmatrix}1&0\\0&1\end{pmatrix},
$$
is nonseparable: its <matrix rank> is two and its <determinant> is one, whereas $\det(vw^T)=0$. Directly, trying $t=(ae_1+be_2)\otimes(cf_1+df_2)$ requires $ac=bd=1$ and $ad=bc=0$. The first two equations force all four <scalars> nonzero, contradicting the others. In contrast, products sharing a factor do combine: $e_1\otimes f_1+e_1\otimes f_2=e_1\otimes(f_1+f_2)$. Thus being written as two summands alone does not establish nonseparability.

= Two-factor tensor rank
{parent=Tensor product}
{title2=$\operatorname{rank}_{\otimes}(t)=\operatorname{rank}C(t)$}

The <two-factor tensor rank> is the smallest number of <pure tensors> whose sum equals $t$, with zero allowed as the empty sum. For two <finite-dimensional vector spaces> over the same <field>, it equals the <matrix rank> of the <tensor-product coefficient matrix>. A sum of $r$ <outer products> has <column space> contained in the span of their $r$ first factors, so its <matrix rank> is at most $r$. Conversely, choose a <basis> $u_1,\ldots,u_r$ for the <column space>, express each column in that <basis>, and collect the coefficients into row <vectors> $w_k^T$. This yields $C=\sum_{k=1}^ru_kw_k^T$, hence a sum of $r$ <pure tensors>.

The <pure tensor> $(2,1)\otimes(-1,3)$ therefore has <two-factor tensor rank> one, whereas $e_1\otimes f_1+e_2\otimes f_2$ has <two-factor tensor rank> two. Both have two factors: the number of factors and the minimum number of product summands are different counts. Over the <real numbers>, the <singular value decomposition> supplies a useful decomposition $C=\sum_k\sigma_ku_kv_k^T$, with one nonzero term per <matrix rank>. The two-factor rank equality does not extend to three or more factors by merely choosing one <matrix> layout.

= Tensor algebra
{title2=$T(V)$}
{parent=Tensor product}
{wiki}

= Free tensor algebra
{synonym}

The tensor algebra of a <vector space> $V$ is the <graded algebra>
$$
T(V)=\bigoplus_{n\geq0}V^{\otimes n},
$$
with multiplication given by concatenation of tensors.

= Truncated tensor algebra
{parent=Tensor algebra}
{title2=$T^{(N)}(V)=\bigoplus_{k=0}^N V^{\otimes k}$}

The truncated <tensor algebra> discards products of degree greater than $N$. Its multiplication is $(a\otimes b)^{(k)}=\sum_{j=0}^k a^{(j)}\otimes b^{(k-j)}$. Every element with scalar part $1$ is invertible by a finite geometric series in its positive-degree part. Truncated exponential and logarithm are finite polynomials.

= Tensor power
{title2=$V^{\otimes n}$}
{parent=Tensor algebra}

The nth tensor power is the iterated <tensor product of modules> $V^{\otimes n}=V\otimes\cdots\otimes V$.

= Tensor square
{title2=$V\otimes V$}
{parent=Tensor power}

The tensor square is the second <tensor power> $V^{\otimes2}=V\otimes V$. Over a field of characteristic different from two it decomposes as the <direct sum> of the <symmetric square> and <exterior square>.

= Symmetric algebra
{title2=$S(V)$}
{parent=Tensor algebra}
{wiki}

The symmetric algebra is the quotient of $T(V)$ by the ideal generated by $v\otimes w-w\otimes v$. It is graded by <symmetric powers>:
$$
S(V)=\bigoplus_{n\geq0}S^nV.
$$

= Symmetric power
{title2=$S^nV$}
{parent=Symmetric algebra}
{wiki}

The nth symmetric power $S^nV$ is the quotient of $V^{\otimes n}$ that identifies tensors differing by a permutation of their factors. Equivalently, over characteristic zero it is the subspace of symmetric tensors.

= Weight-transfer proof of irreducibility of symmetric powers
{parent=Symmetric power}
{title2=$E_{ij}\cdot x^a=a_jx^{a+e_i-e_j}$}

In the <symmetric power> $\operatorname{Sym}^m\mathbb C^n$ of the defining <unitary group> representation, diagonal <torus> weights are indexed by nonnegative integer tuples summing to $m$, with one monomial per <weight space>. Torus averaging extracts a monomial from any nonzero invariant complex subspace. The complexified <Lie algebra> acts by $E_{ij}=x_i\partial_{x_j}$ on the symmetric algebra. Whenever $a_j>0$, the displayed action moves one unit of degree from $j$ to $i$ with nonzero coefficient. Finite transfers connect every degree-$m$ monomial, so the subspace is the entire representation. The case $m=0$ is the one-dimensional trivial representation. This proof needs neither the <Weyl character formula> nor the <Weyl dimension formula>.

= Symmetric powers of the three-dimensional SU2 representation
{parent=Symmetric power}
{title2=$\operatorname{Sym}^n(V_2)=\bigoplus_{j=0}^{\lfloor n/2\rfloor}V_{2n-4j}$}

The three-dimensional irreducible <representation> $V_2$ of <SU(2)> has weights $2,0,-2$. Thus the <character> of its $n$th <symmetric power> is $\sum_{a+b+c=n}t^{2(a-c)}$. Its weight $2\ell$ has multiplicity $\lfloor(n-|\ell|)/2\rfloor+1$ for $|\ell|\leq n$. Subtracting adjacent weight multiplicities gives one copy of each $V_{2n},V_{2n-4},\ldots$. The invariant subspace is one-dimensional for even $n$ and zero for odd $n$.

= Character generating series of symmetric powers
{parent=Symmetric power}
{title2=$\sum_{n\geq0}\chi_{S^nV}(g)t^n=\det(I-tg)^{-1}$}

For <eigenvalues> $\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_d$ of a finite-order linear action, its <symmetric power> characters satisfy $\sum_{n\geq0}\chi_{S^nV}t^n=\prod_j(1-\lambda_jt)^{-1}$. The denominator is the alternating generating <polynomial> of its <exterior power> characters.

= Symmetric square
{title2=$S^2V$}
{parent=Symmetric power}

The symmetric square is the quotient of $V\otimes V$ by $v\otimes w-w\otimes v$, and records symmetric tensors of rank two.

= Traceless symmetric square of the defining even orthogonal representation
{parent=Symmetric square}
{title2=$\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb C^{2n})=\mathbb C\oplus\operatorname{Sym}^2_0(\mathbb C^{2n})$}

For $n\geq2$, contraction with the invariant <symmetric bilinear form> splits off the trivial line in the <symmetric square> of the defining <special orthogonal group> <representation>. Its <null space> is the traceless <symmetric square>. It contains a <highest-weight vector> of <weight> $2e_1$, and the <Weyl dimension formula> gives $(n+1)(2n-1)$, exactly its <dimension>. <Complete reducibility of compact-group representations> therefore makes that <null space> <irreducible>. The entire <symmetric square> is reducible because of the invariant line.

= Tensor-square decomposition of the defining even orthogonal representation
{parent=Traceless symmetric square of the defining even orthogonal representation}
{title2=$V\otimes V=L(2\varepsilon_1)\oplus L(\varepsilon_1+\varepsilon_2)\oplus L(0)$}

For $V=\mathbb C^{2n}$, $n\geq3$, the <symmetric square> splits into an invariant line and its irreducible traceless part of highest weight $2\varepsilon_1$. The <exterior square of the defining orthogonal representation> is the adjoint module of highest weight $\varepsilon_1+\varepsilon_2$. For $n=2$, the exterior square splits instead into highest weights $\varepsilon_1-\varepsilon_2$ and $\varepsilon_1+\varepsilon_2$. This exception reflects that <so4 Lie algebra> is semisimple but not simple.

= Symmetric square of a direct sum
{parent=Symmetric square}
{title2=$S^2(V\oplus W)$}

Over every <field>, including <characteristic two>, there is a natural isomorphism
$$
S^2(V\oplus W)\cong S^2V\oplus(V\otimes W)\oplus S^2W.
$$
The middle summand maps $v\otimes w$ to the mixed product $vw$. Monomial <bases> prove bijectivity, and the <Leibniz rule> makes the map equivariant for <Lie algebra representations>.

= Symmetric trace-free square of the defining orthogonal representation
{parent=Symmetric square}
{title2=$\operatorname{Sym}_0^2(\mathbb R^d)$}

= Symmetric traceless rank-two tensor
{synonym}

= Symmetric traceless square
{synonym}

For the defining real representation of $SO(d)$, the <symmetric square> splits as a scalar trace plus symmetric trace-free tensors: $\operatorname{Sym}^2(\mathbb R^d)=\mathbb R\oplus\operatorname{Sym}_0^2(\mathbb R^d)$. The trace projection is $S\mapsto S-(\operatorname{tr}S)I/d$, so the second summand has dimension $d(d+1)/2-1$.

For $d\geq3$ the trace-free summand is an irreducible real <group representation>. To see this, diagonalize a nonzero symmetric trace-free matrix in a nonzero invariant subspace. Two diagonal entries differ. Infinitesimal rotation in their plane yields a nonzero symmetric off-diagonal matrix; rotations carry it to every coordinate pair, and a rotation through $\pi/4$ produces a difference of two diagonal entries. These off-diagonal matrices and diagonal differences span all symmetric trace-free matrices, so the invariant subspace is the whole summand. For $d=24$, its dimension is 299, the third-level nontrivial multiplet of the <lowest levels of a fully transverse ND bosonic string>.

= Exterior algebra
{title2=$\Lambda^\bullet V$}
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

The exterior algebra is the graded algebra
$$
\Lambda^\bullet V=\bigoplus_{k=0}^{\dim V}\Lambda^kV
$$
with multiplication given by the antisymmetric wedge product.

= Bivector
{parent=Exterior algebra}
{title2=$w\in\Lambda^2V$}
{wiki}

A bivector is an antisymmetric contravariant tensor of degree two. A simple bivector is a single <exterior product> $u\wedge v$. A general bivector is a sum of such products; in four dimensions simplicity is equivalent to $w\wedge w=0$ for a nonzero bivector. Covariant counterparts are <differential two-forms>.

= Exterior product
{parent=Exterior algebra}
{title2=$\wedge$}

= Wedge product
{synonym}

The exterior product is the multiplication $\bigwedge^pV\otimes\bigwedge^qV\to\bigwedge^{p+q}V$ of the <exterior algebra>. For <homogeneous elements of a graded algebra>, $u\wedge v=(-1)^{pq}v\wedge u$.

= Polynomial polyvector field
{parent=Exterior algebra}
{title2=$\bigwedge_A^p\operatorname{Der}_k(A)$}

For a <polynomial ring> $A$, a degree-$p$ polynomial polyvector field is an element of $\bigwedge_A^p\operatorname{Der}_k(A)$. Its product is the <exterior product>. Together with the <Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket> these spaces form a <Gerstenhaber algebra>.

= Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket
{parent=Polynomial polyvector field}
{c}

The left Schouten-Nijenhuis bracket on <polynomial polyvector fields> is the degree-minus-one <Gerstenhaber bracket> extending the <commutator> of <derivations> and $[D,f]=D(f)$ by graded antisymmetry and the left <graded Leibniz rule>. For $\Pi=\partial_X\wedge\partial_Y$, this convention gives $[h\Pi,f]=h(f_Y\partial_X-f_X\partial_Y)$ and $[F\partial_X+G\partial_Y,h\Pi]=(D(h)-h(F_X+G_Y))\Pi$. A right insertion convention reverses the degree-two-with-function formula.

= Grassmann algebra
{c}
{parent=Exterior algebra}
{wiki}

A Grassmann algebra is an exterior algebra regarded as an algebra of anticommuting generators. Its odd elements satisfy $\theta_i\theta_j=-\theta_j\theta_i$.

= Grassmann parity
{c}
{parent=Grassmann algebra}
{title2=$|X|\in\mathbb Z/2\mathbb Z$}

The parity grading records whether a homogeneous element has even or odd Grassmann degree. Homogeneous elements obey $XY=(-1)^{|X||Y|}YX$. An odd differential uses the <graded Leibniz rule>; this is the sign bookkeeping behind <left-acting BRST differential> calculations.

= Grassmann derivative
{c}
{parent=Grassmann algebra}
{title2=$\partial_\theta^L,\quad\partial_\theta^R$}

A <Grassmann derivative> is an odd differentiation operation on a <Grassmann algebra>. A <left Grassmann derivative> obeys $\partial_\theta^L(ab)=(\partial_\theta^La)b+(-1)^{|a|}a(\partial_\theta^Lb)$ for homogeneous $a$. Right derivatives use the corresponding right-sided product rule. Their placement must be fixed when differentiating fermionic sources in a <generating functional>, since commuting ordinary derivatives would lose <fermionic signs>.

= Left Grassmann derivative
{parent=Grassmann algebra}
{title2=$\partial_\theta^L$}

Left differentiation with respect to an odd <Grassmann variable> is the odd linear operation $\partial_\theta^L\theta=1$, with zero derivative on every other generator. For a homogeneous element $a$ of parity $|a|$, it obeys the graded product rule
$$
\partial_\theta^L(ab)=(\partial_\theta^La)b+(-1)^{|a|}a(\partial_\theta^Lb).
$$
For independent odd generators, $\partial_{\bar\theta}^L(\theta\bar\theta)=-\theta$. This sign comes from moving the derivative through the first odd factor in the <Grassmann algebra>. It fixes chirality in the <chiral-superfield component expansion>; an ordinary commuting-variable product rule would give the wrong sign.

= Grassmann variable
{c}
{parent=Grassmann algebra}

A Grassmann variable is an odd generator $\theta$ of a <Grassmann algebra>. In characteristic zero it obeys $\theta^2=0$, so functions of finitely many Grassmann variables have finite Taylor expansions.

= Exterior power
{title2=$\Lambda^kV$}
{parent=Exterior algebra}

The $k$th exterior power $\Lambda^kV$ is the quotient of $V^{\otimes k}$ that makes every tensor with two equal factors zero. It represents alternating $k$-linear maps and has dimension $\binom{\dim V}{k}$ in finite dimensions.

= Exterior square
{title2=$\bigwedge^2V$}
{parent=Exterior power}

The exterior square is the quotient of $V\otimes V$ by tensors $v\otimes v$, and records antisymmetric tensors of rank two.

= Exterior square of a direct sum
{parent=Exterior square}
{title2=$\bigwedge^2(V\oplus W)\cong\bigwedge^2V\oplus\bigwedge^2W\oplus(V\otimes W)$}

The <exterior square> of a <direct sum> splits into wedges of two vectors from $V$, two from $W$, or one from each. The mixed map sends $v\otimes w$ to $(v,0)\wedge(0,w)$. A combined <basis> proves it is an isomorphism, and the <exterior-power Lie algebra representation> proves equivariance for modules. No division by $2$ is used.

= Symplectic contraction of an exterior square
{parent=Exterior square}

For a <symplectic vector space> $(V,\omega)$, contraction is the map $c:\Lambda^2V\to\mathbb C$ defined by $c(v\wedge w)=\omega(v,w)$. It is equivariant for the <symplectic Lie algebra>. In dimension four its five-dimensional <kernel> is irreducible of <highest weight> $\omega_2$, while the invariant inverse-form bivector spans a complementary <trivial Lie algebra representation>.

= Primitive exterior square
{parent=Symplectic contraction of an exterior square}
{title2=$\Lambda_0^2V$}

The primitive exterior square of a <symplectic vector space> is the <kernel> of its <symplectic contraction of an exterior square>. In <dimension> four, the invariant inverse-form bivector $\Omega$ has nonzero wedge square. Choose $\operatorname{vol}=\Omega\wedge\Omega/2$; the <symmetric bilinear form> $\xi\wedge\eta=B(\xi,\eta)\operatorname{vol}$ is <nondegenerate> on $\Lambda^2V$. The primitive subspace is $\Omega^\perp$, and therefore inherits a nondegenerate form of dimension five. The <symplectic Lie algebra> acts on it by infinitesimal <orthogonal transformations>.

= Tensor
{parent=Multilinear algebra}
{wiki}

A rank-$n$ tensor on a vector space $V$ is a multilinear map of $n$ vector or covector arguments, equivalently an element of an $n$-fold tensor product. Its components transform by one copy of the change-of-basis matrix for each index.

= Tensor index antisymmetrization
{parent=Tensor}
{title2=$T_{[ab]}$}

Antisymmetrization over selected <tensor> indices averages their permutations with the <sign of a permutation>. Square brackets denote it, for example $T_{[ab]}=(T_{ab}-T_{ba})/2$. It projects onto the alternating part. Antisymmetrized second derivatives of a scalar vanish for a <torsion-free connection>, but antisymmetrized later derivatives act on a tensor and can produce <curvature>.

= Tensor index symmetrization
{parent=Tensor}
{title2=$T_{(ab)}$}

Symmetrization over $r$ selected <tensor> indices is the average over all $r!$ permutations, with every sign positive. Parentheses denote this operation, for example $T_{(ab)}=(T_{ab}+T_{ba})/2$. It is a <projection> onto the symmetric part and commutes with a <covariant derivative> acting on indices outside the parentheses.

= Axially invariant second-rank tensor
{parent=Tensor}
{title2=$T=\alpha I+\beta nn^T$}

A <Cartesian second-rank tensor> invariant under every proper rotation preserving an unoriented axis $n$ has this form. Rotations about the axis give a planar block $aI+bJ$ and eliminate mixed axial-planar entries. A half-turn about a transverse axis reverses $J$, forcing $b=0$. If only rotations about the oriented axis are imposed, this planar antisymmetric term may survive.

= Smallest axial rotation group forcing second-rank transverse isotropy
{parent=Axially invariant second-rank tensor}
{title2=$\langle R_z(2\pi/3),R_x(\pi)\rangle\cong S_3$}

The six-element <dihedral group> generated by the displayed rotations already forces every invariant second-rank <tensor> to have the same form as an <axially invariant second-rank tensor>. The axial threefold rotation eliminates mixed entries and leaves only a planar scalar plus a planar antisymmetric part; the transverse half-turn eliminates the latter. No smaller finite <subgroup> suffices: cyclic rotation <groups> retain an axial antisymmetric <tensor>, while a four-element noncyclic rotation <group> retains arbitrary diagonal <tensors> along its three mutually perpendicular half-turn axes.

= Cartesian second-rank tensor
{c}
{parent=Tensor}
{title2=$T\prime=RTR^T$}

= Cartesian rank-two tensor
{c}
{synonym}

A Cartesian second-rank tensor has component matrix $T$ in each <orthonormal basis>, with transformation $T\prime=RTR^T$ under an orthogonal component change $R$. Both indices transform; this is why assigning arbitrary matrices independently in different bases does not define a <tensor>. The <determinant>, <trace>, and <Frobenius inner product> with another such tensor are unchanged by these basis transformations.

= Cubically invariant second-rank tensor
{parent=Cartesian second-rank tensor}
{title2=$A=aI$}

A <Cartesian second-rank tensor> in three dimensions invariant under quarter-turn <rotations> about all three coordinate axes is a scalar multiple of the <identity matrix>. Squaring the quarter-turns gives coordinate half-turns; invariance under these forces every off-diagonal component to vanish. The quarter-turns then equate the three diagonal components. This conclusion does not require the original <tensor> to be symmetric.

= Scalar, symmetric-traceless and axial tensor decomposition
{parent=Cartesian second-rank tensor}
{title2=$P_{ij}=P\delta_{ij}+S_{ij}+\epsilon_{ijk}A_k$}

Every three-dimensional <Cartesian second-rank tensor> splits into scalar, symmetric-traceless, and antisymmetric parts. Their coefficients are $P=P_{kk}/3$, $S_{ij}=(P_{ij}+P_{ji})/2-P\delta_{ij}$, and $A_k=\epsilon_{kij}P_{ij}/2$. The epsilon contraction identity reconstructs the antisymmetric part and proves uniqueness. Under proper rotations $A$ behaves as a vector, but under reflections it is an <axial vector>.

= Divergence of a Cartesian second-rank tensor
{parent=Cartesian second-rank tensor}
{title2=$U_i=\partial_jT_{ij}$}

For a constant orthogonal Cartesian coordinate change $x'=Rx$, a <Cartesian second-rank tensor> obeys $T'_{ij}=R_{ik}R_{jl}T_{kl}$. The <chain rule> gives $\partial'_j=R_{jm}\partial_m$. Consequently $\partial'_jT'_{ij}=R_{ik}R_{jl}R_{jm}\partial_mT_{kl}=R_{ik}\partial_lT_{kl}$, the transformation rule for a <vector>. Under non-Cartesian position-dependent coordinate changes, partial derivatives must be replaced by the appropriate <covariant derivative>.

= Oriented-axis invariant Cartesian tensor
{parent=Cartesian second-rank tensor}
{title2=$T_{ij}=\alpha(\delta_{ij}-n_in_j)+\beta n_in_j+\omega\epsilon_{ijk}n_k$}

A <Cartesian second-rank tensor> invariant under all rotations about an oriented unit axis $n$ has the displayed form. A half-turn about the axis eliminates the mixed axial-transverse entries, and a quarter-turn makes the transverse block a scalar matrix plus an antisymmetric planar rotation generator. Conversely both transverse terms commute with every rotation about the axis. The antisymmetric term is allowed unless symmetry or an additional axis-reversing rotation is imposed. This differs from an <axially invariant second-rank tensor> for an unoriented axis.

= Coordinate half-turn invariance of a second-rank tensor
{parent=Cartesian second-rank tensor}

A <Cartesian second-rank tensor> invariant under half-turns about all three coordinate axes is diagonal. Each half-turn has diagonal signs, and each off-diagonal entry changes sign under at least one of them. Invariance therefore kills all off-diagonal entries. It does not make the diagonal entries equal: that requires additional rotational invariance, as for an <isotropic second-rank tensor>.

= Composition of mixed tensors
{parent=Tensor}
{title2=$C^a{}_b=B^a{}_cA^c{}_b$}

A type-$(1,1)$ <tensor> identifies with an <endomorphism> of a finite-dimensional <vector space>. Composing two such maps produces another type-$(1,1)$ <tensor>, because $(X,\eta)\mapsto\eta(B(A(X)))$ is a <multilinear map>. In output-first components the result is $C^a{}_b=B^a{}_cA^c{}_b$; the contracted index implements composition, and a basis change conjugates the corresponding matrices.

= Pseudotensor
{parent=Tensor}

Under an orthogonal coordinate transformation $R$, a Cartesian pseudotensor has the usual <tensor> transformation law multiplied by $\det R$. Thus proper <rotation matrices> act as on ordinary tensors, while an orientation-reversing change contributes an extra minus sign. Interpreted as a rank-three pseudotensor, the <Levi-Civita symbol> has the same numerical components in every orthonormal frame, because its ordinary three-index transformation already contributes a factor $\det R$.

= Symmetric tensor
{parent=Tensor}
{wiki}

A symmetric <tensor> is unchanged by every <permutation> of its slots. A <symmetric second-rank tensor> is the rank-two case; symmetry for higher rank requires invariance under every such slot exchange.

= Axisymmetric symmetric rank-two tensor
{parent=Symmetric tensor}
{title2=$T=\alpha I+\beta yy^T$}

A <symmetric tensor> invariant under rotations about a fixed nonzero vector $y$ has the form $\alpha I+\beta yy^T$. Choose the third coordinate axis parallel to $y$: rotation by a half-turn makes the mixed axial-transverse entries vanish, and all transverse rotations make the transverse block a scalar multiple of the identity. Its two transverse eigenvalues are $\alpha$ and its axial eigenvalue is $\alpha+\beta|y|^2$. The <trace> and the quadratic contraction $y^TTy$ determine these two coefficients. Full isotropy holds exactly when $\beta=0$.

= Weighted chord tensor of a sphere
{parent=Axisymmetric symmetric rank-two tensor}
{title2=$T(y)=\int_{|x|=a}(y-x)(y-x)^T|y-x|^{2n-2}\,dS_x$}

For $|y|=a$ and $n>-1$, this surface integral is finite: near $x=y$ its integrand has size $O(|x-y|^{2n})$ and the two-dimensional area element supplies one further power of distance. Rotational covariance and symmetry make it an <axisymmetric symmetric rank-two tensor>. Put $K=\pi(2a)^{2n+2}$. Polar integration about the $y$ axis gives $\operatorname{tr}T=K/(n+1)$ and $y^TTy=a^2K/(n+2)$. Solving $3\alpha+\beta a^2=\operatorname{tr}T$ and $\alpha+\beta a^2=y^TTy/a^2$ gives $\alpha=K/[2(n+1)(n+2)]$ and $\beta=\alpha(2n+1)/a^2$. Thus the weighted chord tensor becomes an <isotropic tensor> exactly at $n=-1/2$.

= Polarization spanning of symmetric tensors
{parent=Symmetric tensor}
{title2=$\operatorname{span}\{a^{\otimes n}\}=\bigl(U^{\otimes n}\bigr)^{S_n}$}

Over a field of <characteristic zero>, degree-$n$ <symmetric tensors> are spanned by pure powers $a^{\otimes n}$. Inclusion-exclusion expands $\sum_{J\subseteq[n]}(-1)^{n-|J|}(\sum_{j\in J}a_j)^{\otimes n}$ into the sum of all <permutation> orderings of $a_1\otimes\cdots\otimes a_n$. Every surviving term uses each of the $n$ labels once. This converts an invariant-tensor spanning problem into pure <tensor powers>.

= Symmetric second-rank tensor
{parent=Symmetric tensor}
{title2=$T_{ij}=T_{ji}$}

A second-rank tensor is symmetric when $T_{ij}=T_{ji}$. Every second-rank tensor has the unique decomposition
$$
T_{ij}=\frac{T_{ij}+T_{ji}}2+\frac{T_{ij}-T_{ji}}2
$$
into symmetric and antisymmetric parts.

= Quotient theorem for Cartesian tensors
{parent=Tensor}
{title2=$T'=RTR^T$}

Suppose an array $T_{ij}$ is specified in each right-handed <orthonormal basis>. If $w_i=T_{ij}v_j$ transforms as a <vector> for every <vector> $v$, then $T$ is a second-order Cartesian <tensor> under those <basis> changes. Indeed $v'=Rv$, $w'=Rw$ imply $T'Rv=RTv$ for every $v$, hence $T'=RTR^T$. The condition on every test <vector> is essential; one contraction is insufficient.

= Symmetric-test criterion for a third-rank Cartesian tensor
{parent=Quotient theorem for Cartesian tensors}
{title2=$d_{ijk}s_{ij}\text{ is a vector for every symmetric }s\ \Longrightarrow\ d^s\text{ is a tensor}$}

If contraction of an array with every <symmetric second-rank tensor> is a <vector> in every orthonormal frame, its part symmetric in the contracted slots obeys the rank-three <tensor> transformation law. The difference between its proposed transformation and actual components is symmetric and contracts to zero against every symmetric <matrix>, so it vanishes. The antisymmetric part is invisible, by the <blindness of symmetric contraction tests to antisymmetric arrays>, and need not be tensorial.
