This is an optimality condition as well as a geometric description. No permitted change within $W$ can remove a further <orthogonal> component of $r$. In the preceding <dot product> example, $r=(2,-1)^T$ is perpendicular to the line spanned by $(1,2)^T$.

The selected <inner product> matters. Keep $x=(3,1)^T$ and the same line, but use the <weighted Euclidean inner product> with $M=\operatorname{diag}(4,1)$. The projection coefficient and residual become
$$
t_M=\frac{\langle x,e\rangle_M}{\langle e,e\rangle_M}
=\frac{14}{8}=\frac74,\qquad
P_W^{(M)}x=\left(\frac74,\frac72\right)^T,\qquad
r_M=\left(\frac54,-\frac52\right)^T.
$$
Now $4(r_M)_1e_1+(r_M)_2e_2=5-5=0$, although $r_M\cdot e=-15/4\ne0$. The <orthogonality> is with respect to $M$, so it need not look perpendicular in the ordinary drawing. The squared weighted error is
$$
\|x-te\|_M^2=4(3-t)^2+(1-2t)^2
=8\left(t-\frac74\right)^2+\frac{25}{2}.
$$
The weighted optimum reduces the first-coordinate error from $2$ to $5/4$, at the cost of increasing the magnitude of the second-coordinate error from $1$ to $5/2$. The weights express which errors are more costly.

\Image[orthogonal-projection-metric-comparison.png]
{title=Changing the inner product changes the closest point: a circle and a weighted error ellipse touch the same permitted line at different points}

In <linear least squares>, residual <orthogonality> to the <column space> is expressed by the <normal equations for linear least squares>. With a <weighted Euclidean inner product>, the corresponding real-matrix equation is $A^TM(x-Ac)=0$. This makes the choice of error measurement part of the mathematical model, rather than merely a plotting convention.

= Orthogonal projection matrix
{parent=Orthogonal projection onto a finite-dimensional subspace}

A <matrix> represents a <linear projection> when applying it twice has the same effect as applying it once. For an <orthogonal projection> with the standard <Hermitian inner product> on $\mathbb C^m$, the additional condition is that the <conjugate transpose> equals the original <matrix>:
$$
P^2=P=P^\dagger.
$$
Thus $P$ is both <idempotent> and a <Hermitian matrix>. Over the <real numbers> this says $P^2=P=P^T$. Its <image of a linear map> is the retained <linear subspace>, while its <kernel of a linear map> is the <orthogonal complement>: <vectors> in the image are unchanged, and <vectors> in the kernel become zero.

For a <matrix> $A$ with <full column rank>, projecting onto its <column space> gives
$$
P=A(A^\dagger A)^{-1}A^\dagger.
$$
The invertibility of $A^\dagger A$ follows from <linear independence> of the columns. If those columns are an <orthonormal set>, $A^\dagger A=I$ and $P=AA^\dagger$. These formulas and the conditions $P^2=P=P^T$ in the real case are developed in https://www.ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06sc-linear-algebra-fall-2011/00e9c8f0eafedeab21a3d079a17ed3d8_MIT18_06SCF11_Ses2.2sum.pdf[MIT 18.06SC, Projections onto Subspaces, pp. 1–3].

For the line spanned by $e=(1,2)^T$,
$$
P=\frac{ee^T}{e^Te}
=\frac15\begin{pmatrix}1&2\\2&4\end{pmatrix},
\qquad
P(3,1)^T=(1,2)^T.
$$
In particular, $Pe=e$ and $P(2,-1)^T=0$. The <eigenvalues> are $1$ on the retained direction and $0$ on the removed direction. This exposes the meaning of an <orthogonal projection> as retaining selected components, rather than stretching them.

Symmetry refers to the specified <inner product>. In the real weighted setting, the formula becomes $P_M=A(A^TMA)^{-1}A^TM$ and satisfies $P_M^2=P_M$ and $P_M^TM=MP_M$. For the preceding weighted line example,
$$
P_M=\frac18\begin{pmatrix}4&2\\8&4\end{pmatrix}.
$$
It is not symmetric under the ordinary <transpose>, but it is an <orthogonal projection> for the <weighted Euclidean inner product>. The matrix criterion $P=P^\dagger$ should therefore be used with the standard <Hermitian inner product>, not silently transferred to a different geometry.

= Transverse projection operator
{parent=Orthogonal projection matrix}

For a nonnull vector $k$ in a space with a nondegenerate bilinear form, $P_{\mu\nu}=\eta_{\mu\nu}-k_\mu k_\nu/k^2$ projects onto the subspace transverse to $k$. It obeys $P_{\mu\nu}k^\nu=0$ and $P^2=P$.

= Least-squares polynomial in an orthogonal-polynomial basis
{parent=Orthogonal projection onto a finite-dimensional subspace}

For orthogonal polynomials $Q_0,\ldots,Q_n$ under a positive weighted integral inner product, the least-squares approximation to $f$ of degree at most $n$ is
$$
p_n^*=\sum_{k=0}^n\frac{\langle f,Q_k\rangle}{\langle Q_k,Q_k\rangle}Q_k.
$$
Its residual is orthogonal to every polynomial of degree at most $n$.

= Orthogonal vectors
{parent=Inner product}
{title2=$\langle u,v\rangle=0$}

= Orthogonal
{synonym}

= Orthogonality
{synonym}

Two <vectors> are orthogonal when their <inner product> is zero.

For real <Euclidean vectors>, the <inner product> is the <dot product>, so $(3,4)$ and $(4,-3)$ are <orthogonal>: $3(4)+4(-3)=0$. For nonzero <Euclidean vectors>, this means their <angle> is $90^\circ$. The <zero vector> is <orthogonal> to every <vector>, although it has no direction or defined <angle>.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06-linear-algebra-spring-2010/resources/lecture-14-orthogonal-vectors-and-subspaces/[Strang, MIT 18.06, Lecture 14: orthogonal vectors and subspaces]{ref}

In an abstract <inner product space>, <orthogonality> always refers to its chosen <inner product>. For example, the <weighted inner product> $\langle u,v\rangle_w=4u_1v_1+u_2v_2$ makes $(1,1)$ and $(1,-4)$ <orthogonal>, since $4-4=0$. Their ordinary <dot product> is $-3$, so their arrows are not perpendicular in the usual <Cartesian coordinate system>. The algebraic definition also applies to <functions>, where arrows need not be available.

= Orthogonal basis
{parent=Orthogonal vectors}
{title2=$\langle u_i,u_j\rangle=0\quad(i\ne j)$}

An <orthogonal basis> of an inner-product <vector space> consists of nonzero mutually <orthogonal vectors> that span the space. The <orthogonal projection> of $v$ onto a finite-dimensional span is $\sum_i\langle v,u_i\rangle u_i/\langle u_i,u_i\rangle$. Dividing each vector by its norm produces an <orthonormal basis>. For centered random variables, the inner product is <covariance>; an orthogonal basis therefore consists of uncorrelated variables, without requiring unit variance.

= Nonorthogonal vectors
{parent=Inner product}

= Nonorthogonal
{synonym}

Two <vectors> are nonorthogonal when their <inner product> is nonzero. In the <power method>, the starting vector must be nonorthogonal to a dominant <eigenvector>.

= Gram-Schmidt process
{parent=Inner product}
{c}
{wiki=Gram–Schmidt_process}
{title2=$u_j=v_j-\sum_{i<j}\langle v_j,q_i\rangle q_i,\quad q_j=u_j/\|u_j\|$}

= Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization
{c}
{synonym}

The <Gram-Schmidt process> turns a finite ordered family of <linearly independent> <vectors> $v_1,\ldots,v_k$ in an <inner product space> over the <real numbers> into an <orthonormal basis> $q_1,\ldots,q_k$ of their <linear span>. It keeps every successive <linear span>, so the first $j$ new <vectors> describe exactly the same directions as the first $j$ inputs. Its idea is geometric: remove the <orthogonal projection> onto directions already found, then divide the remaining <vector> by its <norm>. This makes a complicated <basis> easier to use without changing the <vector subspace> it describes.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-06sc-linear-algebra-fall-2011/7ac32be444c25e48590f47573833ccc6_MIT18_06SCF11_Ses2.4sum.pdf[MIT 18.06SC, Orthogonal matrices and Gram-Schmidt, pp. 1–3]{ref}

Start with $u_1=v_1$ and $q_1=v_1/\|v_1\|$. At step $j$, use the previously obtained <orthonormal set> to define
$$
u_j=v_j-\sum_{i=1}^{j-1}\langle v_j,q_i\rangle q_i,
\qquad q_j=\frac{u_j}{\|u_j\|},
\qquad \|u_j\|=\sqrt{\langle u_j,u_j\rangle}.
$$
The sum is the <orthogonal projection onto a finite-dimensional subspace> $W_{j-1}=\operatorname{span}(q_1,\ldots,q_{j-1})$, and $u_j$ is the <orthogonal projection residual>. <Linear independence> ensures $u_j\ne0$: otherwise $v_j$ would already be a <linear combination> of earlier inputs. If the input family is <linearly dependent>, use only the retained <orthonormal> <vectors> in the sum and omit an input whenever its remainder is the <zero vector>; do not divide by zero. The retained outputs still form an <orthonormal basis> of the original <linear span>, but their number can be smaller than $k$.

For a short verification, <orthonormality> makes every earlier <inner product> vanish:
$$
\langle u_j,q_\ell\rangle
=\langle v_j,q_\ell\rangle-\sum_{i<j}\langle v_j,q_i\rangle\langle q_i,q_\ell\rangle=0
\quad(\ell<j).
$$
Also $u_j$ is $v_j$ minus a <linear combination> of earlier <vectors>, while $v_j$ is $u_j$ plus that same <linear combination>. These two inclusions, followed by division by a nonzero <norm>, give
$$
\operatorname{span}(q_1,\ldots,q_j)=\operatorname{span}(v_1,\ldots,v_j).
$$
The argument also works in an <inner product space> over the <complex numbers> with the existing <inner product> convention, linear in the first argument: the <coefficients> remain $\langle v_j,q_i\rangle$. With the convention linear in the second argument, write $\langle q_i,v_j\rangle$ instead.

For a first picture, take $v_1=(1,1)^T$ and $v_2=(1,0)^T$ in $\mathbb R^2$ with the ordinary <dot product>. Then
$$
q_1=\frac{(1,1)^T}{\sqrt2},\qquad
\langle v_2,q_1\rangle q_1=\frac12(1,1)^T,\qquad
u_2=\frac12(1,-1)^T,\qquad
q_2=\frac{(1,-1)^T}{\sqrt2}.
$$
Subtracting the diagonal component leaves an <orthogonal> direction; dividing by its <norm> $1/\sqrt2$ makes its length one. If the second input were $(2,2)^T$ instead, its <orthogonal projection> would be the entire input and $u_2=0$, revealing <linear dependence>.

\Image[gram-schmidt-vector-steps.png]
{title=<Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization> in $\mathbb R^2$: input <vectors>, subtraction of an <orthogonal projection>, and an <orthonormal basis>}

For a full three-dimensional calculation, use the ordinary <dot product> and the input <vectors>
$$
v_1=(1,1,0)^T,\qquad v_2=(1,0,1)^T,\qquad v_3=(0,1,1)^T.
$$
The first two steps give
$$
q_1=\frac{(1,1,0)^T}{\sqrt2},\qquad
\langle v_2,q_1\rangle=\frac1{\sqrt2},\qquad
u_2=(1/2,-1/2,1)^T,
$$
$$
\|u_2\|^2=\frac32,\qquad
q_2=\frac{(1,-1,2)^T}{\sqrt6}.
$$
At the third step, the <inner products> are $\langle v_3,q_1\rangle=1/\sqrt2$ and $\langle v_3,q_2\rangle=1/\sqrt6$. Consequently
$$
u_3=(0,1,1)^T-\frac12(1,1,0)^T-\frac16(1,-1,2)^T
=\left(-\frac23,\frac23,\frac23\right)^T,
$$
$$
\|u_3\|^2=\frac43,\qquad
q_3=\frac{(-1,1,1)^T}{\sqrt3}.
$$
The three numerator <vectors> have pairwise <dot products> $0$ and squared <Euclidean norms> $2,6,3$. Thus the displayed outputs are indeed an <orthonormal basis>, and the three nonzero remainders also confirm <linear independence> of the inputs. Keeping the removed <coefficients> produces the <QR decomposition> below.

An immediate reward is a reusable <orthogonal projection>: once an <orthonormal basis> of a <vector subspace> $W$ is known, any <vector> $b$ has
$$
P_Wb=\sum_i\langle b,q_i\rangle q_i.
$$
For the <linear span> of just the first two inputs in the three-dimensional example, $b=(0,0,1)^T$ gives $P_Wb=(1/3,-1/3,2/3)^T$. Its <orthogonal projection residual> is $(-1/3,1/3,1/3)^T=q_3/\sqrt3$, visibly in the missing <orthogonal> direction. This is the geometry behind the <linear least-squares problem>.

The formulas above describe exact arithmetic. With <floating-point arithmetic>, subtracting an <orthogonal projection> nearly equal to the input can leave a small remainder; dividing by its small <norm> can magnify existing <roundoff error>. The <classical Gram-Schmidt> and <modified Gram-Schmidt> arrangements agree in exact arithmetic but can give different rounded results. Exact <linear dependence> and a small computed remainder therefore require different judgments in a numerical implementation.https://nhigham.com/wp-content/uploads/2023/10/high95m.pdf[Higham, The Test Matrix Toolbox for Matlab, §8, p. 22]{ref}

= Classical Gram-Schmidt
{parent=Gram-Schmidt process}
{c}

<Classical Gram-Schmidt> computes all <orthogonal projection> <coefficients> $\langle v_j,q_i\rangle$ from the original input $v_j$, then subtracts their sum as in the displayed <Gram-Schmidt process> formula.

= Modified Gram-Schmidt
{parent=Gram-Schmidt process}
{c}
{title2=$w\leftarrow w-\langle w,q_i\rangle q_i$}

<Modified Gram-Schmidt> starts with $w=v_j$ and, for each retained <orthonormal> <vector> $q_i$ in order, replaces $w$ by $w-\langle w,q_i\rangle q_i$. Each <orthogonal projection> <coefficient> uses the current remainder. In exact arithmetic, subtracting earlier <orthogonal> directions does not change the <inner product> with $q_i$, so the final $w$ equals the <classical Gram-Schmidt> remainder $u_j$; then divide by $\|w\|$ if it is nonzero.

= QR decomposition
{title2=$A=QR$}
{c}
{parent=Gram-Schmidt process}
{wiki}

= QR factorization
{c}
{synonym}

A reduced <QR decomposition> of an $m\times k$ <matrix> over the <real numbers> with <full column rank>, $m\geq k$, writes $A=QR$, where $Q$ is $m\times k$ with <orthonormal> columns and $R$ is a $k\times k$ <upper triangular matrix> with positive diagonal. Applying the <Gram-Schmidt process> to the columns $v_j$ of $A$ constructs $Q=(q_1\ \cdots\ q_k)$ and records the <coefficients>
$$
r_{ij}=\begin{cases}
\langle v_j,q_i\rangle,&i<j,\\
\|u_j\|,&i=j,\\
0,&i>j.
\end{cases}
\qquad v_j=\sum_{i\leq j}r_{ij}q_i.
$$
This last column identity is exactly the <matrix multiplication> $A=QR$. The <upper triangular matrix> records that the $j$th input uses only the first $j$ new directions, while its positive diagonal records the <norms> of the nonzero <orthogonal projection residuals>. Since $Q^TQ=I$, one also has $R=Q^TA$; $Q^T$ denotes the <transpose> and $I$ the <identity matrix>. Over the <complex numbers>, use the <conjugate transpose> $Q^*$ in these identities.

For the three-dimensional <Gram-Schmidt process> example, the exact <matrices> are
$$
A=\begin{pmatrix}1&1&0\\1&0&1\\0&1&1\end{pmatrix},\qquad
Q=\begin{pmatrix}
1/\sqrt2&1/\sqrt6&-1/\sqrt3\\
1/\sqrt2&-1/\sqrt6&1/\sqrt3\\
0&2/\sqrt6&1/\sqrt3
\end{pmatrix},
$$
$$
R=\begin{pmatrix}
\sqrt2&1/\sqrt2&1/\sqrt2\\
0&\sqrt{3/2}&1/\sqrt6\\
0&0&2/\sqrt3
\end{pmatrix}.
$$
For example, the last column reconstructs as
$$
\frac{q_1}{\sqrt2}+\frac{q_2}{\sqrt6}+\frac{2q_3}{\sqrt3}
=\frac12(1,1,0)^T+\frac16(1,-1,2)^T+\frac23(-1,1,1)^T
=(0,1,1)^T.
$$
The diagonal entries are approximately $1.414214,1.224745,1.154701$. Each is a <norm> of the new direction before it was scaled to length one.

\Image[gram-schmidt-qr-factorization.png]
{title=The computed <QR factorization>: input <matrix> $A$, <orthonormal> columns of $Q$, and the <upper triangular matrix> $R$ of <orthogonal projection> <coefficients>}

For a <linear least-squares problem>, the <orthogonal projection> of $b$ onto the <column space> of $A$ is $QQ^Tb$. Hence the minimizing <coefficients> solve the <linear system> $Rx=Q^Tb$ by <back substitution>. Let $A_2=(v_1\ v_2)$ consist of the first two columns of the example; its reduced factors are $Q_2=(q_1\ q_2)$ and the leading $2\times2$ block $R_2$ of $R$. For $b=(0,0,1)^T$, the <linear system> $R_2x=Q_2^Tb$ is
$$
\begin{pmatrix}\sqrt2&1/\sqrt2\\0&\sqrt{3/2}\end{pmatrix}
\begin{pmatrix}x_1\\x_2\end{pmatrix}
=\begin{pmatrix}0\\2/\sqrt6\end{pmatrix},
\qquad x_2=\frac23,\quad x_1=-\frac13.
$$
Thus $A_2x=(1/3,-1/3,2/3)^T$, agreeing with the <orthogonal projection> computed above, and the minimum <Euclidean norm> of $A_2x-b$ is $1/\sqrt3$. The <QR decomposition> separates building an <orthonormal basis> from recovering <coefficients> in the original <basis>.https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-065-matrix-methods-in-data-analysis-signal-processing-and-machine-learning-spring-2018/resources/lecture-9-four-ways-to-solve-least-squares-problems/[Strang, MIT 18.065, Lecture 9: Four Ways to Solve Least Squares Problems]{ref}

= Positive-diagonal QR decomposition preserves a complex structure
{parent=QR decomposition}

Let $K$ be block diagonal with $2\times2$ blocks $J=\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}$. If a real invertible <matrix> $A$ commutes with $K$, its <QR decomposition> $A=BC$, with $B$ orthogonal and $C$ upper triangular with positive diagonal, has both $BK=KB$ and $CK=KC$. Indeed $CKC^{-1}=B^{-1}KB$, so $E=K^{-1}CKC^{-1}$ is orthogonal and upper triangular in $2\times2$ blocks. Orthogonality forces its off-diagonal blocks to vanish. Its diagonal blocks are $J^{-1}C_iJC_i^{-1}$. For $C_i=\begin{pmatrix}a&b\\0&d\end{pmatrix}$ with $a,d>0$, this block is $\begin{pmatrix}d/a&-b/a\\-b/a&(a^2+b^2)/(ad)\end{pmatrix}$. Orthogonality of its columns forces $b=0,d=a$, and hence each block is the identity. Thus $E=I$, proving the claim.

= Thin QR factorization from Cholesky decomposition
{parent=QR decomposition}
{title2=$A=QR,\quad Q^TQ=I,\quad R^TR=A^TA$}

If a real $m$-by-$n$ matrix $A$ has full column rank, with $m\ge n$, then $A^TA$ is <positive definite>. Its upper-triangular <Cholesky decomposition> $A^TA=R^TR$, with positive diagonal, is unique. Defining $Q=AR^{-1}$ gives $Q^TQ=I$ and $A=QR$. Any other such factorization yields another Cholesky factor of $A^TA$, so its $R$ and then its $Q$ must agree. This proves existence and uniqueness; it does not assert that forming $A^TA$ is the most numerically stable algorithm.

= Orthogonal coordinate reduction of a subspace
{parent=QR decomposition}

For a full-column-rank $n\times k$ matrix $V$, extend the thin <QR decomposition> $V=Q_1R_1$ to an <orthogonal matrix> $Q=(Q_1\ Q_2)$. Then
$$
Q^TV=\begin{pmatrix}R_1\\0\end{pmatrix},
$$
so $Q^T$ maps the column space of $V$ onto the first $k$ coordinate directions. Successive <Householder transformations> construct the same reduction without first forming a full orthogonal basis.

= Linear least-squares problem
{title2=$\min_x\|Ax-b\|_2$}
{parent=QR decomposition}

A linear least-squares problem minimizes $\|Ax-b\|_2$ over $x$. If $A=QR$ has full column rank, orthogonality reduces it to the upper-triangular system formed from the leading block of $R$ and the leading entries of $Q^Tb$.

= Gram-Schmidt orthogonalization for a symmetric bilinear form
{parent=Gram-Schmidt process}

For a symmetric bilinear form $B$, the same projection formula
$$
u_k=v_k-\sum_{j<k}\frac{B(v_k,u_j)}{B(u_j,u_j)}u_j
$$
produces a $B$-orthogonal basis whenever the chosen pivots $B(u_j,u_j)$ are nonzero. Reordering the input vectors can avoid a zero pivot when a suitable anisotropic vector remains.

= Quadratic form
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}
{title2=$Q(av)=a^2Q(v)$}

A <quadratic form> on a <vector space> over a <field> $F$ is a function $Q:V\to F$ satisfying $Q(av)=a^2Q(v)$ whose <polar bilinear form of a quadratic form> $B_Q(v,w)=Q(v+w)-Q(v)-Q(w)$ is a <bilinear form>. In finite <dimension>, it is represented in <coordinates> by a homogeneous degree-two <polynomial>, or by $Q(v)=v^TAv$ with a suitable <matrix> $A$.

When the <characteristic> is not two, one may choose $A$ to be a <symmetric matrix> and recover it from $B_Q/2$. In <characteristic> two, the symmetric-matrix restriction would lose examples: $Q(x,y)=xy$ has an upper-triangular representing <matrix> $\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\0&0\end{pmatrix}$, while $v^TAv$ for a <symmetric matrix> has no $xy$ cross term. Its polar form is $B_Q((x,y),(u,v))=xv+yu$, an <alternating bilinear form>. The distinction matters when studying <quadratic forms> over <finite fields>.<Bilinear Forms by Keith Conrad>[§§6–7: quadratic forms and characteristic two]{ref}

= Polar bilinear form of a quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}
{title2=$B_Q(x,y)=Q(x+y)-Q(x)-Q(y)$}

The displayed <bilinear form> is the polar form of a <quadratic form>. In odd <characteristic of a field>, $Q(x)=B_Q(x,x)/2$ recovers the <quadratic form>; in <characteristic of a field> two, different forms can have the same <alternating bilinear form>. Over $\mathbb F_2$, two such forms differ by a <linear map> to the <field>, giving the affine parametrization of <binary quadratic refinements>.

= Integral quadratic lattice
{parent=Quadratic form}
{title2=$L\cong\mathbb Z^n,\quad B:L\times L\to\mathbb Z$}

An integral quadratic lattice is a finite-rank <free abelian group> with a nondegenerate integral symmetric <bilinear form>. Its norm convention here is $q(x)=B(x,x)$. A basis gives an integral <Gram matrix>; basis changes preserve its determinant up to multiplication by the square of a unit.

= Even lattice
{parent=Integral quadratic lattice}
{title2=$B(x,x)\in2\mathbb Z\quad\text{for all }x\in L$}

Every vector in an even lattice has even integral norm. The <E8 lattice> and the hyperbolic plane appearing in the <K3 intersection lattice> are examples.

= Unimodular lattice
{parent=Integral quadratic lattice}
{title2=$|\det B|=1$}

The integral Gram determinant has absolute value one, equivalently the lattice equals its bilinear-form dual. The <K3 intersection lattice> is an indefinite example.

= Even unimodular lattice
{parent=Unimodular lattice}

An <integral quadratic lattice> that is both an <even lattice> and a <unimodular lattice>. The <K3 intersection lattice> has signature $(3,19)$, while the positive-definite <E8 lattice> has rank eight.

= E8 lattice
{parent=Even unimodular lattice}
{c}
{title2=$E_8$}

The positive-definite rank-eight <even unimodular lattice> can be realized as vectors in $\mathbb Z^8$ or $(\mathbb Z+1/2)^8$ whose coordinate sum is even, with the ordinary Euclidean inner product. Negating this form gives $E_8(-1)$ in the <K3 intersection lattice>.

= Off-diagonal all-ones quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}
{title2=$q(x)=(\sum_i x_i)^2-\sum_i x_i^2$}

Its symmetric matrix is $\mathbf1\mathbf1^T-I$. The line spanned by $\mathbf1$ has eigenvalue $n-1$ and its orthogonal complement has eigenvalue $-1$. Thus for $n>1$ it is nondegenerate with inertia $(1,n-1,0)$; for $n=1$ it vanishes. This gives a useful explicit example for <Sylvester's law of inertia>.

= Arf invariant of a quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}
{c}
{title2=$\operatorname{Arf}(Q)\in\mathbb F_2$}

For a nonsingular quadratic form over $\mathbb F_2$ with symplectic basis $(e_i,f_i)$, the invariant is $\sum_iQ(e_i)Q(f_i)$. For fixed polar form in dimension $2m$, the two types have $2^{m-1}(2^m+1)$ and $2^{m-1}(2^m-1)$ forms.

= Binary quadratic refinement
{parent=Arf invariant of a quadratic form}
{title2=$Q_v(x)=Q_0(x)+B(v,x)$}

A <binary quadratic refinement> of a fixed nonsingular <alternating bilinear form> $B$ has polar form $B$. The difference of two refinements is linear, so nondegeneracy gives the displayed unique parametrization by $v$. For $\dim V=2m$, refinements of plus and minus <Arf invariant of a quadratic form> have respectively $2^{m-1}(2^m+1)$ and $2^{m-1}(2^m-1)$ elements. Completing the square in the binary character sum gives $\epsilon(Q_v)=\epsilon(Q_0)(-1)^{Q_0(v)}$.

= Two-transitive binary quadratic-form actions
{parent=Binary quadratic refinement}
{title2=$Sp_{2m}(2)\curvearrowright Q^\pm(B)$}

The <symplectic group over a finite field> is transitive on each of the two quadratic types. The stabilizer of a refinement is its <orthogonal group over a finite field>. In the displayed parametrization, other refinements of the same type correspond to nonzero singular vectors, and refinements of opposite type to nonsingular vectors. <Witt's lemma> makes the orthogonal stabilizer transitive on each of these vector sets. Thus both type actions are <two-transitive> for $m\ge2$, and a minus stabilizer is transitive on the plus orbit. For $m=1$ the minus orbit is a singleton.

= Symmetric coefficients of a quadratic polynomial
{parent=Quadratic form}
{title2=$K^{ij}p_ip_j=K^{(ij)}p_ip_j$}

A quadratic <homogeneous polynomial> determines a unique symmetric bilinear coefficient tensor. An antisymmetric addition to its coefficient array gives zero contribution, so tensorial conditions on its coefficients must refer to this symmetric representative. In particular, the correspondence between <quadratic geodesic first integrals> and <rank-two Killing tensors> uses symmetric coefficients.

= Principal-axis reduction of a quadric
{parent=Quadratic form}
{title2=$x_0=-H^{-1}q$}

Write a real quadratic polynomial as $x^THx+2q^Tx+c$, with $H$ symmetric. If $H$ is invertible, translation by $x_0=-H^{-1}q$ removes the linear term. An orthonormal eigenbasis then makes the remaining <quadratic form> diagonal. Eigenvalue signs and the translated constant determine the real geometry, such as an ellipsoid or a <one-sheet hyperboloid>. A singular $H$ requires separate treatment of linear terms in its null directions; a center need not exist.

= Matrix congruence
{parent=Quadratic form}
{title2=$C=P^TAP$}

Two square <matrices> $A,C$ are congruent when $C=P^TAP$ for an <invertible matrix> $P$. This is the transformation of the <matrix of a bilinear form> under <change of basis>, and also transforms a <quadratic form> under an invertible <linear map> on its <coordinates>. The <matrices> need not be symmetric. It differs from <matrix similarity>, which uses $P^{-1}AP$ for a <linear operator>. For real <symmetric matrices>, <completing the square> diagonalizes the corresponding <quadratic form> by <matrix congruence>.

= Simultaneous congruence diagonalization with a positive form
{parent=Matrix congruence}
{title2=$P^TAP=I,\quad P^TBP=\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_j)$}

If $A$ is real symmetric positive definite and $B$ is real symmetric, let $S=A^{-1/2}$. The matrix $SBS$ is symmetric and has an orthonormal eigenbasis, represented by $Q$. Taking $P=SQ$ gives the two displayed congruences. The diagonal entries are determined by the <generalized eigenvalue problem> $\det(B-\lambda A)=0$, since $P^T(B-\lambda A)P$ is diagonal with entries $\lambda_j-\lambda$. Positive definiteness of the first form is essential to this construction.

= Positive-definite quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}

A real quadratic form $q$ is positive definite when $q(v)>0$ for every nonzero vector $v$. In a basis that diagonalizes it, this is equivalent to every diagonal coefficient being positive.

= Indefinite quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}
{title2=$q(u)>0,\quad q(v)<0$}

A real <quadratic form> is indefinite when it takes both positive and negative values. For a real <symmetric matrix> representing it, this means that its <eigenvalues> include both signs. For example, $q(x,y)=x^2-y^2$ has $q(1,0)=1$ and $q(0,1)=-1$; a zero value at the nonzero <vector> $(1,1)$ does not imply <positive semidefiniteness>.<Bilinear Forms by Keith Conrad>[Definition 6.21 and footnote 6, p. 40: definiteness and a nondegenerate indefinite example]{ref}

= Ternary quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}

A ternary quadratic form is a <quadratic form> in three variables. A diagonal integral example is $aX^2+bY^2+cZ^2$ with integer coefficients $a,b,c$.

= Isotropy of nondegenerate ternary quadratic forms over finite fields
{parent=Ternary quadratic form}

Over a <finite field> of odd cardinality $q$, a diagonal nondegenerate <ternary quadratic form> $A x^2+B y^2+C z^2$ has a zero with $z=1$: the sets $\{A x^2\}$ and $\{-C-B y^2\}$ both have $(q+1)/2$ elements and must intersect. This gives a nonzero nonsingular zero. Over a local integer ring with these coefficients units, the <Hensel lemma> lifts it to an isotropic vector in the fraction field.

= Local isotropy of the five seven thirteen form
{parent=Isotropy of nondegenerate ternary quadratic forms over finite fields}
{title2=$5x^2+7y^2+13z^2$}

This <ternary quadratic form> has a nonzero zero over $\mathbb Q_p$ exactly when $p\ne7$. Away from $2,5,7,13$, the finite-field square-set intersection proof supplies a nonsingular residue zero, and <Hensel lemma> lifts it. At 5 use $(0,1,1)$ modulo 5; at 13 use $(3,1,0)$. At 2 hold $x=2,z=1$ and apply the <strong form of Hensel lemma> to $7Y^2+33$ at $Y=1$: the function and derivative have <valuations> 3 and 1. At 7, the equation modulo 7 forces $x=z=0$ because 5 is not a square; reduction after dividing by 7 then forces $y=0$. Any normalized integral nonzero solution would therefore have all coordinates divisible by 7, a contradiction.

= Hyperbolic plane
{disambiguate=quadratic form}
{parent=Quadratic form}
{title2=$q(xe+yf)=xy$}

= Hyperbolic plane of a quadratic form
{synonym}

= Hyperbolic planes of a quadratic form
{synonym}

A hyperbolic plane is a two-dimensional <vector space> with a <quadratic form> and a <basis> $e,f$ satisfying $q(e)=q(f)=0$ and $B(e,f)=1$, where $B$ is the <polar bilinear form of a quadratic form>. Equivalently, $q(xe+yf)=xy$. This definition also applies in characteristic two. Over the <real numbers>, the identity $xy=((x+y)/2)^2-((x-y)/2)^2$ gives one positive and one negative square, hence signature zero. The signature statement is specific to real quadratic forms.

= Parallelogram law
{parent=Quadratic form}
{wiki}
{title2=$\|u+v\|^2+\|u-v\|^2=2\|u\|^2+2\|v\|^2$}

= Parallelogram identity
{synonym}

The parallelogram law for a quadratic form $Q$ is
$$
Q(x+y)+Q(x-y)=2Q(x)+2Q(y).
$$
Conversely, under the usual divisibility assumptions, a function satisfying this identity and the appropriate homogeneity comes from a symmetric bilinear form by polarization.

For an <inner product space>, taking $Q(v)=\|v\|^2$ gives the displayed squared-norm identity by expanding and cancelling the mixed <inner products>. It is the geometric ingredient that makes minimizing sequences converge in the <Hilbert projection theorem>.

= Rank of a quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}

The rank of a quadratic form is the rank of its associated symmetric bilinear form.

= Rank-one quadratic form
{parent=Rank of a quadratic form}

= Rank
{synonym}

A rank-one quadratic form is the square of a <linear form> up to a scalar. More generally, a bilinear expression $(u^Tx)(v^Ty)$ is rank one because its coefficient matrix is the <outer product> $uv^T$.

= Signature of a quadratic form
{title2=$\sigma$}
{parent=Quadratic form}

= Signature
{synonym}

The signature of a real quadratic form is the number of positive squares minus the number of negative squares in its diagonal normal form.

= Polarization identity
{parent=Quadratic form}
{wiki}

Over a field of characteristic other than two, a quadratic form recovers its symmetric bilinear form by
$$B(u,v)=\frac12\bigl(Q(u+v)-Q(u)-Q(v)\bigr).$$

= Polarization argument for a vanishing quadratic form
{parent=Polarization identity}

For a complex sesquilinear form $B$, the values $B(x,x)$ determine $B(x,y)$. In particular, expanding at $x+y$ and $x+iy$ shows that $B(x,x)=0$ for every $x$ implies $B=0$. Over the reals this conclusion can fail for nonsymmetric bilinear forms, as a nonzero skew-symmetric form has zero diagonal.

= Positive semidefinite bilinear form
{parent=Quadratic form}

A symmetric bilinear form is positive semidefinite when $B(v,v)\geq0$ for every $v$, and positive definite when equality is possible only for $v=0$.

= Definite matrix
{parent=Quadratic form}
{wiki}

A definite matrix is a Hermitian matrix whose associated quadratic form has a fixed sign. Positive and negative semidefinite matrices allow zero as well.

= Positive semidefinite matrix
{parent=Definite matrix}

= Positive semidefiniteness
{synonym}

= Positive semidefinite
{synonym}

A real symmetric or complex Hermitian matrix $A$ is positive semidefinite when $x^\dagger Ax\geq0$ for every <vector> $x$, equivalently when all its <eigenvalue>[eigenvalues] are nonnegative.

= Bipartite block-constant positive semidefinite matrix
{parent=Positive semidefinite matrix}

For positive block sizes, the real <matrix> $\begin{pmatrix}aJ&bJ\\bJ&aJ\end{pmatrix}$ is a <positive semidefinite matrix> exactly when $a\geq|b|$. For test-vector block sums $s,t$, its <quadratic form> is $(a+b)(s+t)^2/2+(a-b)(s-t)^2/2$. This proves sufficiency; testing $s=\pm t$ proves necessity. Zero sums in both blocks describe part of its <kernel>.

= Completely positive matrix
{parent=Positive semidefinite matrix}
{title2=$B=\sum_jx_jx_j^T,\ x_j\geq0$}

A real <symmetric matrix> admitting a finite sum of nonnegative <rank-one matrices> $x_jx_j^T$. It is both a <positive semidefinite matrix> and a <nonnegative matrix>, but these two properties alone need not imply membership in the <completely positive cone> in arbitrary <dimension>. This <matrix> notion is distinct from a <completely positive map>.

= Zero quadratic form of a positive semidefinite matrix
{parent=Positive semidefinite matrix}

If $P$ is a real <positive semidefinite matrix>, then $x^TPx=0$ if and only if $Px=0$. Indeed $x^TPx=\|\sqrt P\,x\|^2$ by the <principal square root of a positive semidefinite matrix>, so a zero <quadratic form> puts $x$ in the <kernel> of $\sqrt P$ and hence of $P$.

= Positive semidefinite trace nonnegativity
{parent=Positive semidefinite matrix}

For real <positive semidefinite matrices> $A,B$, their product need not be symmetric, but its <matrix trace> is nonnegative:
$$
\operatorname{tr}(AB)=\operatorname{tr}(\sqrt A\,B\sqrt A)\geq0.
$$
The equality uses the cyclic property of the <matrix trace>, and the last <matrix> is a <positive semidefinite matrix>. Consequently the <Loewner order> inequality $A\preceq C$ implies $\operatorname{tr}(AB)\leq\operatorname{tr}(CB)$ whenever $B\succeq0$.

= Loewner order
{title2=$\preceq$}
{c}
{parent=Positive semidefinite matrix}
{wiki}

The Loewner order on <Hermitian matrices> is defined by $A\preceq B$ when $B-A$ is a <positive semidefinite matrix>.

= Square root of a matrix
{title2=$B^2=A$}
{parent=Positive semidefinite matrix}
{wiki}

= Square roots of a matrix
{synonym}

A square root of a square matrix $A$ is a matrix $B$ satisfying $B^2=A$.

= Principal square root of a positive semidefinite matrix
{title2=$\sqrt A$}
{parent=Square root of a matrix}

If $A=Q\operatorname{diag}(\lambda_1,\ldots,\lambda_n)Q^T$ is real symmetric and positive semidefinite, its principal square root is
$$
\sqrt A=Q\operatorname{diag}(\sqrt{\lambda_1},\ldots,\sqrt{\lambda_n})Q^T.
$$
It is symmetric and positive semidefinite, and is positive definite exactly when $A$ is.

= Polar decomposition of an invertible real matrix
{parent=Principal square root of a positive semidefinite matrix}

Every invertible real matrix has the polar decomposition
$$
M=RP,
\qquad
P=\sqrt{M^TM},
\qquad
R=MP^{-1},
$$
where $P$ is symmetric positive definite and $R$ is orthogonal.

This is the invertible real-matrix case of <polar decomposition>.

= Polar decomposition of an invertible complex matrix
{parent=Polar decomposition of an invertible real matrix}

Every invertible complex matrix has the unique polar decomposition
$$
M=PU,
\qquad
P=\sqrt{MM^*},
\qquad
U=P^{-1}M,
$$
where $P$ is a positive-definite <Hermitian matrix> and $U$ is a <unitary matrix>. Writing $P=e^B$ gives $B=\frac12\log(MM^*)$, which is Hermitian.

= Positive-definite matrix
{parent=Definite matrix}

A real symmetric matrix $A$ is positive definite when
$$
x^TAx>0
$$
for every nonzero real <vector> $x$. It therefore defines the <inner product> $\langle x,y\rangle_A=x^TAy$ and is invertible.

= Cholesky decomposition
{c}
{parent=Positive-definite matrix}
{wiki}

Every real symmetric <positive-definite matrix> has a unique factorization $A=LL^T$ in which $L$ is a lower <triangular matrix> with positive diagonal entries.

= Rank-one Cholesky update
{c}
{parent=Cholesky decomposition}

Given a <Cholesky decomposition> $A=LL^T$, a rank-one Cholesky update computes a triangular factor of $A+xx^T$ in $O(n^2)$ operations without refactorizing the matrix from scratch.

= Sylvester's criterion
{c}
{parent=Positive-definite matrix}
{wiki}

A Hermitian matrix is positive definite exactly when all its leading principal minors are positive.

= Condition number
{title2=$\kappa$}
{parent=Positive-definite matrix}
{wiki}

The condition number of a problem measures how strongly relative perturbations of its input can amplify relative changes in its output. For an invertible matrix and a chosen operator norm, $\kappa(A)=\lVert A\rVert\lVert A^{-1}\rVert$.

= Spectral condition number of a positive-definite matrix
{title2=$\kappa_2(A)$}
{parent=Condition number}

For a real symmetric positive-definite matrix,
$$
\kappa_2(A)=\frac{\lambda_{\max}(A)}{\lambda_{\min}(A)}.
$$
It measures the sensitivity of the linear system and controls convergence bounds for iterative solvers.

= Hermitian positive-definite matrix
{parent=Positive-definite matrix}

A complex matrix $A$ is Hermitian positive definite when $A^*=A$ and $z^*Az>0$ for every nonzero complex vector $z$.

= Toeplitz matrix
{parent=Hermitian positive-definite matrix}
{c}
{wiki}

A Toeplitz matrix is constant along each diagonal, so its entries have the form $T_{nm}=t_{n-m}$.

= Symmetric tridiagonal Toeplitz matrix
{parent=Toeplitz matrix}

An $m\times m$ symmetric tridiagonal Toeplitz matrix has one constant diagonal $\alpha$ and equal constant subdiagonal and superdiagonal $\beta$. Its eigenvectors are the <discrete sine transform> vectors $q^{(k)}_j=\sin(jk\pi/(m+1))$, with eigenvalues $\alpha+2\beta\cos(k\pi/(m+1))$.

= Toeplitz antisymmetric tridiagonal matrix
{c}
{parent=Toeplitz matrix}

An $M\times M$ Toeplitz antisymmetric tridiagonal matrix with diagonal $a$, superdiagonal $b$, and subdiagonal $-b$ has eigenvalues
$$
\lambda_j=a+2ib\cos\frac{j\pi}{M+1},
\qquad 1\leq j\leq M.
$$
All such matrices share an orthonormal eigenbasis after the same diagonal unitary change from the discrete sine basis.

= Negative-definite matrix
{parent=Definite matrix}

= Negative definite
{synonym}

A real symmetric matrix $A$ is negative definite when $x^TAx<0$ for every nonzero real <vector> $x$. Equivalently, $-A$ is a <positive-definite matrix> and every <eigenvalue> of $A$ is negative.

= Sylvester's law of inertia
{parent=Quadratic form}
{c}
{wiki}

Every real quadratic form is congruent to a diagonal form with entries $+1,-1,0$, and the numbers of entries of each kind are invariant.

= Criterion for membership in a diagonal basis
{parent=Sylvester's law of inertia}

A vector $v$ can be included in a diagonal <basis> for a real <quadratic form> precisely when $v\ne0$ and either its quadratic value is nonzero or it lies in the <radical of a bilinear form>. A nonzero quadratic value gives the direct splitting $\mathbb Rv\oplus v^\perp$ and induction. A nonzero radical vector can be included in a radical basis. Conversely, an <isotropic vector> in a diagonal basis is orthogonal to every basis vector, and must lie in the radical.

= Isotropic quadratic form
{parent=Quadratic form}
{wiki}

An isotropic quadratic form has a nonzero vector on which it vanishes.

= Hasse-Minkowski theorem
{c}
{parent=Isotropic quadratic form}
{wiki=Hasse–Minkowski_theorem}

= Hasse-Minkowski principle
{c}
{synonym}

A nondegenerate <quadratic form> over a <number field> is isotropic if and only if it is isotropic over every completion. Over $\mathbb Q$, these are the real field and all <p-adic numbers>. It turns a rational existence problem into local tests; denominators can then be cleared for an integral homogeneous equation.

= Hasse invariant of a quadratic form
{c}
{parent=Hasse-Minkowski theorem}
{title2=$\epsilon_v(q)=\prod_{i<j}(a_i,a_j)_v$}

For a nondegenerate diagonal <quadratic form> $q=\langle a_1,\ldots,a_m\rangle$, this product of <quadratic Hilbert symbols> is independent of diagonalization. Over a <p-adic field>, dimension, determinant square class and this invariant classify the form up to isometry. The product of the local invariants of a globally diagonalized form is one by the <Hilbert reciprocity law>; real places additionally record signature.

= Totally isotropic subspace
{parent=Isotropic quadratic form}

A subspace $E$ is totally isotropic for $B$ when $B(u,v)=0$ for all $u,v\in E$. For a real <nondegenerate bilinear form> of inertia $(p,q)$, its dimension is at most $\min(p,q)$.

= Isotropic dimension bound from real inertia
{parent=Totally isotropic subspace}
{title2=$\dim U\leq\min(p,q)$}

For a nondegenerate real <quadratic form> of inertia $(p,q)$, projection of a totally isotropic subspace onto either its positive or negative coordinate subspace is injective: a vector with one projection zero would have strictly one-signed quadratic value unless it vanished. Thus its dimension is at most both $p$ and $q$. This bound is attained by pairing one positive and one negative coordinate for each of $\min(p,q)$ independent null vectors.

= Complex quadratic forms have a large isotropic subspace
{parent=Totally isotropic subspace}
{title2=$\dim E=n-\lceil r/2\rceil$}

A <quadratic form> of rank $r$ on $\mathbb C^n$ is congruent to $x_1^2+\cdots+x_r^2$. Pair its nonzero coordinates into vectors $e_{2j-1}+ie_{2j}$, and include the <radical of a bilinear form>. Their span is a <totally isotropic subspace> and has dimension $\lfloor r/2\rfloor+n-r=n-\lceil r/2\rceil\ge\lfloor n/2\rfloor$. The form vanishes on every vector of this subspace, not merely on its basis vectors.

= Common zero of complex quadratic forms
{parent=Complex quadratic forms have a large isotropic subspace}
{title2=$n\ge2^d$}

= Common zeros of complex quadratic forms
{synonym}

Any $d$ <quadratic forms> on $\mathbb C^n$ have a common nonzero zero when $n\ge2^d$. Choose a <totally isotropic subspace> for the first form, of dimension at least $\lfloor n/2\rfloor$. Restrict the remaining forms to it and repeat by induction. After $d$ stages at least one dimension remains. This elementary sufficient bound does not assert that $2^d$ is the best possible threshold.

= Maximum dimension of a totally isotropic subspace
{parent=Totally isotropic subspace}
{title2=$n-(r+|s|)/2$}

For a real <quadratic form> on an $n$-dimensional <vector space>, with rank $r$ and signature $s$ defined as positive minus negative index, the maximum dimension of a <totally isotropic subspace> is $n-(r+|s|)/2$. Quotienting by the <radical of a bilinear form> leaves a nondegenerate form of indices $p,q$. Projection of an isotropic subspace to each sign-coordinate space is injective, bounding its dimension by $\min(p,q)$. The radical and vectors pairing one positive with one negative unit coordinate attain the bound $n-r+\min(p,q)$.

= Isotropic vector
{parent=Isotropic quadratic form}

A vector $v$ is isotropic for a quadratic form $q$ when $q(v)=0$.

= Quadratic form gradient
{parent=Quadratic form}

For symmetric $A$, the quadratic form $x^TAx$ has gradient $2Ax$.

= Schur product theorem
{parent=Linear algebra}
{c}
{wiki}

The entrywise product of two positive semidefinite matrices is positive semidefinite. Writing each matrix as a sum of rank-one Gram matrices reduces the claim to $(uu^T)\circ(vv^T)=(u\circ v)(u\circ v)^T$.

= Coefficient-dominated entrywise positivity
{parent=Schur product theorem}

Let real <power series> $f(t)=\sum_{k\geq0}f_kt^k$ and $g(t)=\sum_{k\geq0}g_kt^k$ converge on $[-1,1]$, with $f_k\geq|g_k|$ for every index including zero. If $X\succeq0$ has entries there, applying $f$ within two diagonal blocks and $g$ across them preserves <positive semidefiniteness>. Indeed the <coefficient> <matrix> $H_k$ is a <bipartite block-constant positive semidefinite matrix>. Each $H_k\circ X^{\circ k}$ is <positive semidefinite> by the <Schur product theorem>, and their sum converges to the transformed <matrix>. Closedness of the <positive semidefinite cone> completes the proof. Convergence at one gives $\sum f_k<\infty$, so <coefficient> domination ensures <absolute convergence> of both series throughout the interval. Without the zero-index condition, $f=-1$, $g=0$ and $X=0$ give a counterexample.

= Orthogonal group
{title2=$O(n)$}
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

The real orthogonal group is $O(n)=\{R:R^TR=I\}$. Its tangent space at the identity is the vector space of skew-symmetric matrices.

= Orthogonal group as a regular level set
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{title2=$\dim_{\mathbb R}O(n)=n(n-1)/2$}

The smooth Gram map $F(A)=A^TA$ takes real matrices to symmetric matrices. At an orthogonal $A$, its derivative sends $AH/2$ to any prescribed symmetric matrix $H$. The regular level set $F^{-1}(I)$ is therefore a <smooth manifold> of the displayed dimension.

// Target: geometry-and-topology.bigb

= Cartan–Dieudonné theorem
{c}
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{wiki}

Every <orthogonal transformation> of a finite-dimensional nondegenerate quadratic space over a field of characteristic different from two is a product of at most its <dimension> many hyperplane reflections. In the real positive-definite case, a reflection normal to $gv-v$ sends $gv$ to $v$ whenever $gv\ne v$. Composing with that reflection fixes $v$ and leaves an <orthogonal transformation> of $v^\perp$, so induction proves the bound. Each reflection has <determinant> minus one, hence orientation-preserving transformations use an <even number> of reflections.

= Determinant-twist projection in odd dimension
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{title2=$\pi(A)=(\det A)A$}

For real <orthogonal matrices> in odd dimension $d$, multiplying by their <determinant> defines a <group homomorphism> from $O(d)$ onto $SO(d)$. Scalar factors commute, so $\pi(AB)=\pi(A)\pi(B)$, while $\det\pi(A)=(\det A)^{d+1}=1$. The map fixes $SO(d)$ and has <kernel> $\{I,-I\}$. Since $-I$ is central and has determinant $-1$ in odd dimension, $O(d)\cong SO(d)\times C_2$. In even dimension the same expression does not map every orthogonal matrix into the <special orthogonal group>.

= Odd-dimensional orthogonal determinant splitting
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{title2=$O(2m+1)\cong SO(2m+1)\times C_2$}

In odd dimension, the map $A\mapsto(\det A)A$ is a surjective <group homomorphism> from the <orthogonal group> to the <special orthogonal group>, with <kernel of a group homomorphism> $\{I,-I\}$. Scalar matrices commute, and $\det[(\det A)A]=(\det A)^{2m+2}=1$. Together with the <determinant> sign, it identifies $O(2m+1)$ with the <direct product of groups> $SO(2m+1)\times C_2$. In even dimension, scalar multiplication by $\det A$ does not repair the determinant, so this construction does not give the same splitting.

= Orthogonal matrix
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{wiki}

A real square matrix is orthogonal when $R^TR=I$, equivalently when its columns form an orthonormal basis.

= Orthogonal transformation
{parent=Orthogonal matrix}
{wiki}

An orthogonal transformation of a real inner-product space is a linear map $Q$ satisfying $Q^TQ=I$. It preserves inner products, lengths, angles, and therefore <Gaussian curvature> when applied to an embedded surface in Euclidean space.

= Improper orthogonal transformation
{parent=Orthogonal group}

An improper orthogonal transformation has determinant $-1$. In three dimensions it may be a plane reflection or a rotation composed with a reflection perpendicular to the rotation axis.

= Three-dimensional improper orthogonal transformation
{parent=Improper orthogonal transformation}

A real <orthogonal matrix> $T$ of size three with $\det T=-1$ has an <eigenvector> $n$ of <eigenvalue> $-1$. Its <orthogonal complement> is invariant, and the restriction there is an orientation-preserving plane rotation. Thus $T$ is a rotation about the axis $\mathbb Rn$ composed with a <reflection (mathematics)> across the plane perpendicular to $n$. Writing the plane angle as $\phi$, $\operatorname{tr}T=-1+2\cos\phi$. A pure plane <reflection (mathematics)> is the case $\phi=0$; if $\det(T-I)\ne0$, it cannot be a pure plane <reflection (mathematics)>.

= Special orthogonal group
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{wiki}

The special orthogonal group is
$$
SO(n)=\{A\in M_n(\mathbb R):A^TA=I,\ \det A=1\}.
$$
Every element of $SO(2)$ is a rotation in the plane. Every element of $SO(3)$ fixes an axis and restricts to a planar rotation on its perpendicular plane.

= Trace height on the special orthogonal group
{parent=Special orthogonal group}
{title2=$f(X)=\operatorname{tr}(CX)$}

For positive distinct diagonal entries $c_1<\cdots<c_n$, the <critical points> of this function are diagonal sign <matrices> $D=\operatorname{diag}(\varepsilon_1,\ldots,\varepsilon_n)$ with $\prod\varepsilon_i=1$. Criticality forces $CX$ symmetric; its square is $C^2$, so it commutes with the distinct-spectrum diagonal <matrix> $C^2$ and is diagonal. In the skew coordinates $a_{ij}$ its <Hessian> is $-\sum_{i<j}(c_i\varepsilon_i+c_j\varepsilon_j)a_{ij}^2$. The sign of each parenthesis is that of $\varepsilon_j$, giving <Morse index> $\sum_{j:\varepsilon_j=1}(j-1)$.

= Low homotopy groups of special orthogonal groups
{parent=Special orthogonal group}
{title2=$\pi_1SO(2)=\mathbb Z,\quad\pi_1SO(n)=\mathbb Z/2\ (n\geq3)$}

All the <special orthogonal groups> are connected, and their second <homotopy groups> vanish. The rotation circle gives $SO(2)\cong S^1$. Conjugation by <unit quaternions> gives the twofold <universal cover> $S^3\to SO(3)$, so $\pi_1SO(3)=\mathbb Z/2$ and $\pi_3SO(3)=\mathbb Z$. The <special orthogonal sphere fibration> $SO(n-1)\to SO(n)\to S^{n-1}$ propagates the first two <homotopy groups> for $n\geq4$. In dimension four its <exact sequence> has a zero map from the <torsion group> $\pi_4S^3=\mathbb Z/2$ to $\pi_3SO(3)=\mathbb Z$, giving $\pi_3SO(4)=\mathbb Z^2$. For $SO(1)$ all positive groups vanish, and $\pi_3SO(2)=0$.

= Special orthogonal sphere fibration
{parent=Special orthogonal group}
{title2=$SO(s)\to SO(s+1)\to S^s$}

The last-column map $p(A)=Ae_{s+1}$ has fiber $SO(s)$, included as $\operatorname{diag}(B,1)$. Local completion of a unit vector by the <Gram-Schmidt process> gives local <fiber bundle> charts. Consequently it has the <homotopy lifting property> and the <long exact sequence of homotopy groups of a fibration>.

= Surjectivity of special orthogonal stabilization below the sphere dimension
{parent=Special orthogonal sphere fibration}
{title2=$\pi_r(SO(s))\twoheadrightarrow\pi_r(SO(s+1))\quad(r<s)$}

Project a based map $\alpha:S^r\to SO(s+1)$ to $S^s$. Since $r<s$, the resulting <homotopy class> vanishes. Lift a based null-homotopy by the <special orthogonal sphere fibration>. Its endpoint lies in the fiber $SO(s)$. If the lift moves its base point, right-multiply at time $t$ by the inverse of its base-point value, which lies in the fiber. This keeps the projected homotopy unchanged and makes the lift based. Thus $\alpha$ is based-homotopic to a map into $SO(s)$, proving the asserted surjectivity.

= SO(4) group
{c}
{parent=Special orthogonal group}
{title2=$SO(4)$}

= SO(4)
{c}
{synonym}

The <SO(4) group> consists of real orthogonal four-by-four matrices of determinant one. It preserves orientation and the Euclidean metric. Its <Spin(4) double cover> identifies it with the quotient of two copies of <SU(2)> by their simultaneous central sign.

= Representations of SO(4) from two SU2 spins
{parent=SO(4) group}
{title2=$(j_L,j_R),\quad j_L+j_R\in\mathbb Z$}

Tensor products $V_{j_L}\otimes V_{j_R}$ give the <irreducible representations> of the <Spin(4) double cover>. The simultaneous central sign acts by $(-1)^{2j_L+2j_R}$, so exactly the integer-sum pairs descend to <SO(4)>. Their dimensions are $(2j_L+1)(2j_R+1)$. Under the diagonal spatial rotation subgroup, the <Clebsch-Gordan decomposition for SU2> gives spins from $|j_L-j_R|$ through $j_L+j_R$ in steps of one.

= Rotations preserving an axis
{parent=Special orthogonal group}
{title2=$SO(2)\rtimes C_2\cong O(2)$}

The subgroup of three-dimensional proper rotations preserving a chosen unoriented axis consists of all rotations about it and all half-turns about perpendicular axes. It is isomorphic to $O(2)$; the continuous molecular point-group notation is $D_\infty$. This uncountable group is different from the abstract countable infinite dihedral group $\mathbb Z\rtimes C_2$.

= SO(3) group
{parent=Special orthogonal group}
{c}
{title2=$SO(3)$}

= SO(3)
{c}
{synonym}

The SO(3) group consists of real three-by-three <orthogonal matrices> with <determinant> one. It acts by <rotation in three dimensions>. Its <group manifold> is $\mathbb{RP}^3$, since the <adjoint double cover from SU(2) to SO(3)> identifies antipodal points of the <three-sphere>.

= SO(3) as real projective three-space
{parent=SO(3) group}
{c}
{title2=$SO(3)\cong\mathbb{RP}^3$}

= SO(3) group manifold
{c}
{synonym}

The <adjoint double cover from SU(2) to SO(3)> has kernel $\{I,-I\}$. Under <SU(2) as the three-sphere>, multiplication by $-I$ is the antipodal map, so the quotient is <real projective space> $\mathbb{RP}^3$. Equivalently, an axis-angle ball of radius $\pi$ has opposite boundary points identified. The <fundamental group> is $\mathbb Z_2$.

= Odd-dimensional special orthogonal transformation has a fixed vector
{parent=Special orthogonal group}

Every real orthogonal transformation of odd dimension with determinant one has eigenvalue $+1$. Nonreal eigenvalues come in conjugate pairs with product one, while real eigenvalues are $+1$ or $-1$. There are an odd number of real eigenvalues and an even number of negative ones, leaving at least one positive eigenvalue. The corresponding eigenvector is fixed.

= Special orthogonal group as a submanifold
{title2=$T_RSO(n)=R\mathfrak{so}(n)$}
{parent=Special orthogonal group}

The map $A\mapsto A^TA$ from real matrices to symmetric matrices has derivative $H\mapsto R^TH+H^TR$ at $R\in O(n)$, which is surjective. Thus $SO(n)$ is a submanifold of dimension $n(n-1)/2$, and
$$
T_RSO(n)=\{H:R^TH+H^TR=0\}
=\{RA:A^T=-A\}.
$$

= Unit-vector stabilizer in a special orthogonal group
{title2=$S_v$}
{parent=Special orthogonal group}

For $v\in S^n$, the stabilizer $S_v=\{R\in SO(n+1):Rv=v\}$ is conjugate to the block subgroup $SO(n)$ acting on $v^\perp$. Hence it is a submanifold of dimension $n(n-1)/2$, with
$$
T_RS_v=\{RA:A^T=-A,\ Av=0\}.
$$

= Distinct unit-vector stabilizers are not transverse
{parent=Unit-vector stabilizer in a special orthogonal group}

For distinct $v,w\in S^n$, the submanifolds $S_v,S_w\subseteq SO(n+1)$ are not transverse. They both contain the identity. If $v,w$ are independent, the bivector $v\wedge w$ is a nonzero normal direction common to both tangent spaces there; if $w=-v$, the two stabilizers coincide.

= Rotation in three dimensions
{parent=Special orthogonal group}

= Rotations in three dimensions
{synonym}

A rotation in three dimensions is an element of $SO(3)$. It fixes an axis and acts as a two-dimensional rotation on the plane perpendicular to that axis.

= Skew-symmetric matrix
{parent=Orthogonal group}
{wiki}

= Skew-symmetric
{synonym}

= Antisymmetric matrix
{synonym}

A matrix is skew-symmetric when $A^T=-A$.

= Unitary skew-diagonalization of an antisymmetric matrix
{parent=Skew-symmetric matrix}
{title2=$U^TZU=\bigoplus_r z_r\begin{pmatrix}0&1\\-1&0\end{pmatrix}$}

A complex <matrix> $Z=-Z^T$ can be put by unitary congruence into two-by-two skew blocks and zero blocks; the magnitudes $|z_r|$ are its <singular values>, each repeated twice. One proof uses the <antilinear map> $A(v)=Z\bar v$. Because $Z^\dagger=-\bar Z$, $A^2=-ZZ^\dagger$. On a nonzero eigenspace of $ZZ^\dagger$ with <eigenvalue> $s^2$, any unit vector $v$ has orthogonal partner $A(v)/s$, and $A(A(v)/s)=-sv$. These orthonormal pairs give the blocks; the orthogonal complement is invariant, so the procedure repeats. Phases of the paired vectors set the block phases.

= Cross-product matrix
{parent=Skew-symmetric matrix}

For $a=(a_1,a_2,a_3)^T$, the map $b\mapsto a\times b$ has matrix
$$
[a]_\times=
\begin{pmatrix}
0&-a_3&a_2\\
a_3&0&-a_1\\
-a_2&a_1&0
\end{pmatrix}.
$$
It has trace zero and annihilates $a$, so its determinant is zero.

= Cross-product matrix spectrum
{parent=Cross-product matrix}
{title2=$\operatorname{spec}([n]_\times)=\{0,i|n|,-i|n|\}$}

The <cross-product matrix> $A\mathbf x=\mathbf n\times\mathbf x$ obeys $A\mathbf n=0$ and $A^2=\mathbf n\mathbf n^T-|\mathbf n|^2I$, by the <vector triple product>. On the <orthogonal complement> of a nonzero $\mathbf n$, this is $-|\mathbf n|^2I$; hence the two remaining complex <eigenvalues> are $\pm i|\mathbf n|$. In an orthonormal basis adapted to $\mathbf n$, $A$ is a planar quarter-turn multiplied by $|\mathbf n|$, with zero action along its axis.

= Tensor product of linear maps
{parent=Linear algebra}

The <tensor product> of linear maps is determined by $(A\otimes B)(x\otimes y)=Ax\otimes By$ and linear extension.

= Tensor-product operator
{parent=Tensor product of linear maps}

A tensor-product operator factors as $A\otimes B$ on a <tensor product> of two spaces. If both factors are <positive semidefinite operators>, their tensor-product operator is positive: a <tensor-product basis> of eigenvectors has eigenvalues $a_ib_j\geq0$. This positivity underlies the necessary direction of the <positive partial transpose criterion>.

= Trace-duality bound
{parent=Linear algebra}

For $M\succeq0$ with $\operatorname{tr}M\leq s$, $\operatorname{tr}(MA)\leq s\max(\lambda_{\max}(A),0)\leq s\lVert A\rVert_{\rm op}$.

= Diagonal matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

A diagonal matrix has zero entries away from its main diagonal. Its eigenvalues are its diagonal entries.

= Scalar matrix
{parent=Diagonal matrix}
{title2=$\lambda I$}

A <scalar matrix> is a scalar multiple of the identity <matrix>, equivalently a <diagonal matrix> with all diagonal entries equal. It commutes with every square <matrix> of the same size. In $GL_n(F)$, the nonzero <scalar matrices> form the central <subgroup> removed to define the <projective linear group>.

= Triangular matrix
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki}

An upper or lower triangular matrix has all entries on one side of the main diagonal equal to zero. Its eigenvalues, with algebraic multiplicity, are its diagonal entries.

= Back substitution
{parent=Triangular matrix}
{title2=$x_i=(b_i-\sum_{j>i}a_{ij}x_j)/a_{ii}$}

An upper <triangular matrix> system with nonzero diagonal is solved from the last row upward. Once $x_{i+1},\ldots,x_n$ are known, use $x_i=(b_i-\sum_{j>i}a_{ij}x_j)/a_{ii}$. The nonzero diagonal guarantees a unique solution; this is the final solve after a <QR factorization>.

= Upper triangular matrix
{parent=Triangular matrix}

An upper triangular matrix has zero entries below its main diagonal. Its <determinant> is the product of its diagonal entries, and an invertible upper triangular linear system is solved by backward substitution.

= Strictly upper triangular matrix
{parent=Upper triangular matrix}
{title2=$a_{ij}=0\quad(i\ge j)$}

A <square matrix> is strictly upper triangular if every entry on or below its diagonal is zero. A product of $n$ such $n$-by-$n$ matrices vanishes, so they form a <nilpotent Lie algebra> under the <commutator>. Multiplication by an <upper triangular matrix> preserves strict upper triangularity and zero <trace>.

= Lower triangular matrix
{parent=Triangular matrix}

A lower triangular matrix has zero entries above its main diagonal. Its <determinant> is the product of its diagonal entries, and an invertible lower triangular linear system is solved by forward substitution.

= Lower triangular matrix algebra
{title2=$T_n(k)$}
{parent=Lower triangular matrix}

The algebra $T_n(k)$ consists of the lower triangular $n$ by $n$ matrices over $k$. Its <Jacobson radical> $N$ consists of the strictly lower triangular matrices, and
$$
N^i=\operatorname{span}\{e_{rs}:r-s\geq i\}.
$$
The $n$ simple left modules $S_r$ are one-dimensional, with a matrix acting through its $r$th diagonal entry. For the left regular module,
$$
N^i/N^{i+1}\cong S_{i+1}\oplus\cdots\oplus S_n.
$$

= Triangular linear system
{parent=Triangular matrix}

A triangular linear system is solved by forward substitution for a lower triangular coefficient matrix or backward substitution for an upper triangular coefficient matrix, using $O(n^2)$ operations for a dense $n$ by $n$ matrix.

= Forward substitution in a triangular system
{parent=Triangular linear system}

Forward substitution solves a lower-triangular system from its first row downward, substituting each newly determined variable into the rows below.

= Backward substitution in a triangular system
{parent=Triangular linear system}

Backward substitution solves an upper-triangular system from its last row upward.

= Axis-angle representation
{parent=Linear algebra}
{wiki=Axis–angle_representation}

An <axis-angle representation> specifies a rotation of three-dimensional Euclidean space by an oriented unit axis and an angle. The axis is fixed, and the perpendicular plane undergoes a pure <rotation>, with no dilation.
